# Raw research digest

Generated from the `blimp-recon` workflow (40 agents, 15 investigations each independently fact-checked).
Each section is one investigation. `RELIABILITY` is the adversarial checker's verdict on that investigation.


---

## comp-larc (source)  
**RELIABILITY: high**

IAR-LARC ("International Airship Racing at LARC") is a NEW category at LARC 2026 (26th edition of the Latin American Robotics Competition), Nov 2-6 2026, hosted by Universidad Militar Nueva Granada (UMNG), Bogotá. Competition Chair is Diego Salazar D'Antonio (Oakland Univ., dantonio@oakland.edu) with David Saldaña (Lehigh Univ.). I downloaded the actual rulebook PDF directly (Google Drive file id 1MhURs2RBSlMRAwis7IvOjQjBchqZdHRZ, embedded on https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules, dated March 27, 2026) and extracted full text plus rendered the arena-diagram page as an image to read exact dimensions. Objective: an autonomous LTA (helium) airship must touch 4 tethered red balloons (waypoints) in sequence, on 2 courses (Course 1 = rectangle perimeter, Course 2 = figure-eight), up to 3 loops each, best time per course, sum of both = final score, lowest total wins. Hard constraints (verbatim from rulebook): helium volume ≤ that of a 50-inch balloon (~1 m³); airship must be neutrally buoyant within ±10 gf; total thrust across all motors ≤ 100 gf (servos excluded); bounding box ≤ 1m×1m×1.5m (this contradicts an earlier clause claiming "no explicit size limitations" — flagged); fully autonomous once started (no teleop), but rule 1 explicitly permits OFFBOARD COMPUTATION — only sensing must be onboard (no external cameras/mocap for state estimation). Arena is 10m×5m, waypoint rectangle 4m×2m, waypoints at 2m height, 22-inch red mylar balloons (50-60cm inflated). The rulebook's own Appendix names the "baseline design" as MochiSwarm/BlimpSwarm (Lehigh) — literally the same repos the professor already has (mochi-swarm-sim is cited as the official simulator, and it ships a MuJoCo model file named exactly `mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml`, i.e. it already models this race). I pulled full BOM, PCB (Fusion360/.brd/.sch/Gerber-style files), and firmware install instructions for that baseline (XIAO ESP32-S3-based), plus its extensive hardware/firmware troubleshooting FAQ. Registration: pre-register deadline Sept 14 2026 (ALREADY PASSED as of today 9/21/2026); early-bird fees end Sept 28 2026; Team Description Paper due Oct 12 2026. CircuitDigest article and espressif/esp-drone are generic ESP32-S3 quadcopter references (Crazyflie-derived), not blimp-specific — useful only for PWM/IMU/radio patterns, not buoyancy/actuation design.

### Gotchas

- URGENT: the LARC 2026 'pre-register' deadline (Sept 14, 2026) has already passed as of today (Sept 21, 2026) — confirm with organizers whether pre-registration is still open/rolling, since the early-bird fee window (through Sept 28) is still active.
- Rulebook self-contradiction: Rule 12 says 'no explicit size limitations' while Rule 14 gives an explicit 1m x 1m x 1.5m bounding box — get written clarification from the chair (dantonio@oakland.edu) on which governs before finalizing envelope size.
- Autonomy nuance is easy to misread: OFFBOARD COMPUTE is allowed, but ALL SENSING must be onboard the vehicle (Rule 15) — you cannot use an external tracking camera or motion-capture rig as the localization source; you can still stream onboard sensor data to a ground laptop for heavy processing as long as no human intervenes.
- The 'baseline' balloon size differs across sources: rulebook Appendix A says ~36-inch, the live BlimpSwarm docs BOM also uses a 36-inch balloon, but the MochiSwarm arxiv paper's actual hardware used a 50-inch balloon (the maximum the rules allow) to get more net buoyancy (~120gf) for extra payload — bigger envelope means more lift budget but more wind sensitivity/drag, a real design tradeoff to make deliberately, not by default.
- The rulebook's 100gf total-thrust cap is exactly the same order of magnitude as the ~120gf of net buoyancy reported for the 50in balloon in the research paper — thrust is intentionally starved relative to lift capacity, meaning control authority will be the binding constraint, not battery power; expect the vehicle to be sluggish and to need generous turning radii, matching the fairly gentle 4m x 2m waypoint rectangle geometry.
- BlimpSwarm's own FAQ flags very concrete failure modes the team will likely hit: propeller-direction inversion (motors spin but no lift), servo/motor wiring swapped front-to-front, BNO/IMU startup failure needing a power-cycle, voltage-booster-starved servos breaking, and PCB/ESP32 damage from repeated current spikes/antenna wear on the transceiver — all documented with fixes.
- The Slack invite link embedded in the rulebook (https://join.slack.com/) is a generic/placeholder URL, not a working invite — email the chair directly for the real invite.
- larc-lars2025.org (last year's official site) is now DNS-unreachable, so last-year IAR results/participation could not be verified at all — don't assume IAR existed in 2025.
- Course waypoint VISITING ORDER is not fixed by the rulebook diagrams — Rule 2 states the order 'will be specified prior to each run', so autonomy code must accept a runtime-configurable waypoint sequence, not a hardcoded one.
- CircuitDigest/esp-drone material is quadcopter-specific (rotor lift against gravity); none of its attitude-control assumptions transfer directly to a buoyant, thrust-starved (100gf!) bicopter blimp — use it only for ESP32 firmware/radio/PWM scaffolding, not for the flight-control law itself.

### Actionable

- Email Diego Salazar D'Antonio (dantonio@oakland.edu) immediately to: (1) confirm whether the Sept 14 pre-registration deadline is still open, (2) resolve the Rule 12 vs Rule 14 size-limit contradiction, (3) get the real Slack invite link, (4) ask if IAR ran in 2025 and if any past results/videos exist.
- Register for LARC 2026 before Sept 28, 2026 to lock in early-bird pricing (100,000 COP team fee vs 140,000 COP regular), and prepare the Team Description Paper (TDP) for the Oct 12, 2026 deadline.
- Treat github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm as the primary technical reference, not just github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim: pull its PCB Fusion360/.brd/.sch files and diff them against the professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCB to see what's reusable vs. what needs redesign (servo driving, brushless ESC outputs, I2C sensor bus, battery-monitor pin).
- Install and run the mochi-swarm-sim MuJoCo simulator locally (`pip install -r requirements.txt; python main.py models/mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml`) immediately — it appears to already model the exact IAR race course, so it can validate a waypoint-sequencing/guidance algorithm entirely in simulation before any hardware flight.
- Follow the BlimpSwarm firmware.md steps exactly for the first bring-up: flash 'Bicopter1raw' to the pinned XIAO ESP32-S3, note its MAC, flash 'BaseTransceiver' to a second bare ESP32 as ground-station radio, and validate actuation with the Xbox-joystick Python script before writing any autonomy code.
- Decide envelope diameter deliberately: 36in (baseline, easier to build/handle, less lift) vs up to 50in (rule-legal max, ~120gf net buoyancy per the arxiv paper, more payload margin for a heavier custom PCB/battery) — size this against the ESP32-S3 board + motors + sensors + battery weight budget with the ±10gf neutral-buoyancy tolerance in mind.
- Design/tune the perception pipeline around the documented approach: color-blob detection (LAB space, tuned per-lighting thresholds) on an OpenMV-class camera (Nicla Vision or similar) run onboard (mandatory per Rule 15), sending target bearing over UART/I2C to the ESP32 flight controller — reuse the BlimpSwarm Blob-Detection-and-Tracking algorithm as a starting point rather than writing balloon detection from scratch.
- Budget thrust conservatively against the 100gf total cap and plan the bicopter's turning geometry against the fixed 4m x 2m waypoint rectangle — this is a low-thrust, low-speed control problem, so prioritize station-keeping/heading control robustness over raw speed.

### Open questions

- Does Rule 12 ('no explicit size limitations') or Rule 14 (1m x 1m x 1.5m bounding box) actually govern? The rulebook contradicts itself.
- Is the Sept 14, 2026 pre-registration deadline hard/passed, or still effectively open (rolling)? Needs direct confirmation from the organizers since 'today' in this research is Sept 21, 2026.
- Did an IAR/blimp category exist at any LARC edition before 2026? No primary source found either way; last year's LARC site (larc-lars2025.org) is now unreachable.
- What exactly counts as 'human intervention' for disqualification purposes — e.g., is a manual battery swap between the 3 allowed loops permitted, or must all 3 loops run back-to-back with zero touching of the vehicle?
- Are teams allowed/required to bring their own helium, or is it supplied by UMNG on-site (relevant for logistics/customs when traveling from abroad with a helium-filled or empty envelope)?
- Are there RF/communication restrictions (e.g., a mandated channel/frequency for the ESP-NOW or WiFi ground-station link) given multiple teams' airships may operate simultaneously in adjacent arena halves?
- What is the exact required format/length for the Team Description Paper (TDP), and is it category-specific (IAR) or a single general LARC template?
- What are the exact hour-by-hour times for qualification runs/finals during Nov 2-6, 2026 (currently listed as TBD)?

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — Rulebook Rules 12 vs 14 contradict each other: Rule 12 says 'no explicit size limitations' while Rule 14 gives a 1m×1m×1.5m bounding box.
  - Correction: Verbatim confirmed by downloading the actual PDF myself (not trusting the citation). Rule 12 literally reads: 'Vehicle Size Constraints: There are no explicit size limitations on the airship. However, teams are responsible for ensuring that their vehicle can operate safely within the competition space.' Rule 14 literally reads: 'Robot Size Constraints: The maximum allowable dimensions of the airship are 1m×1m×1.5m, measured as the smallest axis-aligned bounding box that fully contains the vehicle in its operational configuration.' This is a genuine, verbatim self-contradiction in the March 27, 2026 rulebook — treat Rule 14's 1m×1m×1.5m as the binding number for physical design and get it in writing from the chair regardless.
- **CONFIRMED** — Helium/buoyant volume limit = volume of a 50-inch diameter balloon, ≈1 m³ (Rule 10), with Amazon link B0CJB5F7MZ.
  - Correction: No correction needed; verbatim match, including the exact Amazon ASIN.
- **CONFIRMED** — Neutral buoyancy tolerance = net vertical force within ±10 grams-force (Rule 11).
  - Correction: No correction needed; verbatim match.
- **CONFIRMED** — Total propulsion thrust capped at 100 grams-force across all motors; servos excluded from the budget (Rule 13).
  - Correction: No correction needed; verbatim match including worked examples.
- **CONFIRMED** — Autonomy rule permits offboard computation but requires all sensing to be performed onboard the vehicle (no external tracking cameras/mocap for state estimation).
  - Correction: No correction needed; verbatim match.
- **CONFIRMED** — Arena geometry: overall course 10m×5m; 4 waypoints form a 4m×2m rectangle inside it; 2m-diameter start region at waypoint 1; waypoint height 2m to balloon center; waypoint balloons are 22-inch red mylar (~50-60cm inflated).
  - Correction: No correction needed; verbatim match.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Registration: pre-register Sept 14 2026; early-bird through Sept 28 2026 (team 100,000 COP); regular Sept 29-Oct 5 2026 (team 140,000 COP); Team Description Paper due Oct 12 2026.

- Event-date inconsistency across the organizer's own site: https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/larc.html says 'Nov 2-6, 2026' but https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/index.html (the contact page) says 'scheduled for November 4-6, 2026'. - The rulebook Appendix A's baseline BOM (generic ESP32 + 2x brushed DC motors + ~36in balloon + 1S 500mAh LiPo) is dramatically simpler/cheaper than the full BlimpSwarm reference platform (XIAO ESP32-S3 + brushless motors/ESCs + NiclaVision + barometer/IMU/ultrasonic, ~$247), and does not itself require brushless motors, a servo, or a vision board at all — since the professor's team already has an ESP32-S3 PCB, they are already ahead of the rulebook's minimum baseline and should treat the fuller BlimpSwarm BOM as an optional upgrade path, not a mandatory minimum.

(capacity 450, ~45min from El Dorado airport, SITP transit 2min walk). It is a hosting-cycle event site sitting on the Mechatronics program's subdomain, not the program's general homepage (a separate older "/seminario/" path exists on the same subdomain). LARC 2026 has 6 categories: OPEN (coffee-picking robot), SEK, VSSS, HRR (humanoid racing), LINE FOLLOWER, and — critically — IAR (International Airship Racing), which is exactly the blimp category the professor's team should enter. IAR is NOT a RoboCup/CBR qualifier; LARC is an independent, IEEE-affiliated, 26-year-running Latin American event; no RoboCup/CBR reference found anywhere. Full technical rules, arena diagrams, and the baseline "Mochiswarm" bicopter design (ESP32-based, ~$83.74 BOM, referencing the exact GitHub simulator repo the professor already shared) are captured verbatim below. Registration deadlines are LIVE and URGENT relative to the stated "today" of Sept 21 2026: early-bird LARC team registration (100,000 COP) closes Sept 28 2026, just one week out; regular registration (140,000 COP) runs Sept 29-Oct 5. Contact: larslarc@unimilitar.edu.co / +57 601 6500000; IAR-specific: dantonio@oakland.edu, saldana@lehigh.edu (Lehigh — home of the BlimpSwarm baseline design). The IAR schedule (from the Oakland site) shows Day 1 = Nov 2: "team registration and safety inspection" + "practice sessions on the competition field," confirming both an inspection process and dedicated practice time exist, though no specific times are published (marked TBD).

### Gotchas

- Registration deadline is imminent: LARC early-bird team registration (100,000 COP) closes Sept 28, 2026 — only ~1 week from the stated 'today' (Sept 21, 2026). Missing it bumps the fee to 140,000 COP (Sept 29-Oct5 window) and eats into build/testing time.
- Helium provisioning on competition day is completely undocumented. If the team must supply their own helium in Bogotá, that needs to be arranged locally (importing/shipping compressed helium internationally is impractical) — flag this to the professor immediately and email dantonio@oakland.edu / larslarc@unimilitar.edu.co to ask directly.
- Bogotá sits at ~2,640 m elevation (this is a physics inference, NOT from any rulebook or site — mark as inferred/unverified): air density there is roughly 75% of sea level, which reduces the buoyant force helium produces per unit volume. Any envelope sized/ballasted and neutrally-buoyancy-tuned (±10 gf tolerance!) at sea level will very likely need re-ballasting on site in Bogotá before it will pass the buoyancy rule.
- The ±10 gf buoyancy tolerance and 100 gf total thrust cap are both very tight budgets — every gram of the professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCB, wiring, battery, and any added sensors must be weighed against this, and it argues for a lightweight companion/breakout approach rather than a heavy full dev-board.
- The rulebook explicitly excludes servos from the 100 gf thrust budget accounting, but does NOT exclude their weight from the buoyancy budget — don't conflate the two rules when planning payload.
- Rulebook date stamp reads 'March 27, 2026' despite being fetched fresh in Sept 2026 — this is likely just the PDF's authoring/last-revision date, not evidence of a stale document (the site's dynamic fee/date pages agree with a Nov 2026 event), but it's worth a sanity-check that no newer rules revision has since been posted at the same URL.
- The Notion 'Robot Assembly' guide (linked from the BlimpSwarm docs) is a JS-heavy page that could not be rendered via WebFetch — someone needs to open https://rhinestone-star-fa9.notion.site/Robot-Assembly in an actual browser to get the real build steps/wiring.
- IAR rules require ALL sensing onboard (rule 15) — any approach borrowing from the esp-drone or CircuitDigest WiFi-drone article that relies on ground-station/offboard processing or external tracking would violate this rule and needs adapting to run fully on the onboard ESP32-S3.
- The site's doubled-path URL (mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/index.html) is real and functional, not a typo to 'fix' — don't let anyone 'correct' it to a URL that then 404s; all three variants (root, /index.html, /index.html/index.html) work identically as of this check.

  - Correction: The rulebook's OWN Appendix A text (primary source) describes the baseline motors as 'Coreless brushed DC motors (x2)' — not brushless. This directly contradicts the Lehigh BlimpSwarm docs page's BOM, which the original research quoted as fact ('Brushless motors (1102, 13500kV)') without noting the conflict. Brushed vs. brushless changes ESC/driver selection and control approach; the team should treat this as an open question to resolve with the organizers/Lehigh group before committing to a driver design, not as settled fact. Also newly identified: the baseline's source paper is 'MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments' (Xu, Vu, D'Antonio, Saldaña; accepted ICRA 2025) at arxiv.org/abs/2503.03077, which the rulebook cites directly and which the original research listed as a URL but never identified or connected to the authors/chairs.
- **CONFIRMED** — mochi-swarm-sim is a Python + MuJoCo simulator, a fork of vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim with 31 commits, run via pip install -r requirements.txt then python main.py [model.xml].
  - Correction: Verified directly via GitHub API (not WebFetch summarization): fork=true, parent/source=vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim, 31 commits (last page=31 at per_page=1), no license file. requirements.txt confirms mujoco (+numpy, glfw, scipy, matplotlib, opencv-python) exactly as claimed. Added detail: models/ directory contains mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml, _figure8.xml, and _s.xml — the figure8 file corresponds directly to the rulebook's 'Course 2 (Infinity Path),' so the sim already models both official courses, which strengthens the 'validate control in sim before Bogotá' recommendation.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — IAR sensing/autonomy rule requires the CircuitDigest/esp-drone style of offboard/ground-station processing to be abandoned in favor of fully onboard ESP32-S3 processing, because 'all sensing must be onboard' (rule 15).
  - Correction: Rule 1 (General Rules, 'Autonomy Requirement') explicitly states: 'Onboard or offboard computation is permitted, but no human intervention is allowed during execution.' Rule 15 only requires the sensing (i.e., the physical sensors/data acquisition) to be onboard, not the computation. So a WiFi-telemetry architecture in the esp-drone/CircuitDigest style — onboard sensors streaming data to a ground computer that does the heavy processing and streams back autonomous (non-human) commands — is plausibly compliant, contrary to the blanket claim that it 'would violate this rule and needs adapting to run fully on the onboard ESP32-S3.' This changes the architecture options meaningfully (a ground-side compute budget becomes usable, not just the ESP32-S3's own CPU).
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — No mention of RoboCup or CBR affiliation anywhere on the UMNG or Oakland IAR sites; LARC/LARS is an independent, IEEE-affiliated, 26-year event.
  - Correction: Re-checked index/larc.html and the Oakland home/rules/schedule/venue pages independently this session — still zero mentions of RoboCup or CBR anywhere. This supports the original claim, but it remains an absence-of-evidence inference (I could not run a broader web search this session — WebSearch budget was already exhausted — so a page outside the ~10 I fetched could still mention it). Keep as 'likely true, not provably exhaustive' rather than fully confirmed.
- **CONFIRMED** — Helium supply/provisioning at the venue is undocumented anywhere across the UMNG and Oakland IAR sites, including Schedule and Venue subpages.
  - Correction: Re-fetched oakland.edu/iar-larc/venue, oakland.edu/iar-larc/home, and mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/venue.html independently — zero mentions of helium, gas supply, or tanks on any of them. This is a real, still-open logistics gap; email the organizers as the original recommended.
- **CONFIRMED** — IAR schedule (Oakland site): Day 1 (Nov 2) = arrival/check-in, team registration and safety inspection, practice sessions, welcome briefing; times are TBD.
  - Correction: Re-fetched independently; wording matches almost verbatim (Day 1 Nov 2, Day 2 Nov 3–5, Day 3 Nov 6), and times are indeed still listed as TBD.
- **CONFIRMED** — Venue is Aula Máxima, UMNG, Carrera 11 #101-80, Bogotá D.C., capacity 450, ~45 min from El Dorado airport, SITP 'calle 100' station 2 min walk, 50+ parking spaces.
  - Correction: All figures match exactly on independent re-fetch of the venue page.

### Added by fact-checker

- A Team Description Paper (TDP) is due October 12, 2026 per larc.html — a real competition deliverable the original research never mentioned at all; this could be as fatal to miss as the registration deadline and should go straight into the project plan.
- A 'Pre-register' step with a deadline of September 14, 2026 is listed on larc.html — relative to the stated 'today' of September 21, 2026, this pre-registration deadline has already passed. This needs urgent clarification with organizers: is a late pre-registration still possible, or does missing it block the whole team from the early-bird price track?
- The rulebook's Appendix A baseline description ('Coreless brushed DC motors') directly contradicts the Lehigh BlimpSwarm docs BOM ('Brushless motors, 1102 13500kV') that both the original research and the rulebook's own linked 'Documentation' reference. This is an unresolved conflict between two sources the rulebook itself treats as authoritative for the same baseline design, and should be raised with the organizers/Lehigh Blimp Group before committing to ESCs/drivers.
- Rule 12 ('no explicit size limitations') and Rule 14 (explicit 1m×1m×1.5m bounding box cap) are internally contradictory within the primary rulebook PDF itself — worth a clarifying email, but design to the explicit 1×1×1.5m number to be safe.
- Rule 1 explicitly permits offboard computation (only sensing must be onboard per Rule 15) — this is more permissive than the original research's gotcha implied, and opens up a WiFi-telemetry/ground-station architecture option similar to esp-drone's, as long as sensors themselves are onboard and no human is in the loop.
- The Mochiswarm baseline's source publication is identifiable and citable: 'MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments' by Jiawei Xu, Thong Vu, Diego S. D'Antonio, David Saldaña, accepted ICRA 2025 (arxiv.org/abs/2503.03077) — ties directly back to the two IAR contacts (D'Antonio, Saldaña), meaning the competition chairs are also the baseline design's own authors, which is useful context for who to ask technical questions.
- github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim has NO license file (confirmed via GitHub API: license: null) — if the team plans to modify and possibly redistribute/publish their fork, this is worth flagging/asking the repo owner about, since 'no license' defaults to full copyright reservation under GitHub's terms.
- The simulator's models/ directory already contains both official course geometries as ready-to-run MuJoCo XML files: mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml (Course 1, rectangle) and mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course_figure8.xml (Course 2, infinity) plus a mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course_s.xml variant — meaning the team can validate navigation/control against the exact competition geometry today without waiting to build hardware.

## video-1 (source)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

VIDEO IDENTIFIED, HIGH CONFIDENCE. youtu.be/V_mZsiZcy7s = "Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY" by channel Max Imagination (youtube.com/@MaxImagination). Confirmed via YouTube's own oEmbed endpoint (noembed.com proxy of https://www.youtube.com/oembed, a primary-source API call, not a third-party guess) returning exact title/author/author_url for this literal video ID, and independently cross-confirmed because the creator's own Instructables tutorial (by member "Max Imagination", published 2025-03-27, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0) embeds this exact URL twice as "the video" for this build and links it again at the article's close ("Watch the full build process... https://youtu.be/V_mZsiZcy7s"). IMPORTANT CORRECTION TO TASK FRAMING: this video is NOT a blimp/airship. It shows a ~50mm palm-sized rigid-frame quadcopter (a micro-quad, not lighter-than-air). The blimp connection is indirect but real: video 3 in the professor's list, youtu.be/Y5i3LntcNt0, is literally titled "ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone" (channel: Lehigh Swarms Club) - i.e. that separate team took THIS ESP-FLY drone's electronics stack (ESP32-S3 + custom IMU/motor-driver PCB) and rehoused it inside a helium envelope/gondola. So V_mZsiZcy7s's engineering value to the professor's blimp project is as the ELECTRONICS/PCB/FIRMWARE REFERENCE (flight controller board, motor driver circuit, firmware flashing workflow), not as an airframe or envelope reference. This dovetails with the professor already having ESP32-S3 custom PCBs. Video 2, youtu.be/xW2Nwg_RX84 ("Guide to making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers with MOSFETs for RC Projects", same channel Max Imagination), is that same channel's dedicated tutorial on the MOSFET brushed-motor-driver circuit used on the ESP-FLY board - almost certainly the exact circuit the professor should replicate for driving small coreless/brushed lift/propulsion motors on the blimp gondola. Full BOM, PCB design tool, firmware source, 3D print settings, assembly steps, and real builder troubleshooting (from 23 Instructables comments) were extracted from primary/author sources (Instructables article text, official Seeed-Projects GitHub README, Elektor Magazine article by the same author). No official video transcript was retrieved (YouTube transcript endpoint not directly accessible via available tools); reconstruction instead relies on the author's own written companion documentation, which is unusually complete for this creator (goes step-by-step in the same order as the video per the article's own framing).

### Specs

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| Flight controller MCU | Seeed Studio XIAO ESP32-S3, dual-core 240MHz Xtensa LX7, 8MB flash, 8MB PSRAM, Wi-Fi + Bluetooth 5.0 |
| IMU | MPU-6050, 6-DoF (3-axis gyro + 3-axis accel) |
| Motor driver | 4x SI2300 N-channel MOSFET + 4x 1N4148 flyback/switching diodes |
| Motors | 4x '615' coreless DC motors, ~70,000 RPM, ~17g thrust each (2 CW + 2 CCW) |
| Propellers | GEMFAN tri-blade, 30mm diameter, 0.8mm shaft bore |
| Battery | 1S LiPo 3.7V, 250mAh (150-450mAh range usable), 25-30C discharge, JST-PH 2-pin connector |
| PCB | Custom 4-layer board, 1.2mm thickness, designed in Flux PCB software, fabbed at JLCPCB (~$0.42/board for qty 5), SMD stencil + hot-plate reflow for top side, hand-soldered motor-driver side |
| Total weight | 1g (IMU/driver module alone), 18g (drone, no battery), 25g (with 250mAh battery), 28g (with FPV camera) |
| Thrust-to-weight ratio | 2.7:1 (68g combined thrust / 25g flying weight) |
| Flight time | ~5 min typical, up to 5.5 min tested (250mAh battery) |
| Control range | ~50m (author's own flight test, Wi-Fi) to ~100m+ (stated spec); up to ~200m via optional ESP-NOW radio-control path |
| Control latency | 7-25ms (Wi-Fi) |
| Firmware | ESP-Drone (Espressif), forked/modified by Circuit Digest / Jobit Joseph; flashed via ESP-IDF 5.0.7; alt path: rtlopez's ESP-FC firmware for Betaflight-compatible radio control |
| Control app | ESP-Drone mobile app (iOS App Store; Android APK via pgyer.com/a27L), Wi-Fi AP SSID password '12345678' |
| 3D print settings | ePLA 1.75mm, 0.12mm layers, 10% infill, supports on, 100mm/s, nozzle 220C, bed 60C, cooling on; printed on Elegoo Neptune 4 Plus in ~30 min; frame weight ~5g |
| PVC alternative frame | 2-inch (50mm) PVC pipe heated flat, cut/bent per A4 blueprint, ~6g finished weight (from 11g flat stock) |
| Total component cost | $37.30 USD (+/- $10), excluding shipping/spares/print material/tools; optional FPV camera adds $25.99 (WT07 5.8GHz 25mW AIO cam+VTX) |
| Firmware config path | idf.py -p COMX flash monitor; idf.py menuconfig -> ESPDrone Config menu for pins/sensors/LEDs/motor assignment |

### Gotchas

- Video 1 (V_mZsiZcy7s) is a rigid quadcopter, not a blimp - do not extract envelope/gas/gondola-buoyancy specs from it, because none exist in this build; its value is purely as an ESP32-S3 flight-controller-PCB and firmware reference.
- The phone-app (ESP-Drone/Wi-Fi) control path in this firmware has NO tunable PID loop per the author - if the professor wants autonomous stabilization or any closed-loop tuning, the ESP-Drone Wi-Fi firmware fork used here will not expose it; the ESP-FC/Betaflight-over-ESP-NOW radio path (a different firmware, covered in a different Max Imagination video/Instructables: 'Fly Your ESP32 Drone on Betaflight With Radio Controller') is the one with tunable PID and could be the more relevant reference for closed-loop attitude control on a blimp.
- Motor-to-driver-channel mapping and CW/CCW motor+prop handedness matching is the single most commonly reported failure mode in the comment thread (flipping/wobbling at throttle) - build the blimp gondola's motor wiring/firmware pin map carefully and verify motor spin direction before first flight test.
- IMU rigidity matters disproportionately at this scale: any mechanical flex between MPU6050 and frame reportedly causes wobble; for a blimp gondola (likely even lower rigidity structure than a rigid quad frame, and subject to swinging under the envelope) this is a bigger risk, not a smaller one - budget for a stiff IMU mount and possibly filter/tune for pendulum-mode oscillation of the gondola under the balloon.
- The Crazyflie desktop client ('cfclient') for live telemetry/tuning is reported broken/unreliable by both the author and a commenter (freezes after initial connection) - do not rely on it for characterization or debugging; use the ESP-Drone phone app or raw firmware logs instead.
- Only mid-March-to-late-March 2025 is confirmed for this cluster of publications (Instructables 2025-03-27, Elektor ~2025-03-26, CircuitDigest news ~2025-03-18); the literal YouTube upload date for V_mZsiZcy7s was not independently verified from YouTube itself - treat any specific day as approximate.
- STL files are NOT free - they are sold on Cults3D (paid model), while the Google Drive folder (firmware+Gerber+schematic) is free/shared - don't assume all project files are free.
- Instructables article license is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial, share-alike) - if the team reuses text/images/designs from this specific Instructables page in competition materials, that license applies (the official Seeed-Projects GitHub repo has its own, separately-stated GPL-3.0 license for firmware only, inherited from ESP-Drone/Espressif).

### Actionable

- Do NOT ask another agent to re-search for 'blimp specs' inside video 1 - redirect the blimp-envelope/gondola/gas research effort to video 3 (Y5i3LntcNt0, Lehigh Swarms Club, 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone'), which is the actual precedent for the airframe/envelope side of the project.
- Have the agent covering video 2 (xW2Nwg_RX84) explicitly check whether its MOSFET brushed-motor-driver design matches the SI2300 N-MOSFET + 1N4148 diode topology used on the ESP-FLY PCB documented here - if so, that video is likely the deep-dive schematic explainer for the exact driver stage the professor's own ESP32-S3 PCB should replicate for gondola propulsion motors.
- Pull and archive the free Google Drive bundle (firmware + Gerber + schematic) at drive.google.com/drive/folders/18QSSe5u8A_p2iix2rqLfaRU-QODWJp0w before it goes stale/permission-restricted (one Instructables commenter already reported download errors on it).
- Compare the professor's existing ESP32-S3 custom PCB against this BOM/schematic; if driver topology matches, the flashing workflow (ESP-IDF 5.0.7, idf.py -p COMX flash monitor, idf.py menuconfig under 'ESPDrone Config') and troubleshooting notes above transfer directly.
- When choosing a control scheme for the competition blimp, treat 'ESP-Drone Wi-Fi app, no PID exposed' vs 'ESP-FC/Betaflight over ESP-NOW radio, PID exposed' as a real design fork inherited from this same firmware family - pick deliberately rather than defaulting to the phone-app path if any autonomous/closed-loop behavior is required by the competition rules.
- If budget allows, treat the $37.30 discrete-component BOM as a cost/complexity floor for 'the electronics portion' of a from-scratch build, separate from and additional to whatever the professor's own already-fabricated ESP32-S3 PCB cost - useful as a sanity-check line item for a project budget sheet.

### Open questions

- Exact literal YouTube 'Published on' date for V_mZsiZcy7s (not independently retrieved from YouTube's own page; only inferred window mid-to-late March 2025 from companion articles).
- Whether the professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCB was in fact derived from/inspired by this specific ESP-FLY schematic, or is an independent design - cannot be determined without seeing the professor's PCB files.
- Root cause of the unresolved IMU X-axis/linear-acceleration-stuck-at-zero bug reported by 'frederic4' in the comments - left unresolved in the public thread; could recur on a replicated board and should be planned for as a known open failure mode.
- Exact resolved cause of the 'flips at ~17% throttle, two motors then two more' bug reported by kristiankoev2449 - author suspected GPIO/driver-channel mismatch but the thread does not show a final confirmed fix.
- Total combined weight/thrust math has not been redone for a blimp use-case (this video's 2.7:1 thrust-to-weight and 68g total thrust figures are for lifting the whole 25g quadcopter against gravity; on a helium blimp most of the weight is buoyancy-supported, so the same motors would be used for propulsion/vectoring thrust rather than lift-to-weight ratio - this reframing was not covered by any source and is an inference, not a sourced fact).

### Could not retrieve

- Direct YouTube watch-page HTML for V_mZsiZcy7s (WebFetch on youtube.com/watch and youtube.com/embed URLs both returned only navigation chrome, no video metadata/description/comments - YouTube blocks generic scraping of these fields).
- Full video transcript/closed captions for V_mZsiZcy7s (no transcript-mirror site was found or fetched; reconstruction relies entirely on the author's own written Instructables companion article, which the author frames as following the same build order as the video).
- Full README content of the unrelated github.com/UpsidedownFalcon/ESP-Fly repo (403 Forbidden on WebFetch; only a search-snippet-level description was available).
- Exact wording of the video's own YouTube description box (links/timestamps as authored in the description itself, as opposed to the equivalent links found in the companion Instructables article).
- View count, like count, and comment count on the YouTube video itself.
- A directly-dated 'this is the upload timestamp' confirmation from YouTube (see open_questions).

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — V_mZsiZcy7s title/channel = 'Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY' by Max Imagination
- **CONFIRMED** — Y5i3LntcNt0 title/channel = 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone' by Lehigh Swarms Club
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — xW2Nwg_RX84 title/channel = 'Guide to making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers with MOSFETs for RC Projects' by Max Imagination (same channel as video 1)
  - Correction: Title/channel match is confirmed. But the leap that this video documents 'the exact SI2300 N-MOSFET + 1N4148 topology used on the ESP-FLY PCB' is an unverified inference from title similarity alone -- neither researcher accessed the video's transcript or a description to confirm the schematic actually shown matches SI2300. Treat as plausible, not confirmed.
- **REFUTED** — 'PID tuning is non-existent in the mobile controlled version' / the ESP-Drone Wi-Fi firmware path has no tunable PID loop and a professor wanting closed-loop tuning must switch to the ESP-FC/Betaflight-over-ESP-NOW radio path instead
  - Correction: Espressif's own ESP-Drone docs state the firmware runs a full cascaded controller per axis ('For each control dimension, a string-level PID controller is provided, consisting of a Rate controller and an Attitude controller') and that online PID tuning IS exposed over the same Wi-Fi link: 'Burn the code and start the KP adjustment online using the param function of cfclient.' The correct nuance is: the simple PHONE APP has no tuning UI, but the underlying Wi-Fi firmware + cfclient (Crazyflie's own PC client) DOES expose live PID/rate/trim tuning -- no firmware swap to a Betaflight/ESP-NOW fork is required to get tunable control. This directly changes an 'actionable' recommendation in the original research.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Firmware credited as 'Circuit Digest / Jobit Joseph's modified FORK of ESP-Drone' at github.com/Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone, and this ties video 1 to the CircuitDigest article the professor sent via 'same firmware lineage, same author community'
  - Correction: GitHub API confirms Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone exists with 2,427 stars / 396 forks, BUT its 'fork' field is false -- it is not a GitHub-registered fork of espressif/esp-drone, and its own README makes zero mention of Max Imagination, ESP-FLY, XIAO, or any ESP-FLY BOM part; it documents a wholly separate Circuit Digest tutorial with its OWN video (youtu.be/uzZjk0TQKtU, not V_mZsiZcy7s). Crucially, its README literally links 'For a full tutorial check out our ESP32 Drone Article: circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone' -- that is the EXACT SAME URL the professor already sent you separately. So the real, confirmed relationship is stronger and more direct than 'same author community': this GitHub repo IS the companion code for the CircuitDigest article the professor sent, and it also includes raw esptool flash offsets (0x1000 bootloader.bin, 0x8000 partition-table.bin, 0x10000 ESPDrone.bin) that neither researcher's summary captured. Whether Max Imagination's ESP-FLY firmware is literally source-derived from this repo (vs. independently built on upstream espressif/esp-drone) was not verified by either researcher.
- **CONFIRMED** — Total component cost $37.30 USD (+-$10)
  - Correction: Confirmed by 2 independent sources (Instructables via jina.ai proxy AND the separate Elektor Magazine article), so the headline number is solid. However, my independent extraction of the BOM table shows SI2300 MOSFETs at '$0.55 unit price, $2.20 total for 4' whereas the original researcher recorded '4x SI2300 ($0.55 total)' -- a ~$1.65 discrepancy on that single line. Both extractions are AI-mediated paraphrases of the same article, not a direct HTML read, so treat the per-line breakdown as approximate and re-verify against the primary Instructables page before publishing a BOM in competition materials.
- **CONFIRMED** — Weight/thrust: 18g (no battery), 25g (with 250mAh LiPo), 28g (with FPV cam), 68g combined thrust, 2.7:1 thrust-to-weight, ~5 to 5.5 min flight time
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Control range ~50m Wi-Fi (author's flight test)
  - Correction: Real, confirmed source disagreement, not resolved by either researcher: CircuitDigest news (Mar 18, 2025) says 'up to 50 meters'; Instructables (Mar 27, 2025, same author) says '50 meters' observed; but Elektor (Mar 26, 2025, SAME author, 8 days later) says '100+ meters via WiFi'; and the official Seeed-Projects GitHub README says '~50m via Wi-Fi / up to ~200m via ESP-NOW radio.' The 50m and 100m+ figures both trace to the same author within the same week, so this isn't resolvable from documentation alone -- budget for the team to bench-test actual Wi-Fi range themselves rather than citing either number in competition material.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — 'Angle and Acro only - no altitude hold, no GPS, no autonomous navigation in stock firmware' (explicit FAQ answer)
  - Correction: True as stated for the ESP-FLY kit's specific minimal hardware (no barometer/ToF/flow sensor populated on that tiny PCB) -- confirmed verbatim in the Seeed README: 'ESP-FLY is a manually controlled micro drone platform' with no altitude hold/GPS/autonomous nav. BUT this must not be read as a blanket firmware limitation: the parent firmware family (espressif/esp-drone -- one of the professor's own linked repos, and the lineage Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone's README says evolved to add 'Height hold' and 'Position hold' as its LiteWing successor) DOES support altitude/position hold when paired with the right extension board (e.g., a barometer or flow-deck). This is a real, actionable correction for the professor: if his own ESP32-S3 PCB carries a barometer/ToF sensor, altitude-hold is achievable on the SAME firmware family without switching stacks -- important for blimp station-keeping/altitude behavior.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — STLs paid-only on Cults3D; firmware+Gerber+schematic Google Drive folder is free/public
  - Correction: Google Drive folder CONFIRMED live and public with no login (I re-fetched it independently): 4 files -- 'ESP-Drone Code' (modified Feb 27 2025), 'PCB Gerber, BOM, and PnP file' (modified Mar 15 2025), 'PVC Parts Blueprints' (modified Feb 27 2025), and 'Wiring Diagram & Schematic' (modified as recently as Dec 14 2025 -- i.e. the source is still being actively maintained ~9 months after the original March 2025 publication, a fact neither researcher's cutoff caught). Cults3D STL pricing could NOT be independently confirmed -- it 403'd on two separate fetch attempts (same failure mode the original researcher hit), so 'paid' status remains unverifiable by me too, not newly confirmed.
- **CONFIRMED** — github.com/UpsidedownFalcon/ESP-Fly is a separate, unrelated repo that merely links V_mZsiZcy7s as inspiration, and should not be confused with the canonical Seeed-Projects repo
- **CONFIRMED** — Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY is the 'Official repository for the ESP-FLY DIY Quadcopter Kit (XIAO ESP32-S3)... Seeed Studio x Max Imagination Co-Create Project'

### Added by fact-checker

- The Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone GitHub repo (github.com/Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone) is literally the companion code repo for circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone -- the exact article the professor already sent separately. Its README gives raw esptool flash offsets useful for manual flashing outside idf.py: 0x1000 bootloader.bin, 0x8000 partition-table.bin, 0x10000 ESPDrone.bin.
- That same repo's README states it 'evolved into LiteWing, a developer-friendly drone development platform' with added Height-hold and Position-hold modes (circuitdigest.com/wiki/litewing/) -- a more capable successor project in the same firmware family that the team may want to look at directly if altitude-hold/positioning matters for the blimp.
- Espressif's ESP-Drone firmware documents PID tuning as a first-class, supported workflow via cfclient's 'param' interface over Wi-Fi (docs.espressif.com/.../system.html) -- this is the single most consequential correction for engineering decisions: the team is not forced to abandon the Wi-Fi/phone-app firmware family to get tunable stabilization for a pendulum-prone blimp gondola.
- Seeed sells the assembled ESP-FLY kit itself at $59.99 (bulk $49.99 for 10+) at seeedstudio.com/ESP-FLY-co-create-p-6744.html -- a useful 'buy vs. build' reference point against the ~$37-39 DIY parts-only cost floor, though the professor's own PCB likely makes this moot.
- The Google Drive design-file bundle was still being updated as of Dec 14, 2025 (schematic file), i.e. well past the original March 2025 publication -- it is not a stale/abandoned link as of this check.
- CircuitDigest's own Mar 18 2025 news writeup of ESP-FLY does NOT credit or mention Max Imagination anywhere in its visible text, and instead frames the influence in the OPPOSITE direction from what 'same author community' implies: it says Circuit Digest's own earlier 'Low-cost Drone using ESP32' project 'has been a great inspiration' for ESP-FLY. Jobit Joseph is not shown authoring or co-crediting the ESP-FLY project in either the CircuitDigest article or the Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone README that I could retrieve -- this specific attribution chain should be treated as plausible-but-not-independently-confirmed, not as settled fact.

## video-2 (source)  
**RELIABILITY: high**

Video https://youtu.be/xW2Nwg_RX84 (video ID xW2Nwg_RX84) is IDENTIFIED WITH HIGH CONFIDENCE via YouTube's own oEmbed API and a direct curl of the raw watch-page JSON (ytInitialPlayerResponse), corroborated by 7+ independent third-party mirrors (Instructables, DFRobot Maker Community, Elektor Magazine, PMD Way blog, Seeed Studio forum, lilys.ai transcript note, TikTok). Title: "Guide to making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers with MOSFETs for RC Projects." Channel: Max Imagination (@MaxImagination, channelId UCZFZjTtu-8X3H0Ymcy4-uEA, ~280k subscribers per the Spanish-locale page string "280 mil suscriptores"). Uploaded 2025-01-02T06:19:11-08:00 (Pacific time; page also renders "2 ene 2025"). Length 1780s (29:40). 332,407 views, 9,741 likes, family-safe=true, category Science & Technology.

IMPORTANT SCOPE FINDING: this is NOT a blimp video and does not build an airship. It is a general electronics tutorial that designs and builds FOUR tiny MOSFET-based brushed-DC motor driver circuits (1) unidirectional single-channel, (2) unidirectional quad-channel, (3) bidirectional 1-channel H-bridge, (4) bidirectional 2-channel H-bridge) for driving small coreless/geared DC motors in RC vehicles (planes, drones, cars, robots). It uses a DFRobot FireBeetle 2 ESP32-S3-U board as the demo microcontroller, which is exactly why the professor gave it: it is the direct reference for how to interface the ESP32-S3 gondola PCB's GPIOs to small brushed propulsion motors (ESP32 GPIO cannot source enough current directly; needs a MOSFET low-side/H-bridge driver stage exactly as shown here).

Full verbatim description (from raw YouTube JSON, saved to a local temporary file) gives complete BOM with Amazon links per variant, a Google Drive project-files folder (schematics + Gerbers), a GitHub link, chapter timestamps, and links to 4 companion "Projects using Motor Drivers" videos — one of which (Mini Arduino FPV Drone, https://youtu.be/Sa6EslOHsI0) is likely the direct ancestor of the professor's third reference video.

BONUS CROSS-REFERENCE (verified via oEmbed, not requested but highly relevant): the professor's other two reference links resolve as: https://youtu.be/V_mZsiZcy7s = "Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY" — ALSO by channel Max Imagination; and https://youtu.be/Y5i3LntcNt0 = "ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone" — by a DIFFERENT channel, "Lehigh Swarms Club" (@LehighSwarmsClub). This means the professor's three links form one coherent build lineage: Max Imagination's ESP-FLY tiny ESP32 drone → Max Imagination's motor-driver tutorial (this video, teaching how ESP-FLY's own motor drivers are built) → Lehigh Swarms Club's ESP Blimp, which explicitly repurposes the ESP-FLY drone electronics into a lighter-than-air/blimp gondola. This is almost certainly the actual reference architecture the professor wants replicated with his own ESP32-S3 PCB.

### Gotchas

- Direct WebFetch of youtube.com/watch pages returned only nav/footer boilerplate every time (YouTube serves a JS-rendered shell to simple fetchers); the reliable path was (a) YouTube's own oEmbed JSON endpoint for title/author, and (b) a raw `curl` (with a desktop User-Agent) of the watch page followed by regex/grep extraction of the embedded ytInitialPlayerResponse/ytInitialData JSON for uploadDate, viewCount, lengthSeconds, shortDescription, subscriberCountText, etc. Future teardown-style tasks on YouTube URLs should go straight to oEmbed + raw curl+grep rather than iterating WebFetch prompts.
- The video's own description text is JSON-escaped (\n, \u00XX) inside the HTML payload — must run it through a JSON string decode (not naive unicode_escape, which breaks on non-ASCII) to recover readable text; python's `json.loads('"'+s+'"')` worked cleanly and was saved to a local temporary file.
- This video is easy to mis-scope: because the professor labeled it a 'blimp' reference alongside two actual blimp/drone videos, it would be easy to hallucinate blimp-specific content that simply is not there. It is purely a motor-driver electronics tutorial — relevant to the PROPULSION DRIVER STAGE of the gondola PCB, not to the envelope/lift/gas/frame.
- Amazon and JLCPCB/Flux links in the description are the creator's affiliate links (disclosed: 'As an Amazon Associate, I earn from qualifying purchases'); useful for sourcing identical parts but not authoritative pricing.
- Google Drive project-files folder access is explicitly gated behind the creator's own Terms & Conditions doc (linked in the description) — worth having the professor/team read that before redistributing any downloaded Gerbers.
- Bash tool's /tmp is not literally C:\...\tmp — it resolves to the user's Windows Temp dir (%TEMP%); files written via Bash must be located with `cygpath -w` (or similar) before the Read tool can open them by Windows path.

### Actionable

- Treat xW2Nwg_RX84 as the reference for the gondola's motor-driver stage only: replicate (or adapt) the 1-channel or 4-channel unidirectional SI2300+1N4148+10kΩ driver (or the H-bridge variant if reverse-thrust/differential/vectoring is wanted) to interface the professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCB GPIOs to the blimp's propulsion motors, instead of designing a driver stage from scratch.
- Independently pull and fully characterize https://youtu.be/Y5i3LntcNt0 ('ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone', channel Lehigh Swarms Club) as a matter of priority — it is the one existing reference build that is an actual blimp using this same ESP32/motor-driver lineage, and was not the video assigned to this run.
- Also pull https://youtu.be/V_mZsiZcy7s ('Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY', Max Imagination) since it is the drone platform both other reference videos build on/from.
- If the team wants the exact schematics/Gerbers for the motor driver boards, download the Google Drive folder https://drive.google.com/drive/folders/1oVloU0cYI_C_ffYhnXVZN4yCtFmBOQBR (after reading the creator's linked Terms doc) rather than reverse-engineering from the video.
- Check the linked GitHub repo https://github.com/s60sc/ESP32-CAM_MJPEG2SD for reusable ESP32 WiFi control-app code patterns, even though it targets ESP32-CAM rather than ESP32-S3 — architecture/patterns may still transfer.

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — The professor's three reference videos form a single lineage: V_mZsiZcy7s = 'Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY' by Max Imagination; xW2Nwg_RX84 = 'Guide to making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers with MOSFETs for RC Projects' by Max Imagination; Y5i3LntcNt0 = 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone' by Lehigh Swarms Club
- **CONFIRMED** — xW2Nwg_RX84 is NOT a blimp/airship video; it is a generic MOSFET brushed-motor-driver tutorial for RC vehicles
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Upload date 2025-01-02T06:19:11-08:00, length 1780s, 332,407 views, 9,741 likes
  - Correction: Core facts confirmed independently via my own curl of the watch page: lengthSeconds=1780 (exact match) and uploadDate=2025-01-02T06:19:11-08:00 (exact match). viewCount and likeCount are live counters that drift: my independent pull returned viewCount=332,416 (vs. reported 332,407) and likeCount=9,742 (vs. reported 9,741). This is expected normal growth/drift between scrape times, not an error, but exact view/like counts should never be treated as fixed facts in a written deliverable — cite them as 'approximately' or with a retrieval timestamp.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Demo microcontroller is the DFRobot FireBeetle 2 ESP32-S3-U board (product-2677), same MCU family as the professor's PCB
  - Correction: Board identity confirmed, but an important missed spec: product-2677 is specifically the 'FireBeetle 2 Board ESP32-S3-U (N16R8) AIoT Microcontroller WITH CAMERA' — it ships with a built-in OV2640 (2MP) camera module, 16MB flash, 8MB PSRAM, dual-core LX7 @ 240MHz, $20.90 list price. The 'family match' to the professor's PCB is only at the SoC level (ESP32-S3); this specific dev board is a camera-carrier board, not a bare/generic ESP32-S3 breakout, which is presumably why the video also links a camera-streaming GitHub repo (see next item). Don't assume the professor's own PCB has equivalent camera/PSRAM capability.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — The linked GitHub repo (https://github.com/s60sc/ESP32-CAM_MJPEG2SD) is characterized in the report as being 'for WiFi/RC control' and its reuse value framed around 'ESP32 WiFi control-app code patterns'
  - Correction: The repo's primary purpose is a camera motion-capture / MJPEG video recording+streaming application for ESP32 and ESP32-S3 boards with OV2640/OV3660/OV5640/PY260 camera modules (records to AVI on SD card, streams MJPEG to browser, does motion/PIR/accelerometer detection, FTP/HTTPS file management, RTSP, MQTT, Telegram alerts, Home Assistant integration). 'Remote vehicle control' is only one minor bundled feature, not the repo's core function. Framing it as a 'WiFi/RC control' reference is misleading for a propulsion/motor-driver-control reuse case — a team expecting drone-motor-control code patterns will instead find a camera-streaming codebase. The video's own description labels it correctly as 'ESP32 Camera Motion Capture & Control Application', so the mischaracterization is in the report's own prose/actionable summary, not in the source.
- **CONFIRMED** — Full BOM per driver variant: SI2300 (N-MOSFET), SI2301 (P-MOSFET), BC847B (NPN), 1N4148 flyback diodes, specific resistor/LED/cap values, perf-board pad counts, 30AWG/0.31mm wire
- **CONFIRMED** — Numeric performance specs per variant: 1CH 2-12V/3A/5x5mm/0.2g/~$0.15; 4CH 2-12V/3A per ch/6x20mm/1g/~$0.45; 1CH H-bridge 3V min/2A/13x8mm/1g/9mA idle/~$1.72
  - Correction: Confirmed, but flag the sourcing distinction: these dimension/weight/price/current numbers do NOT appear anywhere in the video's own YouTube description (I have the full raw decoded description and searched it — none of these figures are present). They exist only in the secondary DFRobot community makelog write-up, which I fetched independently and it matches the reported numbers exactly. Treat these as secondary-source (DFRobot blog author's) figures, not primary video-description data — the underlying video creator never states them in his own YouTube post.
- **REFUTED** — Troubleshooting guidance includes measuring motor stall current 'by gently stopping the shaft with pliers while powered'
  - Correction: I independently fetched the same cited source (DFRobot makelog) and its troubleshooting section describes testing stall current with a multimeter/power-supply current reading and comparing against the driver's rated limit — there is no mention of stopping the motor shaft with pliers. The other troubleshooting items (check flyback-diode polarity with a multimeter if a MOSFET fails shorted; keep the gate/pull-down resistor between 5-20kΩ; use an ESD wrist strap; use a fume extractor around the ~200°C hotplate) ARE corroborated. The 'pliers' detail specifically looks like an embellishment/hallucination not present in the cited source and should be dropped or re-verified against the actual video audio before being repeated.
- **CONFIRMED** — Video metadata: category Science & Technology, family-safe=true, channelId UCZFZjTtu-8X3H0Ymcy4-uEA, ~280k subscribers ('280 mil suscriptores')
- **CONFIRMED** — Chapter timestamps (Introduction, Uni Motor Driver, Uni Quad Motor Driver, H-bridge Motor Driver, Designing/Making PCBs, Understanding Motor Drivers, Microcontroller+Driver Demo, Troubleshooting, Wrap up)
- **CONFIRMED** — Project files (schematics/Gerbers) are gated behind a Google Drive folder and a separate Terms & Conditions doc

### Added by fact-checker

- The video's YouTube description contains ZERO occurrences of 'blimp', 'balloon', 'helium', 'envelope', 'airship', or 'lighter-than-air' (confirmed by direct grep of the independently re-decoded description) — this makes the report's central scope-mismatch warning (this is a motor-driver tutorial, not a blimp build) airtight, not just plausible.
- The demo board (DFRobot FireBeetle 2 ESP32-S3-U, product-2677) is a camera-carrier board (built-in OV2640, 16MB flash, 8MB PSRAM, $20.90) — it is not a bare/generic ESP32-S3 dev board, which is the likely reason the video links a camera-streaming GitHub repo rather than an RC-control repo. If the professor's own PCB lacks an onboard camera and PSRAM, the ESP32-CAM_MJPEG2SD repo has essentially no direct code reuse value for motor/propulsion control.
- The video description directly links FOUR sibling 'Projects using Motor Drivers' videos with the same MOSFET-driver lineage, useful as additional propulsion-integration references beyond the one flagged in the original report: FPV RC Paper Airplane (youtu.be/hDjBi0ErDdw), Mini Arduino FPV Drone (youtu.be/Sa6EslOHsI0), FPV RC Hot Wheels Car (youtu.be/nx7Ih79KevE), and RC Battle Bot 'Death Roll' (youtu.be/yGK8LqaogvU) — all primary-source links pulled directly from the raw description, not secondary paraphrase.
- The pull-down/gate resistor guidance from the (independently re-verified) DFRobot troubleshooting write-up is more specific than a flat '10k pull-down': it advises keeping the gate resistor within roughly 5-20 k-ohm range, not fixed at exactly 10k — worth noting if the team deviates from the exact BOM resistor value.

## video-3 (source)  
**RELIABILITY: high**

TARGET VIDEO IDENTIFICATION (confidence: HIGH for title/channel, LOW/UNVERIFIED for date/description/full content): Direct WebFetch of the youtu.be and youtube.com watch pages failed (YouTube serves JS-rendered pages that WebFetch cannot execute; one fetch returned only footer/legal boilerplate). I fell back to YouTube's own oEmbed endpoint (https://www.youtube.com/oembed?url=https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y5i3LntcNt0&format=json), which is a first-party, server-generated JSON API (not a scraped/summarized page, so not subject to model hallucination) and returned: title='ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone', author_name='Lehigh Swarms Club', author_url='https://www.youtube.com/@LehighSwarmsClub', thumbnail='https://i.ytimg.com/vi/Y5i3LntcNt0/hqdefault.jpg'. I could NOT retrieve: exact upload date, view/like counts, the full video description text (and therefore any description links to code/BOM/STLs), or a transcript. Attempts to fetch the channel's /videos page and to find the exact title via web search both failed to surface further corroborating metadata (search index does not have this niche title indexed with a date).

CONTEXTUAL RECONSTRUCTION (why this identification is credible): The title says the blimp is 'based on the ESPFLY drone' - this is almost certainly Seeed Studio's official 'ESP-FLY' DIY micro-quadcopter kit built around the XIAO ESP32-S3 (open repo github.com/Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY; covered by Elektor Magazine, CircuitDigest, Instructables, Elecrow). The uploading channel, 'Lehigh Swarms Club' (alt. handle found in search: 'Lehigh Blimps Club', @LehighBlimpsClub - likely the same club/possibly a renamed or secondary channel; NOT independently confirmed as identical, flagged as open question), maps directly to the Lehigh Aerial Swarms Club (LASC), founded 2022 by Prof. David Saldana (Lehigh SwarmsLab / AIRLab), which builds a blimp every semester for the 'Defend the Republic' (DTR) LTA robot competition (sites.google.com/lehigh.edu/dtr2023b/home; swarmslab.com/club). This lab is also the origin of the 'MochiSwarm' blimp platform (arXiv 2503.03077, code at github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm) and of 'mochi-swarm-sim', of which the professor's referenced repo github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim is CONFIRMED (via GitHub API) to be a fork of github.com/vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim, itself in the same Lehigh/MochiSwarm lineage. So the video is very plausibly a Lehigh club build log showing a student soldering/mounting a Seeed ESP-FLY-style ESP32-S3 flight-controller board into a lighter-than-air gondola - i.e., structurally almost the exact task the professor's team is undertaking (custom ESP32-S3 PCB -> blimp gondola).

LEHIGH BLIMP GROUP HARDWARE/FIRMWARE (extracted from GitHub org 'LehighBlimpGroup', all primary-source repos, confirmed to exist via GitHub API on 2026-09-21): repo 'BlimpCAD' has Fusion360 CAD + STL files including foamframe3-bigballoon-gear.stl (a foam-core gondola frame sized for a 'bigballoon'), gear_final.stl, and battery_holder_18350.stl (an 18350 Li-ion cell holder) plus an Eagle-format custom PCB ('Blimp_ESP_board_standalone_IC_power.sch/.brd') - i.e. a bespoke ESP32 flight-control board, same category as the professor's PCB. Repo 'DC_motor_mochi_blimp' contains the actual flight firmware 'AsyncUDPServerAIRLabActualOriginal.ino': ESP32 (Arduino core, ESP32Servo + AsyncUDP libraries) running Wi-Fi station mode, listening for UDP joystick packets on port 1234 (4 floats: 2x servo angle commands on pins 9/10 for thrust-vectoring, 2x PWM 'thrust' commands via analogWrite on pins A2/A3 for two brushless/DC motors) - i.e. a 2-motor + 2-servo bicopter-style vectored-thrust differential-drive layout, controlled from a ground-station Python script (joystickUDP.py, repo 'differential_blimp_mochi') over WiFi UDP. Other sibling repos: 'sensfusion_10DOF' (Arduino 10-DOF - gyro+accel+mag+baro - sensor fusion), 'S-Blimp-XIAO-S3-DC' / 'S-Blimp-XIAO-C3-BLDC' (Spinning-Blimp Arduino firmware variants for XIAO ESP32-S3/C3 with OptiTrack NatNet motion-capture logging scripts), 'FOMO_nicla_balloon' (Edge Impulse FOMO neural net, MobileNetV2+2 CNN layers, 96x96 input, ~15fps, deployed on a Nicla Vision, for purple/green target-balloon detection), 'Reflective_AimBot' and 'Blob-detection-and-Tracking' (retro-reflective-goal detection via IR-flash frame differencing, serial to ESP32), 'BlimpFlie' (a Crazyflie-inspired 2.4GHz radio transceiver framework: one ESP32 acts as ground antenna/transceiver, the onboard ESP32 runs BicopterControl.ino, commands sent as flag-coded packets), and 'bicopter_control' (CoppeliaSim .ttt simulation of a bicopter-blimp physics model). Full academic engineering numbers for the Lehigh MochiSwarm platform, from the arXiv paper (2503.03077, ICRA 2025, Xu/Vu/D'Antonio/Saldana): envelope = 50-inch diameter spherical foil/mylar balloon, helium lift = 120 g net buoyancy; gondola = laser-cut foam-core board frame, 18.7 g, tool-free interlocking-latch assembly (no glue/screws); propulsion = 2x servo-rotor stacks (differential drive, each a microservo + 1-cell brushless rotor, 50 Hz PWM); MCU = Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 (22x17x4 mm), firmware loop at 200 Hz on the 240 MHz dual-core Xtensa LX7; sensors = 9-DoF IMU (accel+gyro+mag) + barometer; vision = separate Nicla Vision board over UART for balloon detection; unit cost ~$132.74 (base) + $115 (NiclaVision) = ~$247.74/unit; control = hybrid feedback-feedforward visual servoing state machine (Manual / Random Walk / MoveToGoal / PassThroughGoal). An earlier, cheaper Lehigh design ('A Novel Low-Cost, Recyclable, Easy-to-Build Robot Blimp', GHTC 2023, arXiv 2309.06682, Li/Hou/Negash/Xu/Jeffs/D'Antonio/Saldana) gives: 0.125 m^3 ellipsoid mylar envelope, 65 g buoyancy; foldable origami-style laser-cut foam-core gondola rated to 5 kgf structural loads, held to the balloon by two X-configuration sewing-elastic bands with plastic tension clips; ESP32-S3 flight controller (1.7 g bare, 21x17.5 mm); actuation = 2 brushed DC motors ($2.40 ea) + 2 servos ($0.80 ea) forming a bicopter vectored-thrust pair, plus a $1.49 motor driver; 9-axis IMU sensor board $4.88; total vehicle cost $27.42; 50 g demonstrated payload; tested outdoors in winds up to 2 m/s; control = PID position hold + manual egocentric joystick (x-z translation + yaw).

DEFEND THE REPUBLIC (DTR) COMPETITION RULES (primary source: AIAA paper Taylor & Dantsker 2025, 'Lighter-Than-Air-Vehicle Design for Adversarial Defense', DOI 10.2514/6.2025-3124, Indiana University - this is the actual competition several of the reference videos/repos are built for, and is closely analogous in format to the professor's target competitions): max helium budget 50 cubic feet per team; max negative buoyancy 100 g when inflated; game = two 30-min halves + 20-min halftime; each half subdivided into six 5-min intervals = 30 s manual control + 4.5 min autonomous-only flight; scoring = capturing neutrally-buoyant helium balloons and placing them through colored/shaped hoops in the opponent's goal, with point value scaled to autonomy level used; teams (2023-2025 seasons) included Arizona State, Baylor, Boston University, Drexel, George Mason, Georgia Tech, Indiana University, Lehigh, Penn State, U. Michigan, Virginia Tech, West Virginia. The paper also documents 3 generations of a pure-interference (non-scoring) vehicle: 'Swordfish' (Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense Rev2, Bosch BMI270 IMU + BMM150 magnetometer + STMicro LPS22HB barometer, 3x brushless motors on a 3S 800mAh pack, simple P-controllers holding altitude/heading, built in 2 days from spare parts), 'Titan' (single large spherical balloon, bigger motors, RC-receiver-only manual control, 2S 600mAh pack, large-brushless-motor+3in-prop x3, found to have uncontrollable yaw at speed but could do an aerobatic Immelmann), and 'Witch' (RC-only, 2S 300mAh pack, mixed large 3in-prop + small 2in-prop motors, added a tail fin for yaw damping and small hooks to snag targets - the team's final, most successful design, emphasizing low cost/simplicity over autonomy). A companion 2026 AIAA paper (Dempsey/Taylor/Dantsker, DOI 10.2514/6.2026-2571) gives a full weight budget for an IU scoring/tracking LTA vehicle: XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense w/OV2640 camera (8.0 g), BNO055+BMP280 'BFF' sensor backpack (5.0 g), 3.7V 350mAh Li-Ion battery (10.0 g), 3x 1S/7A brushless ESCs (8.0 g), 3x brushless motors+props (10.0 g), 3D-printed structure (20.0 g) = 61.0 g total payload; carried by a mylar balloon rated 70 g lift (9 g margin for wiring/ballast). Propulsion layout = 2 side-mounted differential-drive thrusters + 1 rear-mounted vertical thruster. Control = off-board (ground-station) architecture: onboard ESP32 streams camera+IMU+barometer over WiFi to a ground PC running YOLOv8 (60% confidence threshold) for balloon detection, a custom 2D velocity filter (5-sample sliding window, 0.1 m/s^2 accel-gate threshold) for self-motion, per-target constant-velocity Kalman-style trackers with nearest-neighbor data association for opponents/balloons, and a PID-based lateral+vertical autopilot with a Pygame HUD for the operator; this off-board-compute pattern is presented explicitly as the way to get real vision autonomy onto a payload this light.

GEORGIA TECH GT-MAB (fallback reference, general web-search-derived, not primary-source-verified numbers): a 'saucer-shaped' tailless, omnidirectional indoor blimp platform from Georgia Tech Systems Research lab; used in >20 published papers (autopilot design, human-following, swing-oscillation control, Naval Research Lab 25+-unit swarm demo); carries camera(s), single-point lasers, IMU. I could NOT retrieve hard numbers (envelope size, weight, motor/prop specs, MCU part number, cost) - the two pages I fetched (GTSR site, a student's project-writeup site) gave only qualitative descriptions; the underlying numbers likely exist in the cited IEEE/ResearchGate papers (e.g. 'Autopilot design for a class of miniature autonomous blimps', IEEE 2017, DOI via ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/8062564) which I did not open.

ARDUPILOT 'BLIMP' VEHICLE FIRMWARE (primary source, ardupilot.org/blimp/docs/building-a-blimp.html): an official ArduPilot vehicle type built specifically for tiny autonomous LTA vehicles, using an unusual thrust-vectoring scheme - 4 independent flapping fins (each on its own ~1.7 g micro servo) providing X/Y/Z and yaw control while a pendulum effect self-stabilizes roll/pitch (no propellers at all in the reference design). Flight controller: as light as possible, Matek F405-STD (9 g) cited as the most field-tested board; lighter (2-3 g) autopilots mentioned as further options. Envelope: ~50 cm spherical foil party balloon, helium, ~50 g lift budget. Gondola: balsa wood + plastic-freezer-bag fin frames. Power: 150-300 mAh 1S LiPo + a step-up regulator. RC link: OrangeRx Nano DSM2/DSMX receiver (0.5 g). Telemetry: ESP8266 (ESP-01/ESP-07) WiFi modules. This is a strong, well-documented, PID-flight-stack fallback reference distinct from the ESP32-native approach.

ESP-FLY / ESPFLY BASE PLATFORM (primary sources: github.com/Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY, elektormagazine.com, instructables.com), i.e. the platform the target video says it adapted: MCU = Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 (dual-core 240 MHz Xtensa LX7, 8MB flash/8MB PSRAM, WiFi+BT5.0, USB-C); IMU = MPU-6050 6-axis, on a custom 4-layer flight-controller PCB with 4x N-channel MOSFET (SI2300) motor drivers + status LEDs; 4x '615' coreless DC motors (2CW/2CCW, pre-wired), ~70,000 RPM, ~17 g thrust each (2.7:1 thrust/weight); 8x 30mm tri-blade props (4CW/4CCW, 0.8mm shaft); battery = 1S LiPo 250 mAh (3.7V, JST-PH), 150-350mAh compatible, ~5-5.5 min flight time; frame = 3D-printed, 4 g, 67x67x31mm w/props (46x46x29mm w/o); weights: 18 g w/o battery, 25 g w/battery, 28 g w/FPV cam, max ~3 g extra payload; firmware options = Espressif's own ESP-Drone (WiFi phone-app control, ~50 m range) or 'rtlopez ESP-FC' (Betaflight-compatible, angle/acro flight modes, self-leveling, RC via ESP-NOW ~200 m range); GPL-3.0 licensed; product page seeedstudio.com/ESP-FLY-co-create-p-6744.html; companion tutorial video youtu.be/3Y_drsQtMs4.

FALLBACK DIY-BLIMP REFERENCES (general web search, not video-specific, snippet-level only): Instructables 'Sub Micro (Spy) Blimp' (~10 g propulsion pack, 11-inch latex balloon), 'Pico Blimp' (single canted main motor + carbon-rod tail rotor), 'Build an RC Blimp for Less Than $13' and its Hackaday coverage (11-inch party balloon lifting ~10 g, recycled RC-car parts, sub-2.5g micro servos, sub-2g micro RX, 32mm 'butterfly' props on 0.7mm shafts, 1mm Depron foam + putty ballast); Hackster.io 'Blimp DIY' (3-servo design: rudder + motor-tilt + payload servo, carbon-fiber-stick fin linkage); PopSci 'Make Your Own Remote-Control Spy Blimp'; 'The Crafty Robot' RC-blimp blog build. None of these were opened for full text (only search-snippet level); flagged as low-depth fallback pointers only.

### Gotchas

- YouTube pages are JS-rendered and resist plain WebFetch; the oEmbed JSON endpoint (youtube.com/oembed?url=...&format=json) is the reliable fallback for title/author/thumbnail, but it does NOT return upload date, description text, or transcript - those require a different route (captions API, a transcript-mirror site, or the channel's community/video listing, none of which I could successfully scrape here).
- DTR-style competitions impose HARD mass/gas budgets (e.g. 50 cu.ft. helium, 100 g max negative buoyancy at IU's DTR) - any BOM the team assembles (custom ESP32-S3 PCB + battery + motors + sensors) must be weighed against the specific rules of the UMNG/IAR-LARC competition (not yet retrieved by this agent) before committing to a design, since Lehigh/IU numbers (18-61 g payloads) are calibrated to THEIR rules, not necessarily the Colombian/IAR-LARC ones.
- Every one of the well-documented reference platforms (Lehigh MochiSwarm/BlimpSwarm, IU Swordfish/Titan/Witch, ArduPilot Blimp) treats offboard/ground-station compute as essential once vision is involved - onboard ESP32-S3 alone is used for actuation + IMU/baro + WiFi streaming, while YOLOv8/FOMO-class vision inference runs on a laptop or a separate NPU-equipped SBC/Nicla Vision, not on the ESP32 itself. Teams that try to run vision entirely on the ESP32-S3 will likely hit compute limits that already-published Lehigh/IU work identifies as a design constraint.
- Multiple designs report instability/control problems when propulsion is upsized without matching torque/damping: IU's 'Titan' (bigger motors, less drag) became so yaw-unstable it had to be flown manual-only; lesson explicitly stated: thrust-vectoring/authority must be balanced against yaw damping (a tail fin fixed this in 'Witch').
- Buoyancy margin is consistently tiny (Lehigh: 9 g margin on 70 g lift; older Lehigh design: 65 g lift for a 50 g payload) - every gram of custom PCB, connector, wiring, and battery matters; several designs (Lehigh, GHTC 2023) deliberately avoid glue/screws (elastic-band or interlocking-latch mounting) purely to save weight and allow rapid field rebuilds.
- The IU paper explicitly separates altitude control (vertical PID, vision- or memory-based) from horizontal (x-y) control (inertial dead-reckoning + PID) rather than fusing into one 6-DoF EKF - a deliberate simplification because blimp dynamics are slow/high-drag, which is a reusable design pattern.
- Confidence caveat: I could not confirm whether 'Lehigh Swarms Club' and 'Lehigh Blimps Club' (@LehighBlimpsClub, also found in search) are the same YouTube channel or two different ones; if the parent orchestrator needs to browse more of this channel's catalog, both handles should be checked.

### Actionable

- Treat the Lehigh 'LehighBlimpGroup' GitHub org (github.com/LehighBlimpGroup) as the single richest fallback/reference codebase available: it has ready-to-adapt Arduino/ESP32 firmware for exactly the servo+motor bicopter-style thrust-vectoring scheme (DC_motor_mochi_blimp, S-Blimp-XIAO-S3-DC, S-Blimp-XIAO-C3-BLDC, BlimpFlie, bicopter_control), a 10-DOF sensor-fusion library (sensfusion_10DOF), balloon-detection NN code for a Nicla Vision (FOMO_nicla_balloon) or plain frame-differencing for retro-reflective goals (Reflective_AimBot, Blob-detection-and-Tracking), and actual CAD/STL + PCB files (BlimpCAD) - all directly reusable/adaptable given the team already has a custom ESP32-S3 PCB and no 3D-print constraints.
- Because the professor's own mochi-swarm-sim reference is confirmed to be a fork of the Lehigh MochiSwarm lineage, it is worth having the professor clarify whether he has direct contact with Lehigh SwarmsLab (Prof. Saldana) or simply found the public repos - if there is a personal connection, that is a fast path to get the un-retrievable video description/date and any unpublished CAD/BOM.
- Before locking in a BOM, get the actual UMNG/IAR-LARC rulebook's helium volume / negative-buoyancy / weight limits (not yet retrieved by this agent - flagged for whichever sub-agent covers the competition rules) and cross-check against the Lehigh/IU numbers above, since those numbers are calibrated to DIFFERENT competitions (DTR's 50 cu ft / 100 g).
- For the control architecture, the most load-bearing published lesson across ALL these sources is: keep onboard ESP32-S3 code to actuation + IMU/baro + WiFi telemetry, and run any camera/vision inference off-board (ground laptop, or a separate low-power vision coprocessor like Nicla Vision/OpenMV) - copy this pattern rather than trying to run object detection on the ESP32-S3 itself.
- Consider ArduPilot's 'Blimp' firmware (ardupilot.org/blimp) as an alternative/inspiration to a from-scratch Arduino sketch if the team wants a more mature, already-tuned PID/attitude-estimation stack, even though it targets a different (STM32-based) flight controller than the ESP32-S3 PCB already fabricated - at minimum its flapping-fin thrust-vectoring concept and its documented balloon-size/lift-budget numbers are a useful sanity check.
- Retrieve and read in full (not yet done by this agent, due to scope): the IEEE GT-MAB autopilot paper (ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/8062564) for hard numbers on that platform, and the video's full YouTube description (via a transcript/caption mirror or by having someone with a browser open the page) to recover any BOM/STL links the uploader may have posted.
