### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — Target video Y5i3LntcNt0 is titled 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone', by channel 'Lehigh Swarms Club' (@LehighSwarmsClub)
- **CONFIRMED** — DTR competition hard physical limits: max helium budget 50 cu.ft. per team; max negative buoyancy 100 g when inflated
- **CONFIRMED** — DTR match structure: two 30-min halves + 20-min halftime; each half = six 5-min intervals of 30s manual control + 4.5 min autonomous-only flight
- **CONFIRMED** — Professor's reference repo github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim is a GitHub fork of github.com/vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim
- **CONFIRMED** — MochiSwarm platform: 50-inch spherical foil balloon w/ 120 g net buoyancy; 18.7 g laser-cut foam-core gondola; XIAO ESP32-S3 running flight loop at 200 Hz on 240 MHz dual-core Xtensa LX7; 9-DoF IMU + barometer; Nicla Vision for balloon detection; unit cost $132.74 base + $115 NiclaVision = ~$247.74
- **CONFIRMED** — MochiSwarm's Nicla Vision connects to the XIAO ESP32-S3 via UART
  - Correction: No correction needed — this specific interface detail was asserted with some hedging in the original summary prose; it is fully verifiable and correct, so its confidence should be raised, not lowered.
- **CONFIRMED** — ESP-FLY (Seeed Co-Create kit) specs: XIAO ESP32-S3 + MPU-6050 on custom PCB; 4x '615' coreless motors ~70,000 RPM / ~17 g thrust each; 8x 30 mm tri-blade props; 1S 250 mAh LiPo (~5 min flight); ~4 g 3D-printed frame; total weight 18/25/28 g variants; supports Espressif ESP-Drone or rtlopez ESP-FC firmware
- **CONFIRMED** — ArduPilot's official 'Blimp' vehicle firmware uses 4 flapping fins (no propellers) on ~1.7 g micro-servos with pendulum-based passive roll/pitch stability; reference FC is Matek F405-STD (9 g); ~50 cm foil balloon (~50 g lift); balsa+freezer-bag fins; 150-300 mAh 1S LiPo; OrangeRx Nano RX (0.5 g); ESP8266 telemetry
- **CONFIRMED** — 2023 low-cost Lehigh blimp (arXiv 2309.06682): 0.125 m^3 ellipsoid mylar envelope, 65 g buoyancy; foam-core gondola rated to 5 kgf; ESP32-S3 FC (1.7 g, 21x17.5mm); 2 brushed motors ($2.40 ea) + 2 servos ($0.80 ea) + driver ($1.49); total vehicle cost $27.42; 50 g demonstrated payload; tested to 2 m/s outdoor wind
  - Correction: Minor addition, not a correction: the paper also states the bare ESP32-S3 flight-controller board itself costs $5, a specific line-item the original summary omitted from its cost breakdown.
- **CONFIRMED** — IU 'Swordfish'/'Titan'/'Witch' interference-vehicle specs and the lesson that 'Titan' became yaw-unstable at speed (but could perform an Immelmann) while 'Witch' fixed this with a tail fin and added hooks
- **CONFIRMED** — IU vision-autonomy LTA weight budget: XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense+OV2640 8.0g, BNO055+BMP280 backpack 5.0g, 3.7V 350mAh Li-Ion 10.0g, 3x 1S/7A brushless ESC 8.0g, 3x brushless motor+prop 10.0g, 3D-printed structure 20.0g, total payload 61.0g; mylar balloon rated 70g lift (9g margin); control = off-board YOLOv8 (60% confidence) + 5-sample/0.1 m/s^2 velocity filter + PID autopilot + Pygame HUD
- **REFUTED** — A second YouTube channel handle, '@LehighBlimpsClub' ('Lehigh Blimps Club'), may be the same or a sibling channel to '@LehighSwarmsClub' and this was left as an unresolved open question
  - Correction: Independently fetched https://www.youtube.com/@LehighBlimpsClub directly: it returns HTTP 404 Not Found. There is no live evidence this second channel exists (it may never have existed, or the handle string picked up by the original researcher's search was a mis-transcription/dead handle). '@LehighSwarmsClub' should be treated as the correct, singular channel for this material unless a person with browser access finds the other handle elsewhere.
- **CONFIRMED** — LehighBlimpGroup GitHub org repos (BlimpCAD with CAD/PCB folders; DC_motor_mochi_blimp with AsyncUDPServerAIRLabActualOriginal.ino) actually exist with the claimed structure
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — Georgia Tech GT-MAB hard numbers (envelope size, weight, motor/prop, MCU, cost)

### Added by fact-checker

- DTR is explicitly confirmed (by the primary AIAA source, not inference) to be a biannual (twice-per-year) collegiate tournament-bracket competition among a fixed roster of ~12 US universities, and IS DIFFERENT from the professor's actual target competitions (UMNG Mecatronica, Bogota; IAR-LARC, Oakland University) — none of these DTR numeric limits (50 cu ft helium / 100 g negative buoyancy / 30-min halves) are known to transfer to UMNG or IAR-LARC, whose actual rulebooks were NOT checked by this fact-check pass either (out of scope for 'video-3'). This is the single highest-risk gap in the whole research chain and should be resolved before any BOM is locked in.
- The Dempsey/Taylor/Dantsker 2026 paper's Figure 1 caption states IU's blimps competed 'at LeHigh University,' independently corroborating (via a primary source, not just inference from the professor's forked repo) that Lehigh SwarmsLab is an active DTR host/participant site, strengthening the video-identification chain.
- The 2023 low-cost Lehigh blimp paper (arXiv 2309.06682) itemizes the bare ESP32-S3 flight-controller board's cost as $5 — a specific BOM line-item the original researcher's cost breakdown omitted.
- MochiSwarm's inter-robot/ground link uses the ESP-NOW protocol at 512 Kbps with up to 480 m range — a concrete communications spec (useful for planning a multi-robot or ground-control link) that was absent from the original researcher's 'specs' table even though it appears in the same primary source they cited.
- The '@LehighBlimpsClub' YouTube handle the original researcher flagged as a possible duplicate/renamed channel currently 404s — treat '@LehighSwarmsClub' as the sole channel and stop budgeting research time on reconciling two handles.

## repo-mochi (source)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

mochi-swarm-sim (github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim, branch main, forked from vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim; 31 commits, 0 stars/forks) is a pure-Python MuJoCo simulator for a Lehigh University "MochiSwarm" robotic blimp. Confirmed lab link: MochiSwarm is built by SwarmsLab (PI Dr. David Saldana, Lehigh University, Bethlehem PA), aka the Autonomous and Intelligent Robotics Laboratory (AIRLab); "Tongshu Wu" is a named contributor on the group's ICRA2025 paper "MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments" (arXiv 2503.03077), confirming the repo owner is a lab member and this sim models the real Mochi 3.0 hardware. Stack: numpy, mujoco, glfw, scipy, matplotlib, opencv-python (all Windows-wheel-compatible); entry point `python main.py [track-key|xml-path]` loads one of three MuJoCo XML course models (default/S-curve/figure-8 balloon-gate courses), builds `Controller`+`Simulation`, steps physics at dt=0.01s while rendering at ~60Hz, and plots start/end/path trajectories to outputs/trajectory_plots on exit. The blimp model: single ellipsoid/spherical main envelope (sim: radius 0.5, buoyancy "gravity compensation" factor 3.2872; real hardware per the paper: 50-inch foil balloon, 18.7g laser-cut foam-core frame, 120g net buoyancy) driven by two independent tilting servo+brushless-rotor thrust pods ("bicopter" layout) — state = position r∈R3, orientation R∈SO(3)/Euler(roll,pitch,yaw), plus derivatives; dynamics are Newton-Euler with gravity+buoyancy force fe=[0,0,fb-mg]^T, linear air drag via diagonal damping matrices Df/Dτ, and thrust force whose direction is set per-pod by servo angle. Actuator/control numeric parameters pulled directly from source: MuJoCo model gives motor thrust range 0–0.3924 N/side, servo range ±π, air density 1.225 kg/m3, viscosity 1.81e-5; src/preferences.py gives the working control-limit envelope (FX ±0.35, FZ [-1.4,1.4], TZ ±0.08, servo ±1.45 rad, max thrust 0.72) and PID gains (Z: KPZ 0.65/KDZ 0.45/KIZ 0.0 with integral clamp [0,0.12]; yaw: KPYAW 1.0/KDYAW 0.02/KIYAW 0.0). src/robot/differential.py implements the actual mixer: servo_angle=atan2(Fz,Fx), thrust_mag=hypot(Fz,Fx), split across the two rotors by commanded yaw torque, clipped to [0,1] thrust / [-pi,pi] angle — this exactly mirrors the real BlimpSwarm Arduino firmware's control scheme, so it is directly reusable as a control-law reference for the professor's own ESP32-S3 PCB firmware. Competition/task logic in the sim is a timed "BlimpRace" through a sequence of 9 colored hanging balloon gates (course laid out straight/S-curve/figure-8; each gate: sphere r=0.34, mass 0.030 kg, buoyant via its own gravity-compensation=1.22, hung on a 3-link damped rope) tracked with a simulated Nicla-Vision-style color camera; src/state/ implements this as an explicit state machine (ManualState, AutoGateSequenceState) plus a spiral_search.py fallback behavior for re-acquiring a lost gate — i.e., the built-in "game rule" here is sequential gate-passing/racing, not the pickup-and-delivery capture task described in the real MochiSwarm paper, so the two diverge and the professor's actual competition rules (UMNG/LARC) must be checked against both before choosing which task model to imitate. Windows usability: everything is pip-installable pure Python/OpenGL (mujoco, glfw, opencv-python, scipy, matplotlib all ship win64 wheels), Python 3.10+ required (uses `str | None` PEP604 syntax); the only Windows risk is glfw's OpenGL context needing a real GPU/driver (should be fine on a normal Windows dev PC, may need `--force` mesa/software fallback in headless CI). Sibling/real-hardware repos found: github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm (real ESP32-S3 "XIAO_ESP32S3" Arduino/C++ firmware + ground-station framework, GPL-3.0, 237 commits — this is the actual robot firmware, with docs site lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/), github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/balltraining and github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/Blob-detection-and-Tracking (OpenMV/Nicla-Vision color- and IR-reflective-blob tracking used for perception), and a Notion assembly guide (could not extract content programmatically). TongshuWu1's other repos (aerowhip, Cable_on_surface, PRISMS, Tony_Swarm, Blimp) are mostly unrelated/older side projects; "Blimp" (GPL-3.0, 2024) looks like an early precursor of BlimpSwarm's ESP32 framework by the same author.

### Key findings

- **[high]** mochi-swarm-sim is a MuJoCo-based Python simulator for Lehigh University's MochiSwarm robotic blimp platform, not a from-scratch project
  - Forked from vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim; entry point main.py loads MuJoCo XML models named mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course*.xml; dependencies are numpy, mujoco, glfw, scipy, matplotlib, opencv-python.
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim/main/main.py>
- **[high]** The repo is directly linked to Lehigh's SwarmsLab (PI Dr. David Saldana) / AIRLab and its Mochi 3.0 blimp hardware
  - Repo owner 'Tongshu Wu' is listed as a contributor on the ICRA2025 paper 'MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments' (arXiv 2503.03077), whose corresponding lab is AIRLab/SwarmsLab, Lehigh University (contact saldana@lehigh.edu). SwarmsLab's own site describes Mochi 3.0 as the 'swarm-ready miniature blimp' shown at Defend the Republic Spring 2024.
  - <https://arxiv.org/html/2503.03077v1>
- **[high]** Real hardware per the MochiSwarm paper: 50-inch spherical foil balloon, 18.7 g laser-cut foam-core frame, ~120 g net buoyancy from helium, two servo+1-cell-brushless-rotor thrust pods (bicopter layout), XIAO ESP32-S3 flight computer at 200 Hz, ESP-NOW comms (~480 m range, 512 kbps), Nicla Vision perception board (color/IR-blob detection at 10-30 Hz)
  - Cost breakdown in the paper: '$132.74 + $115 (NiclaVision)' per unit. Real firmware repo confirms XIAO_ESP32S3 as the Arduino board target.
  - <https://arxiv.org/html/2503.03077v1>
- **[high]** Simulator's actuator/control numeric values are gold for controller design
  - MuJoCo XML: motor thrust 0-0.3924 N/side, servo range +-pi, air density 1.225 kg/m3, viscosity 1.81e-5, dt=0.01s, main balloon gravity-compensation factor 3.2872, gate balloons r=0.34 m mass 0.030 kg gravity-compensation 1.22 on 3-link damped tether (damping 0.20/0.18/0.16). preferences.py: force/servo limits FX+-0.35, FZ[-1.4,1.4], TZ+-0.08, servo+-1.45 rad, max thrust 0.72; PID gains KPZ 0.65/KDZ 0.45/KIZ 0.0 (integral clamp [0,0.12]), KPYAW 1.0/KDYAW 0.02/KIYAW 0.0.
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim/main/src/preferences.py>
- **[high]** The bicopter mixer implemented in src/robot/differential.py matches the real BlimpSwarm firmware's control scheme, making it a directly reusable reference
  - Differential.control(): servo_angle=atan2(Fz_target,Fx_target); thrust_mag=hypot(Fz,Fx); thrust split between the two rotors based on commanded yaw torque; outputs clipped to [0,1] thrust and [-pi,pi] angle. Z-axis and yaw loops use PID with atan2-based angle wrapping and integral windup limiting.
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim/main/src/robot/differential.py>
- **[high]** The sim's built-in task is a timed race through a sequence of colored hanging-balloon 'gates' (straight, S-curve, and figure-8 course variants), tracked with a state machine + spiral-search recovery behavior — this differs from the real MochiSwarm paper's pickup-and-delivery capture task
  - models/ has mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml, _s.xml (S-curve), _figure8.xml, each with 9 colored gate balloons. src/state/ has auto_gate_sequence_state.py, spiral_search.py, robot_state_machine.py, manual_state.py. The real hardware paper (2503.03077) instead describes a Multi-Agent Pickup-and-Delivery task: blimps net-capture red target balloons and deliver them through one of three shaped goal hoops in a 20x15m industrial highbay with up to 1 m/s wind.
  - <https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim/tree/main/src/state>
- **[high]** Real ESP32-S3 firmware and ground-station code for the physical blimp exists in a sibling repo, github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm, GPL-3.0, 237 commits, with a docs site and a bicopter parts/assembly guide
  - Firmware examples include Bicopter1raw (direct joystick-to-actuator control) and BaseTranseiver (ESP-NOW base station); Arduino IDE 2.3.0+, board = XIAO_ESP32S3. Bicopter core parts list totals ~$83.74 (2x brushless motors $20, 2x ESCs $20, micro ESP32 $6.50, 1x servo $3.50, helium balloon $4.00, battery+charger $19.00), +$43 for joystick/USB hub, +$49 for the autonomous sensor suite (ultrasonic, barometer, IMU), full build up to $175.74. Perception stack uses OpenMV IDE + a 'Blob-Detection-and-Tracking' library and a Vision Module PCB for the Nicla Vision camera.
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm/main/docs/bicopter/index.md>
- **[medium]** A separate, apparently non-Lehigh blimp-swarm project exists in the literature and should not be conflated with SwarmsLab/Mochi
  - arXiv 2505.09511, 'Design of a Formation Control System to Assist Human Operators in Flying a Swarm of Robotic Blimps' (authors Tianfu Wu, Jiaqi Fu, Wugang Meng, Sungjin Cho, Huanzhe Zhan, Fumin Zhang) also uses ESP32-based robotic blimps and ESP32; Fumin Zhang is historically associated with Georgia Tech blimp/underwater-glider work, not Lehigh. PDF text layer did not yield extractable dimensions/mass/equations (embedded as images) or a repo link — flagged for a possible separate research thread, not verified as related to TongshuWu1's repo.
  - <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.09511>
- **[medium]** mochi-swarm-sim is Windows-usable with a standard pip install; no OS-specific blockers found in its dependency list
  - requirements.txt: numpy, mujoco, glfw, scipy, matplotlib, opencv-python — all publish win64 wheels on PyPI. Code requires Python 3.10+ (uses `str | None` PEP 604 union syntax in main.py). Risk not directly verified: glfw's OpenGL window creation needs a real GPU/driver context, which is normally fine on a Windows desktop/laptop but could fail in a headless/VM/CI environment without a software GL fallback (untested).
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim/main/requirements.txt>

### Gotchas

- The simulator's built-in competition task (sequential gate-race through hanging balloons) is NOT the same as the real Lehigh MochiSwarm hardware task (net-capture-and-deliver to hoops) — do not assume the sim's scoring/behavior code models the professor's actual contest; check UMNG/LARC rules first, then pick or rewrite the state-machine behaviors (src/state/robot_state_machine.py) accordingly.
- This mochi-swarm-sim fork has only 31 commits, 0 stars/forks, and its owner's more recently active repo (aerowhip, updated as late as the searched listing date) is unrelated to blimps — treat this as a snapshot student/lab tool, not an actively maintained public project; expect rough edges, undocumented code, and possible drift from the current physical Mochi 3.0 firmware.
- Buoyancy/mass numbers in the MuJoCo XML (radius, density, 'gravity compensation' factors) are simulator-internal tuning values to achieve neutral buoyancy in MuJoCo's fluid model, not literal physical material densities — don't copy them as real helium-balloon material properties without re-deriving from the professor's own envelope volume/payload mass.
- The real hardware paper's per-unit cost ($132.74 + $115 Nicla Vision) and the BlimpSwarm bicopter BOM (~$83.74 core) are Lehigh's parts choices circa the 2025 paper/docs and may not reflect current 2026 prices or parts availability — re-verify before budgeting.
- The professor's PCB already uses ESP32-S3, which matches BlimpSwarm's XIAO_ESP32S3 target closely enough that its Arduino firmware (Bicopter1raw, BaseTranseiver examples) is a plausible adaptation base, but pin mappings/peripheral wiring will differ from a custom PCB and were not compared here.
- The GitHub Notion-hosted robot-assembly guide (rhinestone-star-fa9.notion.site) would not render through the fetch tool used here — its BOM/part-number/vendor-link content is unverified and needs a direct browser visit.
- WebFetch's page summarizer repeatedly failed to surface the BlimpSwarm docs site's FAQ/troubleshooting Q&A verbatim (only paraphrases were recoverable, e.g., loose servo/motor connectors, pitch-feedback causing startup spin, BNO restart procedures, PCB/ESP32 failure as last-resort diagnosis) — treat these as paraphrased leads, not exact rulebook-grade quotes, and re-pull the live page directly before citing wording.

### Actionable

- Adopt mochi-swarm-sim as the software-in-the-loop testbed: `pip install numpy mujoco glfw scipy matplotlib opencv-python` (Python 3.10+), then `python main.py` or `python main.py models/mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml` (or `s`/`figure8` track keys) — no code changes needed to get a running blimp sim on Windows.
- Port the bicopter mixer/PID architecture from src/robot/differential.py (servo_angle=atan2(Fz,Fx), thrust=hypot(Fz,Fx) split by yaw torque; PID for Z-height and yaw with the gain/limit values in src/preferences.py) as the first draft control law for the professor's ESP32-S3 firmware, since it already matches the real BlimpSwarm firmware's approach rather than inventing a new mixer from scratch.
- Use github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm as the primary real-firmware reference to adapt for the professor's own PCB (same ESP32-S3 family), rather than starting from the more generic circuitdigest WiFi-drone tutorial or esp-drone (a quadcopter codebase) which was in scope for a separate research thread.
- For onboard perception, reuse the color-blob / IR-reflective detection approach and thresholding method in github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/Blob-detection-and-Tracking and github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/balltraining instead of writing vision code from zero, if the competition needs gate/target tracking.
- Before committing to either task model, get the actual UMNG/LARC rulebook and check whether it's closer to a gate-race (matches mochi-swarm-sim's built-in AutoGateSequenceState/spiral_search behavior) or a capture-and-deliver task (matches the real MochiSwarm paper's MAPD scenario) — then reuse or discard the corresponding src/state/*.py behavior files accordingly.
- Re-derive buoyancy/mass parameters from the professor's own balloon volume and gondola payload mass rather than copying the sim's or paper's numbers verbatim; use the paper's real-hardware numbers (120 g net buoyancy off a 50-inch balloon, 18.7 g frame) only as a sanity-check reference point for expected scale.

### Open questions

- Does the UMNG/LARC 2026 competition rule set define a gate-race task, a capture-and-delivery task, or something else entirely? This determines whether mochi-swarm-sim's built-in AutoGateSequenceState logic or the real MochiSwarm paper's MAPD logic is the better starting template.
- Is mochi-swarm-sim still kept in sync with the current physical Mochi 3.0 firmware/BlimpSwarm codebase, or has it diverged as a stale one-off student project (only 31 commits, no stars/forks, forked from a third party 'vincentzouras')?
- Does the professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCB expose the same peripheral set (2x servo, 2x ESC/brushless driver, IMU e.g. BNO, ultrasonic rangefinder) assumed by BlimpSwarm's Bicopter firmware, or will pin/peripheral mapping need significant rework?
- What exactly is on the Notion assembly-guide page (rhinestone-star-fa9.notion.site/Robot-Assembly-...) — full BOM with vendor part numbers was not extractable via the fetch tool used and needs direct human review.
- Is arXiv 2505.09511's blimp-swarm formation-control project (possibly Georgia Tech, Fumin Zhang) related to or independent of Lehigh's SwarmsLab/Mochi line — worth a dedicated check since it could be another usable reference architecture.

### Could not retrieve

- Verbatim, complete FAQ/troubleshooting Q&A text from https://github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm (docs/index.md) — only paraphrased fragments were recoverable via repeated WebFetch calls and one WebSearch snippet; exact wording unverified.
- Full bill of materials with vendor names/part numbers/links from the Notion 'Robot Assembly' guide (rhinestone-star-fa9.notion.site/Robot-Assembly-f31e5685cc46427f9f4b832dc4a56b95) — page did not yield content through the fetch tool.
- Dimensional/mass parameter values and full dynamics equations from arXiv 2505.09511 (Formation Control System paper) — embedded as images in the PDF, not extractable as text; author-institution affiliation also not confirmed from the PDF metadata.
- docs/figs/, docs/Gemfile, docs/_config.yml contents of LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm (Jekyll site plumbing, low priority, not fetched).
- Full contents of src/vision/ and src/disturbance/ subfolders of mochi-swarm-sim beyond what preferences.py (TURBULENCE class) and simulation.py implied (not individually fetched given time budget).

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — Tongshu Wu (repo owner of mochi-swarm-sim) is a named contributor on the Lehigh SwarmsLab/AIRLab ICRA2025 paper 'MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments' (arXiv 2503.03077), confirming lab affiliation.
  - Correction: Confirmed but with a location nuance: Tongshu Wu is NOT one of the 4 listed paper authors (Jiawei Xu, Thong Vu, Diego S. D'Antonio, David Saldana — all AIRLab, Lehigh University). He appears only in the Acknowledgments sentence naming contributors/developers of the open-source platform. The original wording ('named contributor,' not 'author') was already accurate; do not upgrade this to co-authorship.
- **CONFIRMED** — Real MochiSwarm hardware: 50-inch diameter balloon, 18.7 g laser-cut foam-core frame, 120 g net buoyancy, XIAO ESP32-S3 flight computer running the control loop at 200 Hz, ESP-NOW comms at ~480 m range / 512 kbps, per-unit cost $132.74 + $115 (Nicla Vision).
  - Correction: All figures match the paper text verbatim: 50-inch diameter balloon, 18.7 g frame, 120 grams net buoyancy, XIAO ESP32S3 board, 200 Hz control loop, ESP-NOW peer-to-peer 480 meters range / 512 Kbps, unit cost $132.74 + $115 (NiclaVision).
- **REFUTED** — Nicla Vision perception runs color/IR-blob detection at 10-30 Hz.
  - Correction: The paper states the Nicla Vision 'runs detection at 10 Hz' -- a single figure, not a 10-30 Hz range. No 30 Hz figure appears in the retrieved paper text. Treat 10 Hz as the sourced number; the upper end of the original range looks like an unsupported addition.
- **CONFIRMED** — The real hardware paper's task is Multi-Agent Pickup-and-Delivery (capture-and-deliver), which differs from the sim's built-in sequential gate-race task -- the two must not be conflated when choosing a competition task model.
  - Correction: No correction needed; confirmed as stated.
- **CONFIRMED** — The sim's built-in competition task is a timed race through a sequence of 9 colored hanging balloon gates.
  - Correction: Verified directly in the MuJoCo XML: exactly 9 named gate balloon bodies (balloon_red, orange, yellow, lime, green, cyan, blue, purple, magenta), each radius 0.34 m, mass 0.030 kg, gravity-compensation 1.22, on a 3-link damped tether -- matching the original numeric claims.
- **CONFIRMED** — The sim's bicopter mixer in src/robot/differential.py (servo_angle=atan2(Fz,Fx), thrust=hypot(Fz,Fx) split by yaw torque) 'exactly mirrors' the real BlimpSwarm Arduino firmware's control scheme, making it directly reusable as a control-law reference for the professor's ESP32-S3 firmware.
  - Correction: Verified against the real firmware's own src/robot/Differential.cpp: it independently computes theta=atan2(fz,fx) for servo angle and splits rotor thrust as f1=0.5*(tauz/(l*cos(theta)) + sqrt(fx^2+fz^2)), f2=0.5*(-tauz/(l*cos(theta)) + sqrt(fx^2+fz^2)) -- the same atan2-angle / magnitude-split-by-yaw-torque architecture as the sim. Minor differences: the C++ version uses sqrt(F_mag) rather than hypot() directly and adds smoothing (alpha=0.95) plus a servoBeta/servoRange degree conversion not mentioned in the original description. Core mixer logic is a genuine match, not an overstatement.
- **CONFIRMED** — mochi-swarm-sim (TongshuWu1) is forked from vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim, has 31 commits, 0 stars, 0 forks.
  - Correction: No correction needed; confirmed as stated.
- **CONFIRMED** — Numeric sim/control parameters: motor thrust 0-0.3924 N/side, servo range +/-pi, air density 1.225 kg/m3, viscosity 1.81e-5, main-balloon gravity-compensation 3.2872; preferences.py: FX +/-0.35, FZ [-1.4,1.4], TZ +/-0.08, servo +/-1.45 rad, max thrust 0.72, PID Z (KPZ 0.65/KDZ 0.45/KIZ 0.0, integral clamp [0,0.12]), yaw (KPYAW 1.0/KDYAW 0.02/KIYAW 0.0).
  - Correction: No correction needed; confirmed as stated.
- **CONFIRMED** — github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm is real ESP32-S3 firmware, GPL-3.0, 237 commits; bicopter core parts BOM ~$83.74, +$43 joystick/hub, +$49 sensor suite, full build up to $175.74.
  - Correction: Exact match. Core BOM: 2x brushless motor $20 + 2x propeller $1.25 + 2x ESC $20 + 1x micro servo $3.50 + 1x micro ESP32 $6.50 + 1x balloon $4.00 + battery kit $19.00 + elastic $9.49 = $83.74; +joystick/hub/cable $43.00 -> $126.74; +sensors (ultrasonic $11.50 + BMP390 barometer $12.50 + 9DOF IMU $25.00 = $49.00) -> full build $175.74.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — arXiv 2505.09511 ('Design of a Formation Control System...') is a separate, non-Lehigh blimp-swarm project; author Fumin Zhang is 'historically associated with Georgia Tech.'
  - Correction: Confirmed unrelated to Lehigh (good call keeping it separate), but the Georgia Tech inference is stale for this paper: the actual listed affiliations are Hong Kong University of Science and Technology (Tianfu Wu, Wugang Meng, Fumin Zhang as corresponding author), Beijing Jiaotong University (Jiaqi Fu), Sunchon National University (Sungjin Cho), and Emory University (Huanzhe Zhan). No Georgia Tech or Lehigh affiliation appears anywhere in the paper. Fumin Zhang has historically published Georgia Tech work, but on this specific 2025 paper he is HKUST.
- **CONFIRMED** — mochi-swarm-sim requires Python 3.10+ because main.py uses PEP 604 'str | None' union-type syntax.
  - Correction: No correction needed; confirmed as stated.

### Added by fact-checker

- The real BlimpSwarm firmware has a whole family of ready-made robot classes directly relevant to adapting to the professor's own ESP32-S3 PCB: src/robot/{RawBicopter.cpp/.h, FullBicopter.cpp/.h, CustomBicopter.cpp/.h, Differential.cpp/.h, SBlimp.cpp/.h, RobotFactory.cpp/.h}, plus actuator drivers in src/act/{AServo.cpp, BLMotor.cpp, LED.cpp} and perception glue in src/state/{custom/, nicla/}. Differential.cpp/.h is the exact real-firmware counterpart to the sim's differential.py and should be the professor's primary firmware reference file, not just an inferred analog.
- preferences.py in the sim contains additional control parameters the original research omitted: second-order yaw gains KPPYAW=0.02 and KDDYAW=0.015 (the yaw loop is actually PD with an extra rate term, not a plain PID), a yaw-integral clamp of +/-0.30 (distinct from the Z-integral clamp [0,0.12] that was reported), and a yaw_term output clip of +/-0.26. Also, the sim's simulated camera is configured at HQVGA resolution, 10 FPS, RGB -- which happens to match the real Nicla Vision's actual 10 Hz detection rate, suggesting the sim author deliberately tuned this to mirror real hardware timing.
- Bicopter1raw.ino (the specific example file the original researcher cited as firmware evidence) is explicitly the 'raw joystick passthrough' example -- its own header comment says input from the joystick goes directly to the motors with no autonomous mixing. The atan2/hypot-style mixer only exists in the separate Differential.cpp/RobotFactory-driven robot classes (e.g. FullBicopter, CustomBicopter), not in Bicopter1raw itself. Anyone adapting firmware should target Differential.cpp + FullBicopter/CustomBicopter, not the raw example, as the actual reusable control-law reference.
- Neither the original research nor this check found any UMNG (Bogota) or IARC/LARC (Oakland) 2025-2026 rulebook text, deadlines, or task specification in the material reviewed -- this remains a hard open question and no numeric claim about those competitions' rules should be trusted until the actual rulebook PDFs/pages are opened directly (out of scope for this specific mochi-swarm-sim fact-check pass).

---

## repo-espdrone (source)  
**RELIABILITY: high**

esp-drone (github.com/espressif/esp-drone) is Espressif's open-source WiFi-controlled micro-quadcopter firmware for ESP32/ESP32-S2/ESP32-S3, built by porting Bitcraze's Crazyflie 2.0 flight-stack (bitcraze/crazyflie-firmware, tag 2021.01, commit b448553, GPL-3.0) into ESP-IDF. CONFIRMED via primary sources (repo source, README.md, docs/en/rst/notice.rst, GitHub API cross-check of the crazyflie-firmware tag/commit/license): lineage and GPL-3.0 copyleft are exactly as suspected — any distributed derivative firmware must stay GPL-3.0. Official hardware targets: ESP32-S2/ESP32-S3 (primary, "Limited Support" per docs/en/rst/index.rst) and legacy ESP32 (ESPlane-FC-V1, deprioritized, needs a manual jumper fix). Official board "ESP32_S2_Drone_V1_2" works on both S2 and S3. Full pinout, sensors (MPU6050 IMU + HMC5883L mag + MS5611 baro, hardcoded via platform.h's SENSOR_INCLUDED_MPU6050_HMC5883L_MS5611; PMW3901+VL53L1X optional flow deck), and motor specs (4x tiny 716/720 coreless brushed DC motors, 46-55mm props, 300-350mAh 1S LiPo — i.e. Crazyflie-class, tens-of-grams thrust budget) are all extracted from main/Kconfig.projbuild and docs/en/rst/hardware.rst. Architecture: ESP-IDF release/v4.4 or v5.0 (per README, CI, and official docs), FreeRTOS at 1000Hz tick, a 1kHz (500Hz w/ Kalman) stabilizerTask in components/core/crazyflie/modules/src/stabilizer.c running estimator->commander->controller->powerDistribution in sequence; default estimator = complementary filter (estimator_complementary.c, 131 lines), Kalman/EKF (kalman_core.c 1123 lines + estimator_kalman.c 753 lines) only engaged when a deck (flow deck) requires it; default controller = cascaded rate+angle PID (controller_pid.c 178 lines, attitude_pid_controller.c 211 lines, generic pid.c 160 lines) — Mellinger and INDI controllers are ported but not default/used. The motor mixer (power_distribution_stock.c) HARD-CODES a 4-motor quad-X (or +) thrust/roll/pitch/yaw allocation — this is the one piece that is architecturally guaranteed wrong for any non-quad vehicle. Comms: CRTP protocol over UDP (device port 2390, app port 2399) riding WiFi AP mode (SSID "ESP-DRONE_XXXX"), with an alternate ESP-NOW transport path for joystick control (espnow_ctrl.c); ground-station options are the official ESP-Drone Android/iOS apps (EspressifApps/ESP-Drone-*) or a forked cfclient PC client (qljz1993/crazyflie-clients-python, esplane branch; cflib fork also referenced). BLIMP FITNESS (mixed confirmed-fact + explicit physics inference): the codebase's HAL layer, sensor drivers, CRTP/WiFi comms stack, param/log/console debug system, and ESP-IDF+Kconfig board-config build system are vehicle-agnostic and highly reusable as-is — this is the main value of starting from esp-drone rather than from scratch, since it hands the team a working telemetry/live-tuning/ground-app link for free. What must be replaced or heavily reworked: power_distribution_stock.c (the mixer, ~150 lines, narrowly scoped — NBR_OF_MOTORS is only referenced in ~15 call sites concentrated in motors.c/stabilizer.c/sitaw.c, so retargeting to 2-3 motors is a bounded, moderate task, not a rewrite); the PID attitude-stabilization cascade, which is tuned for a fast, lightly-damped, thrust-dominant rigid body and is very likely the WRONG control architecture entirely for a passively pendulum-stable, heavily damped, high-added-mass blimp (this is my physics inference, not a repo fact) — a blimp with 2-3 motors typically doesn't even need active roll/pitch stabilization (gondola-below-envelope geometry provides passive restoring torque), so most of Crazyflie's "hard" fast-attitude-loop code is arguably not needed at all, and forcing it on could fight the vehicle's natural dynamics; sitaw.c's free-fall/tumble crash-safety heuristics are quad-specific and should be disabled. RECOMMENDATION (inference): reuse esp-drone for HAL/drivers/comms/build-system/param-log framework and the pluggable estimator/controller/mixer interface pattern (add new ControllerType/PowerDistribution variants behind the existing function-pointer tables rather than gutting stabilizer.c), but write the actual blimp control law (probably just altitude-trim + heading-hold + slow position-hold, running the stabilizer loop at a much lower, throttled rate) from scratch. This is very likely a better starting point than a bare ESP-IDF app, given the amount of working comms/telemetry infrastructure it provides "for free," provided the team treats the flight-control layer as a from-scratch job, not a tuning job. Maintenance: 2188 stars/508 forks/34 open issues (github.com/espressif/esp-drone, checked 2026-09-21); last commit to master 2026-06-04 (db0f656); "Support Policy: From December 2022, limited support" (notice.rst); however an active, unmerged in-progress branch/PR by an Espressif employee (leeebo, company:@espressif) — PR #137 "refactor: support ESP-IDF 5.5/6.1 and ESP32-P4/S31" (created 2026-08-10, NOT merged) — proposes IDF 5.5/6.1, ESP32-P4 support, an "ESP32-S31" preview target (this chip name is NOT independently verifiable against any publicly known Espressif part as of my knowledge; treat as unverified/pending), and real safety hardening (disarm-by-default, propagate LEDC/sensor failures instead of silently continuing). No known esp-drone+blimp precedent was found anywhere (web search), confirming this is a novel application, not a repeat of prior art. No significant active community fork exists (top forks all have ≤4 stars).

### Key findings

- **[high]** esp-drone's flight core is a direct port of bitcraze/crazyflie-firmware tag 2021.01, commit b448553, GPL-3.0
  - Stated explicitly in README.md and docs/en/rst/notice.rst 'Third Party Copyrighted Code' table; independently verified the tag and commit exist in bitcraze/crazyflie-firmware via GitHub API (tag 2021.01 -> sha a944ab90..., commit b448553 dated 2021-01-13, message 'sensfusion6: Rename quaternion components for clarity'), and both repos' license field returns gpl-3.0 via API.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/README.md>
- **[high]** Official supported chips are ESP32-S2 and ESP32-S3 (primary), plain ESP32 legacy/deprioritized
  - docs/en/rst/index.rst support table: 'master | release/v4.4, release/v5.0 | ESP32-S2 / ESP32-S3 | Limited Support'. hardware.rst separately lists ESPlane-FC-V1 (plain ESP32) as 'an old version hardware' needing a GPIO14->MPU6050 INT jumper fix and an espefuse.py flash-voltage fix. sdkconfig.defaults.esp32s3 sets 240MHz dual-core; sdkconfig.defaults.esp32s2 sets 240MHz single-core (CONFIG_FREERTOS_UNICORE=y).
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/main/Kconfig.projbuild>
- **[high]** IMU/mag/baro stack is hardcoded to MPU6050 + HMC5883L + MS5611; MPU9250/BMI088 driver code exists but is dead for ESP32 builds
  - components/platform/platform.h unconditionally #defines SENSOR_INCLUDED_MPU6050_HMC5883L_MS5611 (the other sensor combos - BMI088+BMP388, MPU9250+LPS25H, Bosch - are guarded behind #ifdefs that are never set for ESP32 targets, they're leftover from STM32 Crazyflie board variants). hardware/datasheet/ only contains MPU6050, HMC5883L, MS5611, VL53L0/L1, PMW3901 PDFs, confirming what's actually used.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/components/platform/platform.h>
- **[high]** Motor mixer is hardcoded for exactly 4 motors in quad-X/+ configuration
  - components/core/crazyflie/modules/src/power_distribution_stock.c computes motorPower.m1..m4 directly from control->thrust/roll/pitch/yaw with #ifdef QUAD_FORMATION_X branching X vs + layout; config.h unconditionally #defines QUAD_FORMATION_X. NBR_OF_MOTORS=4 is a #define in motors.h referenced in only ~15 places total (motors.c driver, stabilizer.c self-test code, sitaw.c), so retargeting to 2-3 motors is a bounded, well-scoped code change, not a full rewrite.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/components/core/crazyflie/modules/src/power_distribution_stock.c>
- **[high]** Default state estimator is a complementary filter; the EKF (Kalman) only activates when a deck requires it
  - components/core/crazyflie/modules/src/estimator.c: '#define DEFAULT_ESTIMATOR complementaryEstimator'; stabilizerInit() calls deckGetRequiredEstimator() and only switches to Kalman if a connected deck (flow deck) demands it. Default controller is PID: controller.c '#define DEFAULT_CONTROLLER ControllerTypePID' (Mellinger/INDI controllers are compiled in but not selected by default).
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/components/core/crazyflie/modules/src/estimator.c>
- **[high]** Control/telemetry link is CRTP-over-UDP (WiFi AP mode), port 2390 on the drone / 2399 on the app-side, with an ESP-NOW fallback transport
  - docs/en/rst/communication.rst gives the full stack table (App/CRTP/UDP/WiFi-STA <-> Firmware/CRTP/UDP/WiFi-AP) and the exact UDP ports (192.168.43.42::2399 <-> ::2390); wifi_esp32.c literally #defines UDP_SERVER_PORT 2390. wifilink.c's detectEspNow()/espnow_ctrl.c implement the ESP-NOW joystick path referenced in the README ('ESP-BOX3 Joystick Control through esp-now'). Ground apps: EspressifApps/ESP-Drone-iOS, EspressifApps/ESP-Drone-Android (linked in README), plus a forked PC client qljz1993/crazyflie-clients-python (esplane branch).
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/docs/en/rst/communication.rst>
- **[high]** Reference hardware uses tiny brushed coreless DC motors driven directly by ESP32 LEDC PWM (no ESC/BLDC), 15kHz PWM, 8-bit duty resolution
  - components/drivers/general/motors/motors.c configures ledc_timer_config with .freq_hz=15000, MOTORS_PWM_BITS=LEDC_TIMER_8_BIT; motors.h enum motorsDrvType has only BRUSHED/BRUSHLESS with only a BRUSHED map defined (motorMapDefaultBrushed). hardware.rst BOM lists '716 motor x4 (Optional: 720 motor)' and '46mm propeller' - i.e. Crazyflie-class micro motors, tens-of-grams thrust class, not brushless ESC-driven.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/blob/master/components/drivers/general/motors/motors.c>
- **[unverified]** A quadrotor's fast attitude-stabilization PID cascade is very likely the wrong control architecture for a passively-stable blimp gondola, and most of it may not be needed at all
  - PHYSICS INFERENCE, not a repo/rulebook fact: a blimp gondola hung below a buoyant envelope has strong passive pendulum-restoring torque on roll/pitch (unlike a quad, which must actively fight gravity every instant via differential thrust). With 2-3 motors a blimp typically cannot even command full independent roll/pitch/yaw torque the way a quad-X mixer assumes; it usually only actively controls yaw (differential/vectored thrust) and altitude (net vertical thrust trim against near-neutral buoyancy), with roll/pitch left passively stable. Reusing controller_pid.c's cascade as-is (rather than writing a much simpler heading+altitude-hold law) risks fighting the vehicle's natural dynamics. Recommend treating flight-control (not just the mixer) as a from-scratch job while reusing HAL/estimator/comms.
- **[high]** esp-drone shows genuine, current (Aug 2026) maintenance activity from an Espressif engineer, despite the 'limited support since Dec 2022' policy notice
  - Unmerged PR #137 'refactor: support ESP-IDF 5.5/6.1 and ESP32-P4/S31' opened 2026-08-10 by user leeebo (GitHub profile: company '@espressif', 68 public repos, 84 followers - i.e. a real Espressif staffer, and the same person whose cfclient fork the README/docs already cite). PR body describes real safety hardening: 'startup is disarmed by default', 'motor startup rotation stays disabled unless explicitly enabled', 'a missing MPU6050 no longer causes an assert'. Branch is 15 commits ahead of master, not merged as of 2026-09-21. Its 'ESP32-S31' target name could not be independently verified against any publicly documented Espressif chip - flag as unverified/pending, do not treat as a confirmed shipping part.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/pull/137>
- **[high]** Repo stats: 2188 stars, 508 forks, 34 open issues, last master commit 2026-06-04 (db0f656), no git tags/releases ever cut
  - Via GitHub API (api.github.com/repos/espressif/esp-drone). Recent open issues cluster around: WiFi not showing on S3, poshold/Kalman+flowdeck-v2 integration bugs, PMW3901 overriding manual control, general 'motors do not work' reports, requests for new-chip support (C6, P4). Recently fixed: MPU6050 I2C latch-up causing reboot loops (issue #105, closed by PR #115, merged 2025-07-24). No significant independent community fork exists - the largest fork (by stars) has only 4 stars.
  - <https://api.github.com/repos/espressif/esp-drone/issues>
- **[medium]** No prior art combining esp-drone (or Crazyflie-derived firmware in general) with a lighter-than-air/blimp airframe was found
  - Targeted web searches for esp-drone+blimp, and Crazyflie+blimp/airship/helium, returned only generic LTA-drone background material (GlobalSpec, Smithsonian, Wikipedia Hybrid airship, a torus blimp research image) with no hit connecting either project to a balloon/blimp airframe. This is consistent with the professor's project being a genuinely novel combination, but absence-of-evidence from web search is not proof of absence - could_not_retrieve if a build log exists only on a forum/Discord not indexed by search.

### Gotchas

- power_distribution_stock.c's mixer will produce nonsensical motor commands if wired directly to a 2-3 motor blimp gondola without modification - do not flash stock esp-drone onto blimp hardware expecting it to 'just fly conservatively'; it assumes 4 independently-torquing motors.
- config.h unconditionally #defines QUAD_FORMATION_X and STABILIZER_TASK_PRI=7 > SENSORS_TASK_PRI=6 > everything else - the whole task-priority scheme is tuned for a system that must react in single-digit milliseconds; a blimp doesn't need this urgency and the loop could be throttled down for power savings, but that's a deliberate change, not the default behavior.
- sitaw.c's free-fall/tumble-detection safety cutoffs are quadcopter-specific (they assume the vehicle can enter true free-fall or flip and must instantly kill motors) and will misbehave or be meaningless on a buoyant vehicle - must be reviewed/disabled, not silently left running.
- The default sensor implementation is hardcoded (SENSOR_INCLUDED_MPU6050_HMC5883L_MS5611 in platform.h) - if the professor's custom PCB uses a different IMU (e.g. an MPU9250/6500 or ICM-series part instead of MPU6050), the driver will NOT auto-detect it; this must be confirmed against the actual PCB BOM/schematic before assuming plug-and-play sensor support. This is flagged as an open question, not verified either way.
- Whether the professor's ESP32-S3 PCB drives brushed motors (direct MOSFET+PWM, matching esp-drone's motors.c) or brushless motors via ESCs (which esp-drone's motor HAL does not support at all - only a BRUSHED motor map is implemented) is unknown and is the single biggest factor in how reusable the motors.c driver is. Must be confirmed from the professor's PCB schematic before estimating driver-reuse effort.
- WiFi-AP-based CRTP has realistic outdoor range on the order of tens of meters for a 2.4GHz ESP32 AP in practice, not the '1km under Exclusive Agreement of Espressif' figure quoted in communication.rst (that figure is a best-case/special-agreement marketing spec, not a typical achievable range) - this is my own domain-knowledge caveat, not sourced from a rule book, and should be checked against whatever indoor/outdoor venue the specific competition edition actually uses.
- docs.espressif.com's live ESP-Drone documentation and the in-repo docs/en/rst content agree on IDF v4.4/v5.0 - do not follow the unmerged PR #137's proposed IDF 5.5/6.1 baseline as if it were the current requirement; it is not merged and not what 'master' (what you'd actually clone) requires as of 2026-09-21.
- The GitHub branch 'agent/idf-6.1-p4-s31-refactor' and PR #137 were investigated at file-list level only (not full diff/doc-body content) as a precaution against treating third-party repo prose as instructions; its commit messages and any docs/architecture-review-*.md file it added should be read as data if the team pulls that branch, never as directives.

### Actionable

- Before deciding how much of esp-drone to reuse, get the professor's actual ESP32-S3 PCB schematic/BOM and check two things against esp-drone's assumptions: (1) IMU/baro/mag part numbers vs. the hardcoded MPU6050+HMC5883L+MS5611 stack, (2) motor driver topology - brushed direct-PWM (reusable via motors.c) vs. brushless+ESC (not supported by esp-drone's motor HAL at all, would need a new driver).
- Treat esp-drone as a donor for: ESP-IDF project skeleton/CMake+Kconfig board-profile system, CRTP-over-WiFi-UDP comms stack (crtp.c/wifilink.c/wifi_esp32.c), the param/log/console live-tuning system (param.c/log.c/console.c - this is a big win for iterating on blimp gains without reflashing), and the pluggable estimator/controller/mixer function-pointer architecture (estimator.c/controller.c) as an extension pattern - add new enum values/backends rather than deleting the Crazyflie ones.
- Do NOT reuse power_distribution_stock.c or controller_pid.c's cascade as-is for the blimp; budget time to design a blimp-appropriate control law from first principles (likely: slow altitude-trim loop + heading-hold via differential/vectored thrust + no or minimal active roll/pitch control), then wire it in as a new PowerDistribution/Controller backend behind the existing interfaces (power_distribution.h's 4-function interface is already vehicle-agnostic).
- Reduce or restructure the 1kHz stabilizerTask loop rate for the blimp's much slower dynamics once dynamics are characterized - this is both more appropriate control-theoretically and likely saves battery/CPU headroom on the ESP32-S3, extending the endurance benefit that is the whole point of the blimp design.
- Disable or rewrite sitaw.c's free-fall/tumble crash-detection logic before first flight - as written it encodes quadcopter-specific assumptions that could trigger inappropriately (or fail to trigger appropriately) on a buoyant airframe.
- Pull and review (but do not blindly adopt) Espressif's unmerged PR #137 (github.com/espressif/esp-drone/pull/137) for its safety-hardening ideas (disarm-by-default, propagate sensor/PWM failures instead of asserting/resetting) even though its IDF-6.1/ESP32-P4/S31 scope is broader than what this project needs - the safety patterns are worth cherry-picking regardless of which IDF version is ultimately targeted.
- Confirm which cfclient/cflib fork actually works against the currently-cloned master (qljz1993/crazyflie-clients-python, esplane branch, per docs/en/rst/gettingstarted.rst) before assuming the stock crazyflie-lib-python/cfclient talks to esp-drone out of the box - it does not, per the docs' explicit branch note.

### Open questions

- What IMU/baro/mag parts and motor-driver topology (brushed direct-PWM vs brushless+ESC) does the professor's actual ESP32-S3 PCB use? This single fact drives most of the HAL-reuse estimate and was not available to this research (no schematic was provided/found).
- Which specific edition/year of the UMNG Mecatronica competition and the IAR/LARC (Oakland) competition will be entered, and do their rules specify an indoor vs outdoor venue, a maximum flight arena size, or restrictions on RF links (relevant to the CRTP-over-WiFi range gotcha)? Out of scope for this esp-drone-focused pass - flagged for whichever agent covers the competition rulebooks.
- Does the professor's blimp design use 2 or 3 motors, and are any of them vectored/tiltable? This determines the exact shape of the replacement mixer.
- Is there an existing, unindexed (forum/Discord/WeChat) build log of someone combining Crazyflie-lineage firmware with a blimp/balloon airframe that a plain web search would miss?

### Could not retrieve

- Could not fetch the ESP32 forum thread (esp32.com/viewtopic.php?t=41383) about ESP-Drone BLDC/motor discussion - it returned a bot-challenge/verification page to WebFetch rather than content.
- Did not attempt to read the full body of docs/architecture-review-2026-08-10.md or the other doc files added on the unmerged 'agent/idf-6.1-p4-s31-refactor' branch/PR #137, as a deliberate precaution against ingesting untrusted third-party document prose as if it were verified fact or instruction; only file-list-level metadata from that branch was examined.
- Could not independently confirm or deny whether 'ESP32-S31', named as a preview target in PR #137, corresponds to a real, publicly documented Espressif chip; this is after my knowledge cutoff (Jan 2026) territory and the PR is unmerged/not a public product announcement.
- Did not deeply verify the iOS/Android ESP-Drone app repos (EspressifApps/ESP-Drone-iOS, EspressifApps/ESP-Drone-Android) beyond confirming their URLs are linked from the README - their own maintenance status/last-commit dates were not checked.
- Espressif's official esp-drone documentation site content was only sampled via a single WebFetch summarization call (docs.espressif.com/projects/espressif-esp-drone/en/latest/), not cross-read page by page, though its key facts (IDF v4.4/v5.0, ESP32-S2/S3 target, Dec-2022 support-policy note) matched the in-repo docs/en/rst sources exactly.

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — esp-drone's flight core is a direct port of bitcraze/crazyflie-firmware tag 2021.01, commit b448553, GPL-3.0
- **CONFIRMED** — Official supported chips are ESP32-S2 and ESP32-S3 ('Limited Support'), IDF release/v4.4 or v5.0, plain ESP32 legacy/deprioritized; 'From December 2022, limited support' policy
- **CONFIRMED** — IMU/mag/baro stack is hardcoded to MPU6050+HMC5883L+MS5611 (other sensor combos are dead #ifdef branches)
- **CONFIRMED** — Motor mixer is hardcoded for exactly 4 motors in quad-X/+ configuration (power_distribution_stock.c)
- **CONFIRMED** — Default state estimator is complementary filter; Kalman/EKF only when a deck requires it; default controller is PID (Mellinger/INDI compiled but not default)
- **CONFIRMED** — CRTP-over-UDP link, device port 2390 / app port 2399, WiFi AP mode; alternate ESP-NOW path exists
- **CONFIRMED** — Reference motors are brushed only (no brushless/ESC support in the motor HAL), driven via ESP32 LEDC PWM at 15 kHz, 8-bit duty resolution
- **CONFIRMED** — BOM: 716 motors x4 (optional 720), 46mm props (optional 55mm), 300mAh 1S LiPo (optional 350mAh)
- **CONFIRMED** — Repo stats: 2188 stars, 508 forks, 34 open issues, GPL-3.0, last master commit db0f656 dated 2026-06-04, no tags/releases
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — PR #137 ('refactor: support ESP-IDF 5.5/6.1 and ESP32-P4/S31') is a real, unmerged, 15-commit PR opened 2026-08-10 by Espressif employee leeebo, proposing an 'ESP32-S31' preview target that is NOT independently verifiable as a real chip
  - Correction: Everything about the PR itself is confirmed (open, 15 commits, opened 2026-08-10 by user leeebo whose GitHub profile lists company '@espressif', 68 public repos, 84 followers). BUT the sub-claim that ESP32-S31 'cannot be independently verified against any publicly known Espressif part' is REFUTED: as of 2026-09-21, Espressif's own site (espressif.com/en/products/socs and a dedicated espressif.com/en/products/socs/esp32-s31 page) lists ESP32-S31 as a real, shipping-adjacent SoC — dual-core RISC-V @ 320MHz, Wi-Fi 6/802.11ax, BLE, Thread, Zigbee — with a datasheet and purchase links.
- **CONFIRMED** — sitaw.c implements quadcopter-specific free-fall/at-rest/tumble crash-safety heuristics that would misbehave on a buoyant airframe
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — communication.rst states WiFi 'Supported Distance: 1 km under the Exclusive Agreement of Espressif'; real-world outdoor range is far shorter
  - Correction: The '1 km under the Exclusive Agreement of Espressif' text is an exact, verbatim quote from the doc's Wi-Fi Performance spec table (both ESP32 and ESP32-S2 rows) — the researcher did not fabricate it. The claim that this is a best-case/special-agreement marketing figure and not typical achievable range is correctly labeled by the original researcher as their own domain-knowledge caveat, not a rulebook fact — that framing is appropriate and I found nothing in the docs contradicting it (no typical-range figure is given anywhere else in the doc).

### Added by fact-checker

- ESP32-S31 IS a real, publicly listed Espressif SoC as of 2026-09-21 — dedicated product page at espressif.com/en/products/socs/esp32-s31, titled 'ESP32-S31 Dual-Core RISC-V + Multi-Protocol SoC', dual-core RISC-V @ 320 MHz, Wi-Fi 6 (802.11ax), Bluetooth, Thread and Zigbee, with datasheet and 'Buy' links live on Espressif's official SoC catalog (espressif.com/en/products/socs). This means PR #137's ESP32-S31 preview-target claim was accurate technical content, not something to distrust — the original researcher's caution here was reasonable given their own knowledge cutoff but is now resolved: it's real, not a placeholder/typo.
- GPL-3.0 copyleft nuance the original researcher didn't spell out for the competition context: GPLv3's obligation to provide complete corresponding source is triggered by 'conveying' the software, not by mere private/internal use. Handing the finished drone/blimp over to competition judges (a 'User Product' under GPLv3, if the software is embodied in a consumer-type device) very plausibly counts as conveying, and would obligate the team to make available the complete corresponding source of ANY modifications they made to the esp-drone/Crazyflie-derived code, under GPL-3.0, including install/build instructions — the team cannot treat their blimp-specific controller code as proprietary if it's a derivative work built on top of esp-drone's GPL-3.0 core and the device is given to or inspected by judges. Many academic robotics competitions also independently require source submission, which would trigger this regardless of GPL mechanics. Worth a 5-minute conversation with the professor before assuming any part of the codebase can stay closed.
- The GitHub code-search API (needs auth/token to be reliable) returned no usable result for counting NBR_OF_MOTORS call sites, so the original researcher's '~15 call sites' figure could not be independently confirmed or refuted here — treat it as an unverified estimate, not a checked number, until someone greps the cloned repo directly.
- Live repo check (2026-09-21) shows repo 'pushed_at' = 2026-08-10 (a push to the open, unmerged PR #137 branch) vs. 'last commit to master' = 2026-06-04 (db0f656) — the two dates the original researcher cited are both correct but describe different things (any-branch push vs. master HEAD); worth not conflating them when judging how 'alive' master itself is.

## article-circuitdigest (source)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

Digested circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone: an ESP32 WROOM WiFi quadcopter tutorial, Intermediate difficulty, 6-8h build, ~$30-50/Rs1093.50 BOM, older design (not S3). MCU: ESP32 WROOM. IMU: MPU6050 (I2C, GPIO21/22). USB-UART: CP2102N. Power: MIC5219-3.3YM5 LDO (500mA/500mV dropout), TP4056 charger (1A max), P-MOSFET power path (AO3401) + SS34 diode, 2N7002DW auto-reset circuit, battery voltage divider. Motors: 4x "720" coreless DC motors, 55mm props (2 CW/2 CCW), each switched by a single SI2302 N-MOSFET (+flyback diode) driven straight off GPIO32/26/25/27, no dedicated ESC/driver IC. Battery: 1S 1300mAh 30C LiPo, ~60g AUW, 5-7min flight (7-10min on 1500-2000mAh); WiFi range 30-50m open / 15-25m indoor. Firmware: ESP-IDF 4.4.5 (C), explicitly a modified fork of Espressif's esp-drone (Crazyflie-derived) - same lineage as one of the two repos the professor already sent. Control: WiFi SoftAP + ESP-Drone APP, password 12345678, CRTP-style stick control. Status LEDs: blue=calibrating, green=WiFi connected, red=low battery. Documented failure mode: cheap/clone MPU6050 boards fail I2C at 400kHz.

Critique for the blimp: the power stage, status-LED UX pattern, and WiFi/CRTP control-link concept transfer directly. The motor-drive stage (one low-side N-MOSFET per motor) is electrically fine but unidirectional - wrong for blimp thrust/steering motors that likely want reverse or differential drive, unless paired with vectoring servos. Airframe-specific parts are wrong to copy wholesale: the X-quad 4-lift-rotor mixing, thrust/prop sizing tuned to overcome gravity at ~60g AUW, the rigid vibration-tolerant frame, and - the biggest risk - the full attitude-stabilization PID loop, which assumes zero passive stability. A blimp's buoyancy-above-CG geometry gives passive pendulum restoring torque that a naively-copied roll/pitch PID could fight, burning the power budget the balloon exists to save (flagged as an engineering inference, unverified against esp-drone's actual PID/mixer source). Battery-selection criterion should flip from high-C-rate/peak-power to high energy-density/low continuous draw.

Found closer ESP32/ESP32-S3 blimp precedents beyond the article: CircuitDigest's own ESP32-S3 LiteWing successor (same MCU family as the professor's PCB, CRTP-over-UDP, optional ToF/optical-flow); FedericoBusero/ESP32-CAM_ZeppelinCAM, an actual ESP32-CAM/XIAO-ESP32S3 blimp with 2 coreless motors (vertical+forward) + DRV8833 H-bridge/servo steering + browser slider/joystick UI - a materially better control-scheme analog than the quadcopter; and Blimpduino 2 (ARM Cortex-M0 + ESP8266, 4 motor + 2 servo outputs, 9-axis IMU + altimeter + forward object-detection sensor, mylar envelope, ~32g gondola payload target), the most complete open RC-blimp precedent found, though its primary spec source (jjrobots.com) failed to load and the full Instructables build returned no body content in this pass - both flagged for re-verification.

### Specs

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| CircuitDigest ESP32 drone motors | 4x 720 coreless DC motors, 55mm props (2x CW type A, 2x CCW type B) |
| CircuitDigest ESP32 drone motor switch MOSFET | SI2302 N-channel, SOT-23, 20V/3A, Vgs(th) approx 0.85V, RDS(on)=55mOhm at Vgs=3.3V, driven directly from GPIO32/26/25/27 |
| CircuitDigest ESP32 drone battery and endurance | 1S 1300mAh 30C LiPo; approx 60g AUW; 5-7min flight (1300mAh) to 7-10min (1500-2000mAh); WiFi range 30-50m open air / 15-25m indoor |
| CircuitDigest ESP32 drone cost/BOM | Total approx Rs1093.50 parts (ESP32 Rs240, CP2102 Rs200, 720 motors+55mm props Rs266, MPU6050 Rs140, PCB Rs100, TP4056 Rs17.50, MOSFETs Rs40, misc passives Rs100); article states $30-50 all-in, 6-8h build |
| LiteWing (ESP32-S3 successor) specs | ESP32-S3 dual-core LX7 @240MHz/512KB SRAM; IRLML6344 motor MOSFETs; SPX3819 500mA LDO; TP4056 charger; approx 45g without battery, approx 25g payload capacity; optional VL53L1X ToF + PMW3901 optical flow; CRTP-over-UDP; supports ESP-Drone app, cfclient/cflib, Arduino IDE, Betaflight |
| ESP32-CAM_ZeppelinCAM motor/prop specs | 4x8mm coreless DC motor with 30mm tail-rotor-style props; DRV8833 H-bridge for steering; SoftAP control at 192.168.4.1, password 12345678; no reverse/backward motion capability by design |
| Blimpduino 2 electronics/envelope | 4-layer PCB, ARM Cortex-M0 + ESP8266; 4 motor outputs + 2 servo outputs; 9-axis IMU + altimeter + forward object-detection sensor; 3 props (2 thrust + 1 altitude); mylar envelope, stock 52in (36in reported sufficient at Denver altitude per forum report); gondola payload target approx 32g |

### Key findings

- **[high]** CircuitDigest has an ESP32-S3-based successor project, LiteWing, architecturally closer to the professor's ESP32-S3 PCB than the original article
  - LiteWing uses ESP32-S3 (dual-core LX7, 240MHz, 512KB SRAM), IRLML6344 motor MOSFETs, SPX3819 LDO, TP4056 charger, ~45g without battery / ~25g payload capacity, optional VL53L1X ToF and PMW3901 optical flow (MS5611 barometer listed coming soon), CRTP-over-UDP protocol, supports ESP-Drone app, cfclient/cflib, Arduino IDE, and Betaflight.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/wiki/litewing/>
- **[high]** Exact BOM and specs of the original article's quadcopter
  - MCU ESP32 WROOM; IMU MPU6050; USB-UART CP2102N; LDO MIC5219-3.3YM5 (500mA, 500mV dropout); charger TP4056 (1A max); power path P-MOSFET (likely AO3401) + SS34 diode; 4x 720 coreless DC motors, 55mm props (2 CW/2 CCW); SI2302 N-MOSFETs x4 + flyback diodes on GPIO32/26/25/27; battery 1S 1300mAh 30C LiPo; weight ~60g; flight time 5-7min (1300mAh) to 7-10min (1500-2000mAh); WiFi range 30-50m open / 15-25m indoor; firmware ESP-IDF 4.4.5 in C, a modified fork of espressif/esp-drone; control app ESP-Drone APP, hotspot password 12345678; cost total Rs1093.50; build time 6-8h.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone>
- **[high]** SI2302 MOSFET gate drive at 3.3V GPIO is electrically adequate, not itself a weak-engineering point, but it is unidirectional
  - SI2302 Vgs(th) approx 0.85V typical, RDS(on)=55mOhm at Vgs=3.3V, rated 3A/20V - fine for small coreless-motor currents. It is a low-side unidirectional switch: fine for a quad's fixed-direction lift rotors, but cannot give reverse thrust, which a blimp propulsion/steering motor likely wants.
  - <https://www.mouser.com/datasheet/2/258/SI2302(SOT-23)-347713.pdf>
- **[high]** FedericoBusero/ESP32-CAM_ZeppelinCAM is a directly relevant open-source ESP32/ESP32-S3 blimp project
  - Uses AI-Thinker ESP32-CAM or XIAO ESP32S3 Sense; 1 vertical-thrust motor + 1 forward motor (8mm coreless DC, 30mm tail-rotor-style props), steering via DRV8833 H-bridge or servo; web UI at 192.168.4.1 with one slider (vertical thrust) + one joystick (steer/forward); SoftAP BlimpCam-[MAC] password 12345678; Arduino IDE 2.0.8+, libraries AsyncTCP 1.1.4, ArduinoWebsockets 0.5.3, ESPAsyncWebSrv 1.2.6; camera defaults OFF to save battery; explicitly cannot move backward.
  - <https://github.com/FedericoBusero/ESP32-CAM_ZeppelinCAM>
- **[medium]** Blimpduino 2 is the most complete documented open-source RC-blimp precedent found
  - 4-layer PCB, ARM Cortex-M0 + ESP8266 WiFi, 4 motor outputs + 2 servo outputs, 9-axis IMU, altimeter, forward object-detection sensor, onboard battery charging; 3 propellers (2 thrust + 1 altitude); mylar envelope with helium, stock kit envelope 52in (one forum user reported 36in sufficient at Denver altitude); gondola payload target approx 32g; smartphone app control, optional 2-phone co-pilot mode; flight endurance reportedly up to 40min on a 500mAh battery per Hackaday's writeup. jjrobots.com's own product/spec page could not be fetched in this pass (DNS failure), so treat exact numeric specs as forum/blog-sourced pending re-verification.
  - <https://hackaday.com/2018/11/27/blimpduino-hits-version-2/>
- **[unverified]** A quadcopter's PID attitude-stabilization approach may be counterproductive if copied verbatim onto a passively-stable blimp airframe
  - Engineering inference, not sourced from a rulebook or datasheet: a blimp's center of buoyancy sits above its center of gravity, giving passive pendulum-like roll/pitch restoring torque absent in a quadcopter, which relies entirely on active differential-thrust PID for attitude. Reusing the ESP-Drone/Crazyflie roll-pitch PID loop unmodified risks the controller fighting the airframe's own passive stability and burning the power budget the balloon was meant to save. Recommend checking this against espressif/esp-drone's actual PID/mixer source before deciding what to strip out.
- **[high]** The CircuitDigest quadcopter's firmware is explicitly a fork of espressif/esp-drone, one of the two repos the professor already sent
  - The article states the firmware base is Espressif ESP-drone firmware (modified), meaning this tutorial's firmware section is largely a derivative of github.com/espressif/esp-drone; the professor's two reference repos and this tutorial share lineage.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone>

### Gotchas

- Instructables' Blimpduino build page and jjrobots.com's own product/spec page could not be fetched with full content in this pass (Instructables returned only the page title with no body text; jjrobots.com failed outright with a DNS resolution error). Figures attributed to those sources here come from secondary aggregation and should be re-verified from jjrobots.com or an archive.org snapshot before being treated as authoritative.
- GPIO pin assignments in the article (GPIO32/26/25/27 for motors, GPIO21/22 for I2C) are for the classic ESP32 WROOM, not ESP32-S3. ESP32-S3 has a different pin map and different strapping pins (GPIO0/3/45/46) - these must NOT be copied onto the professor's already-fabricated ESP32-S3 PCB without checking its actual schematic.
- ESP32-S3 has native USB (OTG/CDC), unlike the classic ESP32 in the article which needs a CP2102N USB-UART bridge chip. If the professor's PCB already uses S3 native USB, the CP2102N-equivalent bridge is unnecessary BOM/cost/board-space.
- SI2302-style single low-side MOSFET motor drive only supports one spin direction per motor; a blimp wanting reverse thrust or differential steering needs an H-bridge (e.g. DRV8833, as used in ZeppelinCAM) or servo-vectored fixed-direction motors (as in Blimpduino 2), not a straight copy of the quadcopter's motor-drive stage.
- The quadcopter's 1S 30C high-discharge-rate battery requirement is driven by peak motor current, a non-issue for a blimp; optimizing for high C-rate would be optimizing for the wrong variable versus energy density (Wh/g) for endurance.

### Actionable

- Independently re-fetch jjrobots.com/blimpduino-2/ (e.g. via web.archive.org) and the full 7-step Instructables Blimpduino build, since both failed to return full content in this pass but represent the best-documented open blimp precedent found.
- Pull the professor's actual ESP32-S3 PCB schematic and cross-check its GPIO assignments against this article's pin map before reusing any wiring/pinout guidance.
- Treat CircuitDigest's LiteWing (ESP32-S3) as the primary CircuitDigest reference for BOM/firmware parallels, not the older ESP32-WROOM article, since it matches the professor's MCU family.
- Study FedericoBusero/ESP32-CAM_ZeppelinCAM's control scheme (1 vertical motor + 1 forward motor + DRV8833/servo steering, browser slider+joystick) as the closest working reference for blimp motor mixing and app UI, rather than the quadcopter's 4-rotor X mixing.
- Before reusing any esp-drone/Crazyflie attitude-PID code, read the actual PID/mixer source in github.com/espressif/esp-drone and decide what must be disabled or retuned for a passively-stable, low-thrust blimp airframe.
- When selecting the blimp's battery, size for capacity-per-gram (energy density/endurance) rather than C-rate.

### Open questions

- Is CRTP-over-UDP / the ESP-Drone app or cfclient/cflib ecosystem an acceptable control-app base for a blimp, or does the flight-mode mapping need a custom app/UI more like ZeppelinCAM's browser slider+joystick?
- What exact motor/servo part numbers and thrust figures does Blimpduino 2 use? Not found in any source reachable in this pass.
- What is the professor's actual ESP32-S3 PCB pinout/schematic? Needed before any pin-level guidance from this article or LiteWing can be applied.
- Does the professor's PCB already exploit ESP32-S3 native USB (eliminating a CP2102N-equivalent bridge chip), or was that chip carried over from an ESP32 non-S3 reference design?

- Full body content of https://www.instructables.com/Blimpduino-Open-Source-Blimp/ (fetch returned only the page title, no step-by-step content)
- https://jjrobots.com/blimpduino-2/ (DNS resolution failure: getaddrinfo EAI_AGAIN jjrobots.com)
