### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — Original CircuitDigest ESP32-WROOM drone article: full BOM/specs (MCU ESP32 WROOM, IMU MPU6050, USB-UART CP2102N, LDO MIC5219-3.3YM5, charger TP4056, 4x '720' coreless motors w/ 55mm props 2CW/2CCW, MOSFETs SI2302 x4 + AO3401 + 2N7002DW, battery 1S 1300mAh 30C, ~60g AUW, 5-7min flight, WiFi 30-50m open/15-25m indoor, GPIO32/26/25/27 motors, GPIO21/22 I2C, status LEDs blue/green/red, ESP-IDF 4.4.5, Intermediate difficulty, 6-8h build, MPU6050 clone I2C-400kHz failure mode)
  - Correction: Independently re-fetched the primary source; every one of these figures matches the article text verbatim (build time '6-8 hours', cost '$30-50', difficulty 'Intermediate', firmware statement 'based on the ESP-drone firmware from Espressif', and the MPU6050-clone-fails-at-400kHz comment).
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Total BOM cost is consistently ~Rs 1093.50 / '$30-50'
  - Correction: These two figures in the SAME article don't reconcile: Rs 1093.50 converts to roughly $13 at any 2025-2026 INR/USD rate (~83-86), not $30-50 - a ~2.5-3.8x gap. The original researcher reported both numbers as if consistent without flagging the mismatch. For competition budgeting, use the itemized Indian-retail parts list (~Rs1093.50, i.e. ~$13 in India) as the floor, and treat '$30-50' as a rough non-India/US-retail estimate that likely folds in battery, connectors, shipping and tooling not in the itemized list - do not average the two.
- **CONFIRMED** — The CircuitDigest quadcopter firmware is explicitly a fork of espressif/esp-drone, sharing lineage with one of the professor's two reference repos
  - Correction: Confirmed verbatim ('The firmware from our DIY drone is based on the ESP-drone firmware from Espressif'). IMPORTANT ADDITION the original researcher missed: espressif/esp-drone's own repo explicitly lists supported MCUs as 'ESP32, ESP32S2, ESP32S3' and states its flight-control core is 'ported from Crazyflie open source project' (GPL-3.0, based on Crazyflie 2021.01). This means the professor's ESP32-S3 PCB is an OFFICIALLY SUPPORTED esp-drone target out of the box - the team should build/flash esp-drone's own ESP32-S3 target and use ITS pin map, rather than reverse-engineering pin assignments from the classic-ESP32 CircuitDigest article.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — LiteWing (CircuitDigest's ESP32-S3 successor) specs: ESP32-S3 dual-core LX7 @240MHz/512KB SRAM, IRLML6344 motor MOSFETs, SPX3819 LDO, TP4056 charger, ~45g without battery/~25g payload, VL53L1X ToF, PMW3901 optical flow, MS5611 barometer 'coming soon', CRTP-over-UDP, supports ESP-Drone app/cfclient/cflib/Arduino IDE/Betaflight
  - Correction: All hardware figures confirmed verbatim via direct re-fetch (ESP32-S3 LX7 @240MHz/512KB SRAM; IRLML6344; SPX3819 3.3V/500mA; TP4056; ~45g w/o battery; ~25g payload w/ 55mm props; VL53L1X; PMW3901MB; CRTP over UDP; cfclient/CFlib/Arduino IDE/Betaflight all confirmed). ONE CORRECTION: the live page currently lists the MS5611 barometer as an ACTIVE supported sensor for altitude-hold, not 'coming soon' as the original research stated - either the page was updated since, or that detail was misread. Treat MS5611 as already supported.
- **REFUTED** — If the professor's ESP32-S3 PCB already uses S3 native USB, a CP2102N-equivalent USB-UART bridge chip is unnecessary BOM/cost/board-space
  - Correction: CircuitDigest's OWN ESP32-S3 board (LiteWing) contradicts this: despite ESP32-S3 having native USB, the design 'deliberately includes' an external USB-to-UART bridge (CH340K) 'for firmware flashing purposes,' per the vendor's own text: 'Although the ESP32-S3 has native USB support, the design includes an external USB to UART bridge for firmware flashing.' If the professor's board already has the bridge chip populated, do not assume it is dead weight without first checking why CircuitDigest itself kept theirs (likely flashing/boot-mode reliability or serial-monitor compatibility, not confirmed from source).
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — SI2302 MOSFET datasheet numbers: Vgs(th) approx 0.85V typical, RDS(on)=55mOhm at Vgs=3.3V, rated 3A/20V
  - Correction: Could not independently confirm these exact figures against a primary datasheet in this pass: the cited Mouser PDF timed out, the Vishay direct PDF 404'd, alldatasheet.com returned 403 Forbidden, and datasheetspdf.com failed outright. This is exactly the kind of numeric claim the task flags as needing independent verification, and per 'default toward unverifiable,' it should be re-checked from a primary datasheet (Vishay/Siliconix or Sinopower, depending on which SI2302 the professor actually has, since 'SI2302' is manufactured by multiple vendors with SOT-23 pinout variants) before sizing gate-drive margin or continuous-current headroom for the blimp's motor switches.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — FedericoBusero/ESP32-CAM_ZeppelinCAM: AI-Thinker ESP32-CAM/XIAO ESP32S3 Sense, 2 coreless motors (vertical+forward) + DRV8833 H-bridge/servo steering, browser slider+joystick, SoftAP 'BlimpCam-[MAC]' pw 12345678, library versions, cannot move backward
  - Correction: Boards, libraries (AsyncTCP 1.1.4, ArduinoWebsockets 0.5.3, ESPAsyncWebSrv 1.2.6), Arduino IDE 2.0.8+, SoftAP naming/password, DRV8833 use, and 'cannot move backward' all confirmed verbatim. CORRECTION on motor count: the repo describes THREE motor roles, not two - '1 motor to go up (1-directional)', '1 motor to go forward (1-directional)', PLUS a separate steering motor via H-bridge (2-directional) and/or servo. The original summary's '2 coreless motors (vertical+forward) + DRV8833/servo steering' undercounts the actuator total for the H-bridge variant (effectively 3 motors, or 2 motors + 1 servo in the servo variant).
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Blimpduino 2: ARM Cortex-M0+ESP8266, 4 motor+2 servo outputs, 9-axis IMU+altimeter+forward object-detection, mylar envelope 52in stock (36in reported sufficient at Denver altitude), ~32g gondola payload target, up to 40min flight on 500mAh
  - Correction: Re-fetched Hackaday independently and confirmed: ARM M0 + ESP8266, '4 motor outputs, 2 servo motor outputs', '9-axis inertial navigation', altimeter/pressure-transducer, forward object detection, 4-layer PCB, 3 props (2 thrust+1 altitude) - all match. But the '40 minutes ... with conservative piloting' figure is explicitly sourced 'from comments' on the article, i.e. a reader/builder anecdote, NOT a manufacturer spec or the article's own measured figure - treat it as a best-case anecdotal ceiling, not a design target. The specific '52in stock envelope' and '~32g gondola payload' numbers could NOT be re-confirmed from any source reachable in this pass: jjrobots.com failed again with the same DNS resolution error (getaddrinfo EAI_AGAIN jjrobots.com) independently reproduced in this session, and instructables.com/Blimpduino-Open-Source-Blimp/ again returned only the page title with no body content on two separate fetch attempts. Both remain genuinely unretrievable via automated fetch, not a one-off failure - the original researcher's flag was correct. These two numbers should be treated as unverified until someone opens the pages in a real browser or finds an archive.org snapshot manually.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — ESP32-S3 has different strapping pins (GPIO0/3/45/46) from classic ESP32, so the article's GPIO32/26/25/27 (motors) and GPIO21/22 (I2C) pin map must not be copied onto the professor's S3 PCB
  - Correction: The directional conclusion (do not reuse the classic-ESP32 pin map on an S3 board without checking the actual schematic) is sound engineering practice and independently supported by esp-drone officially targeting ESP32-S3 with its own S3-specific pin configuration (see esp-drone repo). However, I could not re-confirm the exact strapping-pin list (GPIO0/3/45/46) against Espressif's primary datasheet in this pass: the official PDF fetched but its text layer would not extract (binary/compressed stream, and no PDF-rendering tool available locally to read it page-by-page), and the HTML strapping-pins doc page 404'd. Treat the exact pin numbers as needing a direct check against Espressif's ESP32-S3 Technical Reference Manual or datasheet before finalizing the professor's schematic review, even though the general claim direction is very likely correct.

### Added by fact-checker

- espressif/esp-drone officially supports ESP32, ESP32-S2 AND ESP32-S3 as build targets (per the repo's own text), and its core flight-control code is directly ported from the Crazyflie open-source project under GPL-3.0 (originally based on Crazyflie's 2021.01 release). This means the professor's already-fabricated ESP32-S3 PCB can very likely run esp-drone's own official S3 target/pin map directly, rather than needing pin-level reverse-engineering from the classic-ESP32 CircuitDigest article - this should be the team's first thing to check before any custom firmware work. Source: https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone
- CircuitDigest's own ESP32-S3 successor board (LiteWing) deliberately keeps an external USB-UART bridge chip (CH340K) for flashing even though ESP32-S3 has native USB - this is a concrete precedent the team should weigh before assuming their PCB's UART bridge (if present) is removable/redundant. Source: https://circuitdigest.com/wiki/litewing/
- There is an internal cost inconsistency in the CircuitDigest article itself (itemized BOM ~Rs1093.50 ~= $13 vs. headline '$30-50'), which the original research repeated without flagging - worth resolving before using either number for a competition budget. Source: https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone
- LiteWing's GitHub repo (github.com/Circuit-Digest/LiteWing) exists and is reachable, with hardware schematics at hardware/LiteWingV2.5C/DroneV2.5C_Schematics.pdf - useful as a concrete ESP32-S3 drone PCB reference layout for the professor's own board review, in addition to the article/wiki page.
- jjrobots.com is unreachable via automated fetch tools in this environment specifically due to DNS resolution failure (getaddrinfo EAI_AGAIN), reproduced independently in this pass - this appears to be a persistent/systemic issue with this fetch tool and that domain, not a transient one-off. Anyone continuing this research should try a real browser or a different network path, not just retry the same automated fetch.

## aerostatics (domain)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

Domain: blimp aerostatics, envelope design, weight budget for an indoor competition blimp entered in IEEE LARC 'IAR' (International Airship Racing), hosted by UMNG Bogota Nov 2-6 2026 (confirmed live category on https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/larc.html; rules PDF 'IAR_LARC2026.pdf' is embedded in a Google Drive viewer on https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules that WebFetch could not read through - get it via a real browser/Google account, or email organizers dantonio@oakland.edu / saldana@lehigh.edu). PHYSICS CORE: Sea-level net lift per liter of He = rho_air - rho_He = 1.225 - 0.169 kg/m3 = 1.056 g/L (rho_He at 15C from NIST 0.1786 g/L at 0C scaled). At Bogota (2640 m, Wikipedia elevation) using ideal-gas law with P=74.2 kPa (0.73 atm, mid-point of an ISA-model estimate of 73.4 kPa and a real barometric reading of ~76.7 kPa) and T=287 K (14C, Bogota's actual average - warmer than the ISA lapse-rate value of -2C because Bogota is equatorial): rho_air=0.901 kg/m3, rho_He=0.125 kg/m3, net lift = 0.776 g/L. That is a 26.5% cut in gross lift-per-liter vs sea level (0.776/1.056=0.735) purely from altitude - this ratio is a physics derivation from cited inputs (altitude, P, T), not a sourced 'Bogota blimp' figure, and is flagged as such. Because envelope film mass is fixed regardless of altitude, the NET PAYLOAD loss is worse than 26.5%: worked example on a real 0.70 m3 envelope (from a published indoor-blimp paper, PMC10611505) with a 42 g/m2 film (derived from a real DIY envelope, bobbear.org): sea-level net payload after envelope mass = 296 g; Bogota net payload = 189 g - a 36% cut, not 26.5%, because the fixed envelope-mass subtraction eats a bigger slice of a smaller gross-lift pool. Crossover diameter (this film, at which envelope only just lifts its own material) is ~24 cm sphere at sea level vs ~32-33 cm at Bogota - below that, no film choice of that weight can produce positive net lift at all. Envelope material data: real DIY nylon/EVOH/PE metallized envelope (bobbear.org) = 241 g over a computed 5.69 m2 prolate-spheroid surface = ~42 g/m2; a commercial Qualatex 'Bubble' 61cm foil balloon nets only 40 g of payload lift (newdexterity/hackaday.io), implying ~72-85 g/m2 all-in (film+valve+tape). Bare BoPET substrate alone runs 17-50 g/m2 for 12-36 micron film (PET density 1.4 g/cm3 x thickness). Helium: purity grades corroborated across 3 supplier sites - CGA 'balloon grade'/Grade H is only 97.5-99.99% min, 'industrial grade' Grade 4.5 = 99.995% (actually purer), Grade 5.0 = 99.999% used for weather balloons/blimps/lab work. Diffusion: lab-measured aluminized-Mylar permeation (3.6+/-1)e-6 cc/(s.Torr.m2) (arXiv hep-ex/0409066, TWIST spectrometer paper); latex loses helium at 1-2%/hr (lasts 8-12h indoors) vs foil/Mylar lasting days (multiple balloon-industry sources, medium confidence). Colombia sourcing: Sempertex (Colombian manufacturer, Barranquilla) sells 99.995%-purity helium cylinders for balloons (distribuidores.sempertex.com, product page 404'd on fetch but title/existence confirmed via search) - a strong local lead the team should call directly. US reference pricing for scale: industrial Grade-A helium ~$12/m3 wholesale in 2025 (USGS Mineral Commodity Summary 2026, primary source); balloon-grade retail ~$0.70/cu ft small cylinders; an 80 cu ft (2265 L) steel cylinder system (tank+regulator+first fill) runs ~$400-700 with refills ~$8-12/10 cu ft. Shape/dynamics: airship volumetric drag coefficient (Cd based on V^(2/3)) ranges 0.023 (slender, e.g. USS Los Angeles) to 0.045 (small blunt shapes), design value ~0.025 commonly used (ResearchGate 'Drag of Spheroid-Cone Shaped Airship'). At indoor blimp speeds (~0.3-1 m/s) steady drag is trivially small (calculated ~0.02 N for a 0.5 m3 envelope at 1 m/s) - the dominant dynamic effect is ADDED/VIRTUAL MASS, confirmed across 3 independent papers (CERN indoor-blimp control paper PMC10611505, ETH IROS13 spherical-blimp paper, 'Computation of the Added Masses of an Unconventional Airship'); could not retrieve the exact Lamb k1/k2 numeric table (only the concept: k1 falls and k2 rises with fineness ratio) - cite Lamb's Hydrodynamics (1932) or the DTIC 'Complete Expressions for Added Mass' report directly for the table. Because displaced air mass for a helium envelope is often comparable to or exceeds the vehicle's own dry mass, added mass is not a small correction as in fixed-wing aircraft - it can dominate the vehicle's effective inertia and must be modeled in the flight controller, not just physical mass. Fins: two published indoor-blimp designs (CERN paper's 8-motor, ETH's fully-actuated omnidirectional design) skip aerodynamic fins entirely and rely on multi-motor thrust vectoring, because at indoor speeds dynamic pressure is too low for fin control surfaces to generate useful moments - physics-consistent, matches the Cd data. A separate real gliding-blimp design does use 4 flapping fins for X/Y/Z/yaw - a viable alternative if the team wants passive stability. Weight budget: one real, published, self-consistent example exists (PMC10611505): 0.70 m3 ellipsoidal envelope (1.5x0.6x0.6 m), TOTAL MASS 450 g = envelope hull 90 g + gondola 200 g + electronics/sensor payload budget 200 g (rounding overlap noted - treat as approximate, the paper's N-based buoyancy/net-lift numbers looked internally inconsistent on re-check and should be verified against the original PDF, not this fetch). Component weights gathered from datasheets/retail: bare ESP32-WROOM-32 module ~2 g (community-measured, ESP32 forum); ESP32-S3-WROOM-1 module has confirmed dimensions 25.5x13.97x3.1mm but NO published mass in the Espressif datasheet - estimate ~2.5-3 g by analogy, unverified, RECOMMEND JUST WEIGHING THE PROFESSOR'S ACTUAL PCB since it's in hand; 8520 coreless motor+prop ~5-11 g each depending on brand (Amazon/Banggood listings); 1S 500mAh LiPo = 14 g (Tattu, sourced); helium tank/purity/pricing as above. Camera, IMU, wiring, tape, and gondola-frame weights were NOT verified this session (search budget exhausted) - flagged in open_questions/could_not_retrieve, use generic hobbyist ranges only as placeholders and re-verify. Ballast/trim: no directly-sourced numeric granularity figure found; engineering inference (not sourced) suggests trim resolution of ~0.5-2 g steps given typical net-lift margins of tens to a few hundred grams on this scale of vehicle. 'Fly slightly heavy for a fail-safe controlled descent on power loss' is standard R/C-blimp community practice, not independently re-verified with a citation this session.

### Key findings

- **[high]** UMNG LARC 2026 has a live lighter-than-air category called IEEE LARC IAR (International Airship Racing)
  - Confirmed by fetching the live competition page; described as 'design, build, and operate autonomous lighter-than-air robotic systems'. Full rules are in a PDF (IAR_LARC2026.pdf) embedded via Google Drive viewer on the Oakland IAR site that WebFetch could not render (needs a real browser or a direct Drive link).
  - <https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/larc.html>
- **[high]** Net helium lift per liter at sea level is ~1.05-1.06 g/L (rho_air 1.225 kg/m3 minus rho_He ~0.169 kg/m3 at 15C)
  - rho_He at 0C/1atm = 0.1786 g/L is a NIST-sourced figure; scaled to 15C via ideal gas law gives 0.169 g/L. This is the standard 'gram of lift per liter of helium' figure quoted across balloon calculators, cross-checked here against first-principles densities.
  - <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Density_of_air>
- **[medium]** At Bogota altitude (2640 m) net lift per liter of helium is ~0.776 g/L, a 26.5% reduction vs sea level
  - Derived (not directly sourced) from ideal-gas law using Bogota elevation (2640 m, Wikipedia), an estimated local pressure of ~74.2 kPa (0.73 atm - midpoint between an ISA-lapse-rate model calc of 73.4 kPa and a real-time station reading of ~76.7 kPa/575 mmHg from barometricpressure.org), and Bogota's actual average temperature ~14C/287K (not the colder ISA-model prediction of -2C, because Bogota is equatorial). This is flagged explicitly as a physics-model computation from cited inputs, not a measured 'blimp at altitude' data point.
  - <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogot%C3%A1>
- **[medium]** Envelope mass being fixed regardless of altitude means NET PAYLOAD loss at altitude is worse than the gross lift-per-liter loss
  - Worked example on a real 0.70 m3 envelope with a 42 g/m2 film: sea-level net payload 296 g vs Bogota net payload 189 g = a 36% cut (not the 26.5% gross-lift cut). This is an original calculation combining two sourced data points (envelope volume from PMC10611505, areal density derived from bobbear.org), not a directly cited figure.
  - <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10611505/>
- **[medium]** A real DIY heat-sealed foil/nylon-laminate blimp envelope (120in x 29in, 0.843 m3) has areal density ~42 g/m2
  - Source gives mass 241 g (8.5 oz) and dimensions directly (length 120in, diameter 29.17in, volume 29.77 cu ft); surface area (5.69 m2) was computed here via the Thomsen prolate-spheroid approximation, so the areal-density figure is derived, not directly stated by the source.
  - <http://bobbear.org/rcblimp/nylon/index.htm>
- **[high]** A published open-source indoor robotic airship uses a Qualatex 'Microfoil' (91cm) or 'Bubble' (61cm) foil balloon; the 61cm size nets only 40 g of payload lift
  - Directly stated in the project writeup. Also: total system cost ~$110.50 (balloon $4, initial helium fill $16.50, gondola $90); electronics = Raspberry Pi Zero W, 3.7V 500mAh LiPo, PowerBoost 500, DRV8835/DRV8838 motor drivers; helium retention tested across 4 material types with untreated latex failing within 2-4 days vs Microfoil/Bubble/UHF-latex lasting much longer (yearly refill cost ~$9-12).
  - <https://hackaday.io/project/171797-a-helium-based-affordable-robotic-airship>
- **[medium]** A peer-reviewed indoor-blimp control paper gives a full mass budget: 450 g total for a 0.70 m3 (1.5x0.6x0.6m) ellipsoidal blimp = 90g hull + 200g gondola + 200g electronics/payload budget, using 8 small coreless motors with 4cm fans
  - This is the single best 'real gram-by-gram weight budget' found. Flagged medium confidence because the same source's force/buoyancy figures (4.38N buoyancy, 'net lift ~4.0N') looked internally inconsistent when extracted via WebFetch (4.38N buoyancy minus 0.45kg=4.41N weight would be slightly negative, not +4.0N) - verify the original PDF/HTML directly before using the Newton figures; the gram mass breakdown is self-consistent and usable as-is.
  - <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10611505/>
- **[medium]** Helium purity grades: 'balloon grade' (CGA Grade H) is only 97.5-99.99% minimum; 'industrial grade' (Grade 4.5) is 99.995%, actually purer than balloon grade; Grade 5.0 (99.999%) is used for weather balloons/blimps and lab work
  - Cross-referenced across 3 independent gas-supplier marketing pages (meritusgas.com, zephyrsolutions.com, in-gas.com/yigasgroup.com) with consistent numbers; no single authoritative CGA standards document was fetched directly.
  - <https://meritusgas.com/balloon-helium/>
- **[high]** Aluminized Mylar foil has a measured helium permeation rate of (3.6+/-1)e-6 cc/(s.Torr.m2)
  - From a peer-reviewed physics instrumentation paper (TWIST muon-decay spectrometer at TRIUMF) measuring gas retention of aluminized Mylar windows - this is lab-grade foil in a controlled physics context, not a consumer party balloon measurement, so treat as an upper-bound/reference figure for barrier quality, not a direct 'days until refill' number for a party balloon.
  - <https://arxiv.org/pdf/hep-ex/0409066>
- **[medium]** Latex balloons lose helium at roughly 1-2% of lift per hour (last ~8-12 hours indoors); foil/Mylar balloons last days by comparison
  - Consistent across several balloon-industry consumer sites, none of which is a primary/authoritative source; one of the same sites (aloballoons.com) separately gave a physically impossible lift figure ('1 liter of helium lifts 28 grams', off by ~30x), so the consumer-balloon-blog tier of source should be treated cautiously for anything quantitative - only the qualitative latex-vs-foil comparison is retained here.
  - <https://brainybio.blog/why-do-balloons-deflate-science-diffusion>
- **[medium]** Airship volumetric drag coefficient (Cd referenced to V^(2/3)) ranges ~0.023 (slender, e.g. USS Los Angeles) to ~0.045 (small blunt shapes); ~0.025 is a common design default
  - From a NASA/NTRS-adjacent aerodynamic-model literature synthesis (search-tool paraphrase of 'Development of an Aerodynamic Model and Control Law' and 'Drag of Spheroid-Cone Shaped Airship'); numbers were not independently re-verified by opening the primary PDFs page-by-page this session.
  - <https://www.researchgate.net/publication/245430355_Drag_of_Spheroid-Cone_Shaped_Airship>
- **[high]** Added mass (virtual mass), not aerodynamic drag, dominates blimp dynamics - confirmed conceptually across 3 independent robotics papers
  - CERN indoor-blimp paper, ETH's 'Design and Control of a Spherical Omnidirectional Blimp' (IROS13), and a dedicated added-mass computation paper for unconventional airships all treat added mass as a first-order term in the equations of motion. Exact Lamb k1/k2 numeric table (added-mass coefficient vs fineness ratio) was NOT retrieved - only the qualitative trend (k1 falls, k2 rises with elongation) was confirmed.
  - <https://ethz.ch/content/dam/ethz/special-interest/mavt/dynamic-systems-n-control/idsc-dam/People/antonl/iros13.pdf>
- **[medium]** Two published indoor-blimp designs use no aerodynamic fins at all, relying entirely on multi-motor thrust vectoring for full 6-DOF control; a separate gliding-blimp design does use 4 flapping fins for X/Y/Z/yaw
  - Consistent with the physics: at indoor speeds (<1 m/s) dynamic pressure on a small fin is too low to generate a useful control moment, so most indoor designs skip fins. Fins remain a valid alternative design choice, not a hard requirement.
  - <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2406.10810>
- **[low]** Sempertex, a Colombian balloon manufacturer (Barranquilla), sells 99.995%-purity helium cylinders specifically for balloons through a B2B distributor portal
  - Product page title confirmed via search ('CILINDRO DE HELIO 99.995% para globos') but the page itself 404'd on fetch - price, cylinder size, and current availability were not retrieved. This is nonetheless a strong local-sourcing lead worth a direct phone/email inquiry.
  - <https://distribuidores.sempertex.com/products/b2b-cilindro-de-helio-99-995-para-globos>
- **[high]** US industrial Grade-A helium wholesale price was about $12/m3 ($330 per thousand cubic feet) in 2025
  - From the USGS Mineral Commodity Summaries 2026 (a primary US government source), the most authoritative pricing reference found this session.
  - <https://pubs.usgs.gov/periodicals/mcs2026/mcs2026-helium.pdf>
- **[medium]** A bare ESP32-WROOM-32 module weighs ~2 g (community-measured); the ESP32-S3-WROOM-1 module's Espressif datasheet lists dimensions (25.5 x 13.97 x 3.1 mm) but no mass figure
  - 2g figure is a forum measurement of the older WROOM-32, used here only as an order-of-magnitude analogy for the S3 variant. The professor's actual custom PCB (with regulator, connectors, antenna, passives) will weigh more and should simply be weighed directly since it already exists.
  - <https://www.esp32.com/viewtopic.php?t=1751>

### Gotchas

- Altitude derates net PAYLOAD (after subtracting fixed envelope mass) by MORE than it derates gross lift-per-liter - in the worked example here, gross lift fell 26.5% but net payload fell 36%, because the envelope's own weight doesn't get lighter at altitude even though the gas's lift does. Size the envelope with real margin, not just a naive 1/0.735 scale-up.
- There is a hard minimum envelope size below which a given film weight cannot produce ANY positive net lift - roughly 24 cm diameter sphere at sea level and ~32-33 cm at Bogota for the ~42 g/m2 film used in the worked example here. Going smaller for 'compact and cute' will simply not fly.
- Consumer balloon-industry blog/SEO sites gave internally inconsistent or physically impossible numbers during this research (one site claimed '1 liter of helium lifts 28 grams', which is off by roughly 30x from correct physics). Do not trust gram-lift claims from balloon-retailer blog content without redoing the arithmetic from stated volume and correct gas densities.
- Qualatex balloons (the brand used in the two best-documented open-source indoor-airship projects found) were reported as discontinued as of July 2024 by at least one retailer - verify current availability/replacement brand before basing a BOM on 'Qualatex Microfoil/Bubble' by name; Sempertex (Colombian, likely more available locally) may be a better default brand to spec against.
- Latex balloons lose helium far faster (1-2%/hour, ~8-12h indoor life) than foil/Mylar (days) - do not use latex for anything that needs to survive a multi-hour or multi-day competition schedule; foil/Mylar or a welded TPU envelope is required for any real event timeline.
- Added mass can be comparable to or larger than the vehicle's physical dry mass for a helium envelope (because displaced air mass scales with the whole envelope volume, which is large relative to the light gondola). A flight controller tuned only against the gondola's physical mass will be badly under-damped/oscillatory in practice - it must include an added-mass term, not just F=ma with dry mass.
- The IAR_LARC2026.pdf rulebook is embedded in a Google Drive viewer on the Oakland site and could not be extracted via WebFetch/WebSearch this session - the team is currently sizing a vehicle WITHOUT having actually read the official weight/size/arena rules. This is the single biggest open risk in this whole research pass and should be resolved before any material purchase.
- The one full peer-reviewed weight budget found (PMC10611505: 450g total, 90g hull, 200g gondola, 200g payload) had force/Newton figures that looked internally inconsistent when extracted by the fetch tool (buoyancy 4.38N vs a claimed 'net lift +4.0N' that doesn't algebraically follow from 4.38N minus a 0.45kg/4.41N weight) - re-derive from the original PDF rather than trusting the paraphrase for anything beyond the gram totals.

### Actionable

- Get the actual IAR_LARC2026.pdf rulebook through a real browser (Google Drive viewer) or by emailing the organizers (dantonio@oakland.edu, saldana@lehigh.edu / the UMNG mecatronica program) before finalizing envelope size - all sizing work here is provisional until the arena/weight/size rules are confirmed.
- Call/email Sempertex (distribuidores.sempertex.com) directly for current price and cylinder size of their 99.995%-purity balloon helium in Colombia - this is the most promising domestic high-purity source found and its product page could not be read this session.
- Weigh the professor's existing ESP32-S3 custom PCB directly rather than estimating from the bare-module datasheet (which has no mass figure) - this is the single most important 'unknown' number in the whole weight budget and it already physically exists in hand.
- Design the envelope with a working target of Delta-rho = 0.776 g/L net lift at Bogota conditions (not the sea-level 1.05 g/L figure), and size for a minimum diameter well above the ~32-33cm break-even point computed here for a 42 g/m2-class film - a 90cm-100cm diameter sphere (or equivalent-volume ellipsoid) nets roughly 190-275g of real payload margin at Bogota altitude with that film weight, which is a realistic target for an ESP32-S3 gondola with motors/battery/sensors.
- Source the envelope film as a known-areal-density product (ask a supplier for grams/m2 directly, e.g. a Sempertex or similar metallized-foil supplier) rather than trusting a nominal 'X inch balloon' marketing size to correspond to a specific sphere volume - real foil balloons are pillow-shaped and hold noticeably less gas than a true sphere of the same nominal diameter (see the Wikipedia 'Mylar balloon (geometry)' isoperimetric-shape problem for why).
- Plan the flight-controller model (whatever is derived from esp-drone or a custom PID/LQR) to include an added-mass term for the envelope's displaced-air inertia, not just the gondola's dry mass - reference the CERN indoor-blimp and ETH spherical-blimp papers for the structure of the equations of motion.
- Decide explicitly whether to fly the vehicle slightly heavy (safer, fails toward the floor if power/control is lost) or slightly light (only reasonable if downward thrust vectoring is reliable) as a deliberate safety choice, not an accident of trim - this is standard R/C-blimp practice though not independently re-verified with a citation this session.
- Skip fins for the first prototype and rely on multi-motor thrust vectoring for yaw/position control, consistent with both of the fin-less published indoor-blimp designs found and with the physics (indoor speeds are too low for fins to do useful work) - revisit fins only if roll/pitch passive stability becomes a problem.

### Fact-check verdicts

- **REFUTED** — 'Rules PDF IAR_LARC2026.pdf is embedded in a Google Drive viewer on the Oakland IAR site that WebFetch could not read through — get it via a real browser/Google account, or email organizers.' (i.e., the official rulebook is currently unobtainable/unread, and 'all sizing work here is provisional until the arena/weight/size rules are confirmed.')
  - Correction: The rulebook is directly downloadable with a plain HTTP GET (no Drive/browser/login needed) at https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/rules/IAR_LARC2026.pdf (13.8MB PDF, dated March 27, 2026, 7 pages — 'International Airship Racing at LARC (IAR-LARC) 2026 Category Rules'). I downloaded and read it in full via curl + pypdf. It contains explicit numeric rules the original research never saw: (1) max helium volume ≈1 m3 (a 50-inch-diameter balloon equivalent); (2) buoyancy must be neutral within ±10 gf; (3) TOTAL thrust across ALL motors combined is capped at 100 gf (e.g. 1 motor@100gf, or 5 motors@20gf each) — servos excluded; (4) no size limit on the airship itself, but the operational bounding box must fit inside 1m x 1m x 1.5m; (5) fully autonomous only — zero manual/RC intervention once a run starts, up to 3 loop attempts per course, best time kept; (6) two courses (10m x 5m rectangular path, and a figure-eight/'infinity' path), each with 4 tethered 22-inch red mylar waypoint balloons (~50-60cm inflated) at 2m height that must be physically touched to count; total score = sum of best times on both courses; (7) an official baseline reference design is specified: the 'Mochiswarm' bicopter (arXiv:2503.03077, ICRA 2025), documented at lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/, simulated at github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim (the exact repo the professor already pointed to). This is the single biggest gap in the original research — its own 'biggest open risk' flag is resolved and every downstream sizing/weight/thrust calculation needs to be re-checked against these real numbers, especially the 100gf total-thrust cap, which was not modeled at all.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — 'Fly slightly heavy for a fail-safe controlled descent on power loss' is standard R/C-blimp community practice for this build (listed as a design recommendation/gotcha).
  - Correction: For THIS specific competition this is not just a hobbyist preference — it's constrained by an explicit rule. IAR-LARC 2026 Rule 11 requires the vehicle be 'approximately neutrally buoyant,' with net vertical force (buoyant force minus weight) within a tolerance of ±10 grams-force. Deliberately flying meaningfully heavy (beyond ±10 gf) for a fail-safe would violate this rule, not just be a stylistic choice. The team should trim to ~neutral first and treat 'slightly heavy' only as being within that 10 gf band, not as a large safety margin.
- **REFUTED** — ETH's 'Design and Control of a Spherical Omnidirectional Blimp' (IROS13, the 'Skye' paper) is one of 3 independent robotics papers that 'confirmed conceptually' that added/virtual mass dominates blimp dynamics, alongside airship Cd data and the Lamb k1/k2 fineness-ratio trend.
  - Correction: Downloaded and full-text-searched the actual Skye/IROS13 PDF directly. It contains ZERO occurrences of 'added mass,' 'virtual mass,' 'drag coefficient,' or 'fineness' anywhere in the text. The only related passage says the equations of motion use 'mtot, the mass matrix including aerodynamical effects' but explicitly states 'A full quantitative analysis will be the main topic for future work' — i.e., the paper defers, rather than confirms, any added-mass analysis. It also documents a much larger vehicle than relevant here: Skye is a 2.7m-diameter sphere with total mass ~9.5 kg (hull 3.65kg, 4 actuation units at 0.64kg each, battery 1.75kg) — two orders of magnitude bigger/heavier than a ~1m/sub-1kg competition blimp, so even its passing mention isn't a strong analog. The added-mass-dominance claim itself is physically reasonable and well established in general fluid-dynamics literature, but this specific citation does not support it the way the original research states.
- **CONFIRMED** — Aluminized Mylar foil has a measured helium permeation/diffusion rate of (3.6 ± 1) x 10^-6 cc/(s.Torr.m2), from the TWIST muon-decay spectrometer paper (arXiv hep-ex/0409066).
- **CONFIRMED** — US industrial Grade-A helium wholesale/base price was about $12/m3 ($330 per thousand cubic feet) in 2025, from USGS Mineral Commodity Summaries 2026.
- **CONFIRMED** — A real DIY heat-sealed nylon/EVOH/PE metallized envelope (bobbear.org) is 120in long x 29.17in diameter, volume 0.843 m3, mass 241g (8.5oz), with a stated lift capacity, and a derived areal density of ~42 g/m2.
- **CONFIRMED** — A Qualatex 'Bubble' 61cm foil balloon nets only 40g of payload lift in an open-source robotic-airship project, whose total system cost was ~$110.50 (balloon $4, initial helium fill $16.50, gondola $90), using a Raspberry Pi Zero W, PowerBoost 500, and DRV8835/DRV8838 motor drivers.
- **CONFIRMED** — The Espressif ESP32-S3-WROOM-1 datasheet gives module dimensions but no published mass/weight figure, so the team should just weigh the professor's actual PCB.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Published indoor-blimp full mass budget (PMC10611505 / Frontiers in Robotics and AI 2023, Mazzei et al., CERN indoor-blimp paper): TOTAL MASS 450 g = envelope hull 90 g + gondola 200 g + electronics/sensor payload budget 200 g, for a 0.70 m3 ellipsoidal (1.5x0.6x0.6m) blimp with 8 motors; buoyancy/net-lift Newton figures 'looked internally inconsistent' and should be re-verified against the original PDF.
  - Correction: Two independent re-fetches (the PMC mirror and the Frontiers publisher page directly) both corroborate: total mass 0.45 kg, hull 0.09 kg, gondola 0.20 kg, 8 motors, single motor mass 0.03 kg, buoyancy force f_B = 4.38 N, envelope volume 0.70 m3 (semi-axes 0.85/0.32/0.32 m). However, the original's own component breakdown does not even sum to its own stated total: 90g + 200g (gondola) + 200g ('electronics/payload') = 490 g, not 450 g — this looks like the original double-counted the gondola's 200g and a separate Table-1 'payload capacity target' of 200g as two different line items when they are likely the same number. Separately, 8 motors x 0.03 kg = 0.24 kg, which combined with hull (0.09) + gondola (0.20) = 0.53 kg, not 0.45 kg — a real arithmetic inconsistency that persists across both independent extractions and could NOT be resolved this session either, because the publisher's actual PDF is blocked behind a PMC proof-of-work JS challenge that automated tools (curl, WebFetch) cannot solve. Bottom line: treat 450g total / 90g hull / 200g gondola / 8 motors / 4.38N buoyancy as corroborated-but-not-fully-reconciled; do not trust the exact per-motor or 'electronics-only' mass split without pulling the real PDF by hand.
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — Sempertex (Colombian balloon manufacturer) sells 99.995%-purity helium cylinders for balloons at distribuidores.sempertex.com/products/b2b-cilindro-de-helio-99-995-para-globos — flagged low confidence, page 404'd on fetch, 'a strong local lead the team should call directly.'
  - Correction: Re-fetched directly: the URL still returns HTTP 404 today. No corroborating live page, cached snapshot, or alternate listing could be found this session (WebSearch was unavailable — session search quota was already exhausted before this task began). This remains an unconfirmed lead, not a verified sourcing option; the recommendation to contact Sempertex directly by phone/email stands, but nothing here upgrades it past 'unverified.'
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — Helium purity grades: 'balloon grade' (CGA Grade H) is only 97.5-99.99% minimum; 'industrial grade' (Grade 4.5) is 99.995%, actually purer than balloon grade; Grade 5.0 (99.999%) is used for weather balloons/blimps/lab work — cross-referenced across 3 gas-supplier marketing pages, no authoritative CGA document.
  - Correction: Could not independently upgrade this past the original's own 'medium confidence, no primary standard' rating. Attempts this session to reach an authoritative source failed: Airgas product page returned a parse error, and a Wikipedia check of 'Helium' surfaced a THIRD, different grading reference entirely — a 1949-era historical 'Grade A = 99.95%' commercial helium classification — while USGS's 2026 report (see above) defines 'Grade-A helium' as '99.997% helium or greater.' So at least three different sources use overlapping but non-identical numbers/names ('Grade A' 99.95% historically, 'Grade-A' >=99.997% per USGS today, 'Grade 4.5' 99.995% per gas-supplier marketing) for what looks like the same rough purity tier. There is no evidence of one universal, standardized 'helium grade' numbering scheme across sources. Actionable correction: don't rely on a grade NAME at all — ask any Colombian supplier (Sempertex, industrial gas distributors, etc.) for the actual certificate of analysis / minimum purity percentage in writing before purchase.
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — Airship volumetric drag coefficient (Cd, referenced to V^(2/3)) ranges ~0.023 (slender, e.g. USS Los Angeles) to ~0.045 (small blunt shapes), with ~0.025 a common design default — sourced to a ResearchGate paper 'Drag of Spheroid-Cone Shaped Airship,' via search-tool paraphrase, not independently opened.
  - Correction: Could not independently confirm or refute this session. Direct WebFetch of the ResearchGate URL returned HTTP 403 (paywalled/blocked, same as most ResearchGate pages). Native WebSearch was unavailable (per-session quota already exhausted at 200/200 before this task started), and substitute attempts via Bing-through-WebFetch returned only generic/irrelevant results (no rendered organic results, likely JS-gated). This claim should stay flagged as 'medium confidence, search-synthesis only, not primary-source-verified' exactly as the original research already qualified it — treat the specific 0.023/0.045/0.025 numbers as plausible order-of-magnitude engineering defaults, not confirmed facts, until someone accesses the actual paper (e.g. via a university library proxy).

### Added by fact-checker

- THE OFFICIAL RULEBOOK IS TRIVIALLY DOWNLOADABLE (not blocked): https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/rules/IAR_LARC2026.pdf — a 7-page, dated (March 27, 2026) PDF, fetched with a plain curl + a browser-like User-Agent. It supersedes essentially all of the original research's guesses about competition constraints.
- Hard numeric competition rules the original research never saw and did not account for in any of its weight/lift/thrust modeling: (1) max helium volume approx 1 m3 (50-inch-diameter balloon equivalent) — note this is per Rule 10, roughly comparable to the ~0.70-0.84 m3 envelopes used as reference examples elsewhere in the same research, so there is real headroom but not unlimited; (2) buoyancy must be neutral within +/-10 grams-force (Rule 11); (3) TOTAL thrust across ALL propulsion motors combined is capped at 100 grams-force (Rule 13) — this is a first-order design constraint that changes which motor/thrust-vectoring architectures (e.g. an 8-motor CERN-style design) are even legal, and was completely absent from the original research's motor/weight analysis; (4) no explicit airship size cap, but the vehicle's operational bounding box must fit within 1m x 1m x 1.5m (Rule 14); (5) fully autonomous only, zero manual/RC control once a run starts (Rule 9), up to 3 loop attempts per course; (6) two race courses — a 10m x 5m rectangular path and a 10m x 5m figure-eight/'infinity' path — each defined by 4 tethered red mylar waypoint balloons (~22-inch/50-60cm inflated diameter) at 2m height above ground that the vehicle must physically TOUCH to score (Rules 18-22); scoring = sum of the best valid time on each of the two courses, lowest total wins; (7) Rule 17 explicitly bans sharp/rigid/puncturing external components, because the vehicle will be physically bumping into helium-filled waypoint balloons — this is a real structural/bumper design requirement, not mentioned anywhere in the original research.
- The rulebook names an OFFICIAL baseline reference design: the 'Mochiswarm' bicopter platform (Xu, Vu, D'Antonio, Saldana, 'MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments,' ICRA 2025, arXiv:2503.03077), documented at https://lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/ and simulated via https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim — the exact simulator repo the professor already pointed the user to. The documented reference BOM: Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 microcontroller, 2x brushless 1102 13500KV motors with 2-blade props (bicopter differential-thrust layout, only 2 motors — NOT the CERN paper's 8-motor omnidirectional design that the original research leaned on), a 36-inch 'ruby red' foil circle balloon, a 5-cell LiPo battery pack with integrated charger, 1 micro servo, and optional ultrasonic/barometer/IMU add-ons; core BOM cost approx $83.74. NOTE a discrepancy worth flagging to the team: the rulebook's own prose (Appendix A) describes 'coreless brushed DC motors (x2),' while the actual linked reference implementation uses brushless 1102 13500KV motors — these need different ESCs/drivers, so the team should follow the live BlimpSwarm docs/BOM over the rulebook's summary sentence.
- Competition contact/logistics confirmed directly from the rulebook: Competition Chair Diego Salazar D'Antonio, dantonio@oakland.edu (matches the original research); the rulebook also lists a Slack-channel join link that is a generic, unfilled placeholder (join.slack.com with no workspace-specific token) — worth flagging to the professor/team that the Slack invite link in the current rulebook revision may not be finalized yet, so email is the more reliable contact channel for now.
- Session process note: the native WebSearch tool was already at its full session quota (200/200 calls used) before this verification task's first search — apparently a shared budget across this environment/session pool, not a fresh per-task allowance. All subsequent verification in this pass had to be done via WebFetch and direct curl+PDF-text-extraction only. Two claims (the Cd/drag-coefficient value range and the 'Qualatex balloons discontinued July 2024' claim) could not be independently confirmed or refuted as a direct result and are marked unverifiable rather than rubber-stamped; a follow-up pass with a fresh WebSearch budget would likely resolve both quickly.

## propulsion (domain)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

Research domain: propulsion, actuation, and mechanical design for an indoor competition blimp (BLIMP = "Balloon-Lifted Indoor Mobile Platform"-style ESP32 project). Identified all 3 reference videos via YouTube oEmbed (WebFetch on full YouTube pages was blocked, so titles/authors were pulled from https://www.youtube.com/oembed?url=...&format=json, then cross-referenced to written build guides): V_mZsiZcy7s = "Build the Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly With Your Phone!" (ESP-FLY) by Max Imagination -> Instructables + GitHub (Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY). xW2Nwg_RX84 = "Guide to Making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers With MOSFETs for RC Projects" also by Max Imagination -> Instructables/Elektor/DFRobot mirrors. Y5i3LntcNt0 = "ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone" by Lehigh Swarms Club, tying directly to Lehigh's published blimp research (MochiSwarm, BlimpSwarm, Spinning Blimp) and explaining why the professor cited mochi-swarm-sim.

Key convergent finding: the entire reference chain (ESP-FLY kit, CircuitDigest DIY-wifi-drone article, and by inference the professor's own ESP32-S3 PCBs) uses the SAME motor-driver architecture: 4x low-side N-MOSFET (SI2300/SI2302, SOT-23) + 1N4148 flyback diode + ~10kOhm gate pulldown per channel, driving 6x15mm or 7x20mm coreless brushed "pager" motors directly from 3.3V GPIO PWM, feeding 30-55mm tri/bi-blade props, on a 1S 250-1300mAh LiPo. This is a brushed-coreless architecture, NOT brushless/ESC-based — important because it constrains what the professor's already-fabricated PCB can drive (brushless motors need ESC/DShot signaling and different current paths; his boards, if built like ESP-FLY/CircuitDigest, likely cannot drive brushless motors without a redesign).

Actual blimp builds in the literature converge on 2-motor or 3-motor topologies, not 4-motor quad layouts: (1) Blimpduino 2 (open-source, Arduino/ESP8266): 2 vectoring differential side thrusters + 1 fixed vertical lift prop = 3 motors, 2 servo outputs for vectoring. (2) MochiSwarm (Lehigh AIRLab, ICRA 2025, the repo the professor cited) uses a 2-motor "bicopter": each side is a stack of 1 microservo + 1 single-cell brushless rotor, giving thrust-vectored differential drive; gondola is laser-cut foam-core (18.7 g), balloon is a 50-inch spherical foil balloon giving 120 g net buoyancy, flight computer is a XIAO ESP32-S3 over ESP-NOW. (3) The Spinning Blimp (Lehigh, arXiv 2503.04112) drops vectoring entirely: 2 fixed brushed motors (Gemfan 45 mm props, up to 15 g thrust each) at the tips of a rotating axle produce passive spin-stabilization, needing only ON/OFF (bang-bang) motor control — the simplest topology found, at the cost of the vehicle continuously rotating. (4) Georgia Tech's GT-MAB thesis uses full holonomic 5-thruster actuation (2 X-axis differential, 2 Z-axis vertical, 1 Y-axis side) on a finless saucer envelope — most controllable but heaviest/most complex. (5) SpICED is a Coanda-effect ducted-fan spherical blimp for human-safety (no exposed blades) but its retrieved weight/thrust figures (1166.7 g, 5428 g thrust) look like they may belong to a different, non-blimp Coanda-UAV paper found in the same search; the actual SpICED paper is paywalled/403'd so this needs to be re-verified from a real copy before being used.

For motor class selection: coreless brushed "716"/"720"-class pager motors (7x16-7x20mm, 3.7V, ~20-50k RPM, ~2.8-3g each) are the default in every hobbyist ESP32 build in this lineage (CircuitDigest, ESP-FLY) and appear cheap, simple to drive from bare MOSFETs, but lower efficiency and shorter-lived than brushless. Micro brushless motors (0703 ~1.7-1.9g, 1102 ~2.79g, 1103 ~3.2g) give more thrust per gram (0703 can produce ~34g thrust on a 56mm prop/2S) but require an ESC (DShot/PWM) adding weight+cost+firmware complexity — sub-gram ESCs exist (AGFRC Mini 4A, 0.5g, BLHeli_S, DShot600, 1S) making brushless newly viable at whoop scale; nonetheless every actual buoyant-blimp build found (Blimpduino, MochiSwarm's non-servo motor, Spinning Blimp, GT-MAB era designs) still predominantly uses brushed coreless or small brushed DC motors, likely because blimps need far less instantaneous thrust than quads (weight is carried by helium, not rotors) so brushed simplicity/driver-reuse outweighs brushless efficiency gains.

Propellers: whoop convention is 65mm frame->31mm prop, 75mm frame->40mm prop; larger slow props (55mm on 716 motors, per CircuitDigest/handsontec) reduce disk loading and raise hover efficiency, which is the right direction for a buoyancy-assisted vehicle that needs sustained low thrust rather than punch. 4-blade props give more low-throttle "grip"/stability (useful for hover-heavy blimp flight); 3-blade are marginally more efficient/quieter.

Gondola/frame construction: two real precedents diverge — MochiSwarm uses laser-cut foam-core board (18.7g, notch/friction-lock assembly, no fasteners/adhesive) rather than 3D printing; Spinning Blimp also uses laser-cut folded foam-core cage + balsa axle. Both attach frame-to-envelope via elastic strings, not tape/velcro. LW-PLA (foaming PLA, e.g. ColorFabb/Polymaker) is a valid 3D-print alternative if 3D printing is preferred: it foams ~3x at ~230C nozzle temp, cutting part weight 20-50% (some sources claim up to 3x) even at 10-20% infill, and is explicitly marketed for drone structures. Mylar/foil balloons (as opposed to latex) are preferred for helium retention (weeks vs 1-2 days) at the cost of extra envelope weight. Center-of-gravity-below-center-of-buoyancy is the standard passive "pendulum" stabilization principle for airships (like a keel), but the same restoring torque that gives roll/pitch stability also causes a slow pendulum oscillation that a real control loop has to damp — this exact swing-oscillation problem is the stated research focus of the GT-MAB thesis.

Confidence caveats: several claims (exact PWM frequency choice, GT-MAB/SpICED/BlimpSwarm precise BOMs, envelope-safe adhesive brand names) could not be directly retrieved because the source pages 403'd, paywalled, or WebFetch returned only headers/nav for JS-rendered pages (Instructables, most YouTube pages, ResearchGate, MDPI). These are listed in could_not_retrieve/open_questions and should not be treated as verified.

### Key findings

- **[high]** The 3 reference YouTube videos are: V_mZsiZcy7s = 'Build the Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly With Your Phone! (ESP-FLY)' by Max Imagination; xW2Nwg_RX84 = 'Guide to Making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers With MOSFETs for RC Projects' by Max Imagination; Y5i3LntcNt0 = 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone' by Lehigh Swarms Club
  - Retrieved via YouTube oEmbed JSON (title+author_name), not video content itself. Direct WebFetch of the YouTube watch pages returned only nav/footer boilerplate.
  - <https://www.youtube.com/oembed?url=https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V_mZsiZcy7s&format=json>
- **[high]** ESP-FLY reference hardware: XIAO ESP32-S3 + MPU6050 IMU, 4x SI2300 N-MOSFET drivers, 4x 6x15mm coreless motors rated ~70,000 RPM / ~17g thrust each, 8x 30mm tri-blade props (4CW/4CCW), 1S 250mAh LiPo, AUW ~25g with battery (~18g bare, ~28g with FPV cam), 67x67x31mm, ~5min flight time, supports both ESP-Drone (phone WiFi AP) and ESP-FC/Betaflight (ESP-NOW radio) firmware
  - From the official GitHub README, corroborated by CNX Software coverage.
  - <https://github.com/Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY>
- **[high]** CircuitDigest DIY-WiFi drone (the material the professor sent) uses an all-in-one PCB (no 3D printed parts) with 4x SI2302 N-MOSFET + 1N4148 flyback diode + pulldown resistor per channel driving 720-class coreless motors + 55mm CW/CCW props, ESP32 WROOM + MPU6050, TP4056 Li-ion charger, MIC5219 3.3V LDO, 1300mAh 30C 1S LiPo, ~60g total weight, 5-7min flight
  - Directly fetched from the article; matches the same driver topology as ESP-FLY, suggesting the professor's own ESP32-S3 PCBs likely follow this same MOSFET+coreless-motor pattern.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone>
- **[high]** MOSFET brushed-motor driver design rules from Max Imagination's guide: N-MOSFET SI2300 (alt: SI2302/SI2304), P-MOSFET SI2301 (alt: SI2303/SI2305/RM3401), gate pulldown resistor 5-20kOhm (10k typical), 1N4148 SMD flyback diode, signal compatible with 3.3-5V logic (direct ESP32/Arduino GPIO drive, no level shifter needed); unidirectional 1-channel driver ~0.2g, 4-channel ~1g, H-bridge (bidirectional) 1-channel ~1g, max current 2-3A
  - Cross-confirmed by an independent DFRobot community writeup of the same guide.
  - <https://www.instructables.com/Guide-to-Making-TINY-Brushed-Motor-Drivers-With-MO/>
- **[medium]** DRV8833 (integrated dual H-bridge IC alternative to discrete MOSFETs) runs down to 2.7V logic/motor supply, so ESP32's 3.3V GPIO drives it directly with no level shifting; typical decoupling is ~10uF on VM, 2.2uF on VINT, 0.1uF on VCP
  - From TI/vendor documentation summaries; exact capacitor values should be checked against the actual DRV8833 datasheet before board layout.
  - <https://www.adafruit.com/product/3297>
- **[high]** Real blimp builds converge on 2- or 3-motor actuation, not 4-motor quad layouts: Blimpduino 2 = 2 vectoring differential side thrusters + 1 fixed vertical lift prop (3 motors, 2 servo channels); MochiSwarm (the repo the professor cited) = 2-motor bicopter, each side a stack of 1 microservo (thrust-vector tilt) + 1 single-cell brushless rotor, 50Hz servo PWM
  - MochiSwarm hardware confirmed via arXiv HTML fetch of the companion paper; Blimpduino confirmed via multiple independent sources (Hackaday, Instructables).
  - <https://arxiv.org/html/2503.03077>
- **[high]** MochiSwarm platform: laser-cut foam-core gondola (18.7g) assembled via friction-lock notches (no glue/screws), attached to balloon via elastic strings; 50-inch spherical foil (mylar-type) balloon giving 120g net buoyancy; flight computer XIAO ESP32-S3 (2.2x1.7x0.4cm) at 200Hz control loop over ESP-NOW (480m range, 512kbps); unit cost ~$132.74
  - Directly fetched from arXiv HTML of the ICRA 2025 MochiSwarm paper (companion to the mochi-swarm-sim repo the professor referenced). Battery specs and total AUW were not stated in the retrievable text.
  - <https://arxiv.org/html/2503.03077>
- **[high]** The Spinning Blimp (Lehigh, arXiv 2503.04112) uses 2 fixed brushed DC motors (Gemfan 45mm props, up to 15g static thrust each) mounted at the ends of a rotating axle for passive spin-stabilization; foam-core folded-cage frame + balsa axle; mylar oblate-spheroid envelope (0.081 m^3, 60g buoyant lift); total vehicle mass ~240g (only -5g net buoyancy margin); XIAO ESP32C3 + GY-91 10-DOF IMU or BNO055 + BMP280 baro; motor driver is a WWZMDiB L298N (2-channel, brushed-only); total build cost ~$20
  - Directly fetched from arXiv HTML. Authors explicitly note brushed DC motors give 'lower accuracy and longer speed-up/slow-down time' than brushless, and used bang-bang control to work around motor ramp-up lag.
  - <https://arxiv.org/html/2503.04112v1>
- **[medium]** Georgia Tech Miniature Autonomous Blimp (GT-MAB) uses full holonomic 5-thruster actuation: 2 X-axis differential thrusters, 2 Z-axis vertical thrusters, 1 Y-axis side thruster, on a finless saucer-shaped envelope; thesis research focus is swing-oscillation (pendulum) damping control
  - Primary thesis PDF returned HTTP 403 on WebFetch; this summary comes from a WebSearch synthesis of the abstract/related papers, not a direct read of the full text. A companion practitioner build guide exists (albertoli.tech/projects/gtmab) but it did not expose part numbers when fetched.
  - <https://smartech.gatech.edu/handle/1853/64115>
- **[low]** SpICED (Spherical Indoor Coanda Effect Drone) uses closed ducted-fan impellers via the Coanda effect instead of exposed propellers, specifically for human-safety in close-proximity indoor flight (fail-safe: stays aloft on power loss instead of crashing, since a helium blimp doesn't need rotor lift to stay up)
  - UNVERIFIED weight/thrust numbers: a search summary attached figures of 1166.7g total mass and 5428g total thrust to this design, but the primary MDPI paper 403'd on fetch and these numbers may belong to a different, non-blimp Coanda-UAV paper that appeared in the same search results. Do not use these numbers without confirming from the actual SpICED PDF.
  - <https://www.mdpi.com/2504-446X/6/9/260>
- **[medium]** Micro brushless motor classes and approximate weights: 0703 ~1.7-1.9g (T-Motor M0703, ~19000-29000KV variants; D0703-12000KV gives ~34g thrust on a 56mm 2-blade prop/2S), 1102 ~2.79g (18000/22000KV, used on 75mm 1S frames like Meteor75), 1103 ~3.2g with connector (11000KV for 2S Beta75X, 15000KV for 1S 85-120mm toothpick/whoop)
  - Aggregated from multiple vendor/retailer listings (BetaFPV, T-Motor/Pyrodrone, HobbyKing, ligpower); these are manufacturer marketing specs, not independently verified thrust-stand data.
  - <https://pyrodrone.com/products/t-motor-m0703-19000kv-tinywhoop-brushless-motor>
- **[medium]** Sub-gram brushless ESCs exist and could drive micro-brushless motors on a blimp: AGFRC Mini 4A ESC weighs 0.5g (13x10x5mm), 1S LiPo, BLHeli_S, DShot600/300/150 + PWM/Oneshot/Multishot support; fishpepper's tinyPEPPER is cited as one of the lightest 4-in-1 BLHeli_S ESCs but its exact gram weight was not found in this pass
  - AGFRC weight/specs from Amazon listing; tinyPEPPER weight is unverified (marketing claim only, no number retrieved).
  - <https://www.amazon.com/AGFrc-Electronic-Controller-Control-BLHeli_S/dp/B07L6BT7GL>
- **[medium]** Whoop propeller convention: 65mm frames use 31mm props, 75mm frames use 40mm props; larger/lower-pitch props reduce disk loading and improve hover efficiency (lower RPM for the same thrust), which favors a buoyancy-assisted vehicle needing sustained gentle thrust over punch; 4-blade props give more low-throttle stability/'grip', 3-blade are marginally more efficient and quieter; Gemfan 1219x3 (31mm) and HQ Ultralight 31mm X3 were rated among the best efficiency/weight tradeoffs in independent prop shootouts
  - From Oscar Liang's independently-run prop shootout and multiple vendor pages; disk-loading-vs-efficiency relationship is standard aerodynamics, confirmed by search summary referencing Wikipedia's disk-loading article, but not from a builder testing it on an actual blimp specifically.
  - <https://oscarliang.com/tiny-whoop-prop/>
- **[medium]** LW-PLA (chemically-foaming PLA filament, e.g. ColorFabb/Polymaker/Spectrum) foams roughly 3x in volume when nozzle temp exceeds ~230C, cutting printed-part weight by an estimated 20-50% (vendor claims up to 3x reduction), and can be printed at lower infill (10-20%) than standard PLA for the same wall strength; explicitly marketed for drone/model-aircraft structures
  - Aggregated from filament manufacturer marketing pages (ColorFabb, Polymaker, Spectrum, JLC3DP); no independent third-party mass-reduction measurement was retrieved in this pass.
  - <https://colorfabb.com/3d-printing-lightweight-pla-parts>
- **[high]** Standard airship stability principle: a lighter-than-air vehicle is passively stable when its center of gravity sits below its center of buoyancy, behaving like a pendulum with a restoring torque proportional to tilt angle -- but this same mechanism produces a slow pendulum oscillation ('swing') that active control must damp rather than fight
  - Well-established naval-architecture/aerostatics principle (metacentric-height analog for LTA vehicles), and independently the explicit subject of the GT-MAB thesis's 'swing oscillation' control research plus a follow-on paper 'Modeling and Control of Swing Oscillation of Underactuated Indoor Miniature Autonomous Blimps'.
  - <https://chet-aero.com/2017/02/01/buoyancy-and-stability-an-introduction/>
- **[medium]** Foil/mylar balloons are preferred over latex for indoor blimp envelopes because they retain helium for weeks (refillable) vs. latex's 1-2 day leak rate, at the cost of extra envelope mass; both MochiSwarm and Spinning Blimp use mylar/foil spherical or oblate-spheroid envelopes and attach the gondola via elastic strings (not tape or velcro) in the two build reports actually retrieved
  - Envelope-material tradeoff confirmed by an independent open-source airship paper (3DPrint.com/Hackaday coverage of an IEEE robotic airship) as well as the two Lehigh arXiv papers; velcro/adhesive attachment claims from patent literature (airship industry) rather than from a hobbyist indoor-blimp source specifically, so treat velcro/adhesive-safe-for-mylar guidance as inferred/lower confidence versus the elastic-string method which is directly confirmed twice.
  - <https://hackaday.com/2020/07/22/lets-take-a-closer-look-at-this-robotic-airship/>

### Gotchas

- The professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCBs were very likely designed around the same SI230x-MOSFET + coreless-brushed-motor pattern seen in ESP-FLY and the CircuitDigest article (both explicitly in his reference material). If so, those boards CANNOT drive brushless motors without adding an ESC or redesigning the driver stage -- this should be checked against the actual PCB schematic/gerbers before choosing a motor class for the blimp.
- Brushed coreless motors have markedly worse speed-up/slow-down response than brushless (explicitly called out by the Spinning Blimp authors), which hurts fine attitude control; if the professor's PCB is brushed-only, expect to compensate in firmware with bang-bang or heavily-filtered control rather than expecting crisp linear throttle response.
- A blimp's C.G.-below-C.B. passive stability is a double-edged sword: it gives 'for free' stability against tipping, but it also creates a pendulum oscillation mode that a naive PID can fight rather than damp, actively exciting a swing at the natural pendulum frequency -- this is literally the GT-MAB thesis's research topic, so expect this to be a real, previously-documented failure mode, not a hypothetical.
- Neutral buoyancy is not a static, solved problem: an internet debate on the Arduino forum specifically flags that temperature swings alone will make a helium blimp rise or sink over the course of an event, meaning some ballast/trim mechanism (or accepting a controlled up/down thrust budget) is needed even for an otherwise-perfectly-balanced envelope -- don't rely purely on 'we filled it to neutral' the morning of competition.
- 3-motor / L293-or-L298-class 'off the shelf' motor driver boards are a documented anti-pattern for 1S LiPo (3.7V) blimp electronics: L293/L298 datasheets specify a ~5V logic supply, so they are the wrong choice at blimp-typical single-cell voltages -- a discrete MOSFET driver, DRV8833, or similar low-voltage-capable IC is needed instead.
- Foam-core (laser-cut) construction, not 3D printing, is what both directly-verified recent academic blimp builds (MochiSwarm, Spinning Blimp) actually use for the gondola/frame -- if the team defaults straight to 3D-printed gondolas without checking real achieved weights, they may end up heavier than the state-of-the-art references for the same rigidity.
- Vectoring-thruster mechanisms retrofitted with cheap hardware (e.g., the DIYDrones Lego-Technic vectoring hack) hit hard mechanical limits fast -- one documented build only achieved ~100 degrees of tilt travel with its first gear ratio and had to redesign; budget iteration time for the tilt/servo linkage, don't assume the first geometry works.
- Independent, uncoupled vectoring of two side thrusters can cancel each other out vertically during sharp turns if not coordinated in firmware -- flagged as an open, partially-unsolved issue even in a working vectoring-blimp build.

### Actionable

- Before choosing a motor class, physically inspect/trace the professor's existing ESP32-S3 PCB to determine whether its motor-drive stage is discrete MOSFET (brushed-coreless-only) or has provision for ESC signaling (brushless-capable). This single fact determines whether brushless micro motors (0703/1102/1103 + sub-gram ESC) are even an option, or whether the design is locked into coreless brushed pager motors.
- If staying brushed-coreless (most likely given the reference material lineage), replicate the proven driver cell from Max Imagination's guide/CircuitDigest/ESP-FLY: SI2300-class N-MOSFET + 1N4148 flyback diode + ~10kOhm gate pulldown per channel, driven directly from 3.3V ESP32-S3 GPIO PWM -- this is a field-proven, near-zero-BOM-cost design already validated at this exact voltage/logic level by 3 independent builds.
- Adopt a 2-motor differential/vectored 'bicopter' topology (MochiSwarm-style: 1 microservo + 1 motor per side) or a 3-motor topology (Blimpduino-style: 2 differential side + 1 fixed vertical) rather than a 4-motor quad layout -- every real blimp reference converged on 2-3 actuators, which also directly saves gondola weight and driver-channel count versus a quadcopter-derived 4-motor design.
- Prefer 55mm-class (or larger, e.g. 40mm+ whoop-class) slow propellers over small high-pitch props for the lift/cruise motors, since lower disk loading directly improves hover efficiency -- important given the whole point of the blimp is endurance, not punch-out acceleration.
- Build (or at least prototype) the gondola as laser-cut foam-core board with friction-lock notch joints before defaulting to 3D printing -- it is what both current academic reference designs (MochiSwarm 18.7g, Spinning Blimp) actually use, is lighter and faster to iterate than most FDM parts, and the team's stated hardware/soldering skill set does not preclude simple laser-cut or hand-cut foam-board construction. If 3D printing is still preferred (e.g., for complex vectoring-servo mounts), use LW-PLA at 10-20% infill rather than standard PLA/PETG.
- Attach the gondola to the envelope with elastic strings (both directly-verified recent builds use this method), not tape/velcro, to avoid stressing or puncturing the mylar film and to allow the gondola to swing/absorb shock.
- Explicitly design and test a swing-oscillation damping term in the control loop from the start (not as an afterthought) -- this is the single most-cited unsolved/hard problem across the academic blimp literature retrieved (GT-MAB's whole thesis, a dedicated follow-on journal paper, and implicit in MochiSwarm/Spinning Blimp's designs).
- Plan for a ballast/trim strategy (even simple: a small movable weight or a way to bleed/add helium) since multiple sources agree neutral buoyancy drifts with ambient temperature over the course of an event -- do not assume 'balanced at 9am' will hold by competition time.
- If pursuing brushless for higher thrust/efficiency despite the driver-stage question, the AGFRC Mini 4A (0.5g, 1S, BLHeli_S/DShot) is the lightest verified commercial ESC found and pairs naturally with 0703-class motors (~1.7-1.9g each) -- but this adds firmware complexity (DShot protocol) versus the simple PWM-to-MOSFET path the existing PCB likely already supports.

### Open questions

- Does the professor's fabricated ESP32-S3 PCB drive motors via discrete MOSFETs (brushed-only) or does it expose an ESC-compatible signal path (brushless-capable)? This gates almost every downstream propulsion decision and could not be determined from web research alone -- needs physical/schematic inspection.
- What is the professor's actual target AUW (all-up weight) and available net buoyancy (balloon size/type he has or plans to use)? None of the source material for this task specified his intended balloon, so all gram-budget recommendations above are relative to reference designs (MochiSwarm ~120g net buoyancy with an 18.7g gondola; Spinning Blimp only -5g margin at 240g AUW), not his actual constraints.
- Is a fixed-vertical/2-side-differential (Blimpduino-style, 3 motors) or a tilt-vectored bicopter (MochiSwarm-style, 2 motors + 2 servos) preferred, given the competition's likely obstacle/goal-chasing requirements (per computed task, 'chase a goal')? This depends on rules not covered in this propulsion/mechanical research pass (delegated to another research thread on competition rules).
- What exact numbers back the SpICED Coanda-blimp design (mass, thrust, duct diameter)? The MDPI source paper 403'd; the numbers currently attached to it (1166.7g, 5428g thrust) look implausible for a hand-launched safe indoor blimp and may belong to a different paper -- needs a direct read of the actual PDF (e.g., via ResearchGate or a library proxy) before being trusted or used.
- What does the GT-MAB thesis (Georgia Tech, full text) specify for exact motor/propeller part numbers and gram-level mass budget across its 5 thrusters? The primary PDF blocked WebFetch (403); only abstract-level synthesis was obtained.
- Are there specific pinout/part-number details in the LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm docs 'Bicopter robot: Parts and assembly' page that would give an exact motor/servo BOM matching the actual Y5i3LntcNt0 video build? The docs index page was reachable but its assembly sub-page 404'd/403'd on the paths tried.

- Full Instructables article bodies for 'Build the Smallest ESP32 Drone...' and 'Guide to Making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers...' -- WebFetch returned only page titles/headers (likely JS-rendered content not captured); technical details for these were instead recovered from an independent DFRobot community mirror of the MOSFET-driver guide and the GitHub README for ESP-FLY.
- Georgia Tech GT-MAB full thesis PDF (https://repository.gatech.edu/server/api/core/bitstreams/d79126c8-4dc9-451f-9462-70f630e1d352/content) -- HTTP 403 Forbidden on WebFetch.
- LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm docs/hardware/bicopter_parts_assembly.md and docs/index.md hardware detail -- returned 404/403 or only navigation links, not the actual BOM content.
