- Exact PWM switching frequency recommended for the MOSFET brushed-motor drivers in Max Imagination's guide specifically (a general 20-25kHz 'silent' recommendation was found from an unrelated generic motor-driver source, not confirmed as this project's actual chosen frequency).
- Exact commercial sub-1-gram servo part number/datasheet (only a generic 0.45g magnetic-actuator product description and a 0.515g 'smallest commercially available servo' claim were found, without a resolvable manufacturer part number).

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — The 3 reference videos are V_mZsiZcy7s = 'Build the Smallest ESP32 Drone...' (ESP-FLY) by Max Imagination; xW2Nwg_RX84 = 'Guide to Making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers...' by Max Imagination; Y5i3LntcNt0 = 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone' by Lehigh Swarms Club
  - Correction: Independently re-pulled all 3 oEmbed records; titles/authors match exactly (video 3's author channel is @LehighSwarmsClub).
- **CONFIRMED** — ESP-FLY hardware: 4x SI2300 N-MOSFET, 6x15mm coreless motors ~70,000RPM/~17g thrust each, 8x30mm tri-blade props (4CW/4CCW), 250mAh 1S LiPo, AUW ~18g bare/~25g w/battery/~28g w/FPV cam, 67x67x31mm, ~5min flight
  - Correction: All figures reproduced independently from the GitHub README match the original researcher's numbers essentially verbatim (RPM, thrust/motor, weights, dimensions, prop count/CW-CCW split, battery, MOSFET part number).
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — CircuitDigest DIY-WiFi drone: 4x SI2302 N-MOSFET + 1N4148 flyback diode, 720-class coreless motors, 55mm CW/CCW props, ESP32 WROOM+MPU6050, TP4056 charger, MIC5219 3.3V LDO, 1300mAh 30C 1S LiPo, ~60g total, 5-7min flight
  - Correction: Core numbers confirmed (SI2302 x4, 720-class motors, 55mm props types A/B, ESP32 WROOM, MPU6050, TP4056, MIC5219-3.3YM5, 1300mAh 30C 1S, ~60g, 5-7min). BUT the article's actual diode BOM is more granular than stated: there is a dedicated SS34 Schottky diode (x1, likely battery/reverse-protection) separate from the 1N4148 (x4, one per motor channel) used as the flyback/protection diodes. Also lists AO3401 P-MOSFET (x1, power-path) and 2N7002DW (x1, auto-reset) not mentioned in the original summary. Gate-pulldown resistor value is still not stated in article text (matches original's own open-question flag).
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — MochiSwarm: 50-inch spherical foil balloon, 120g net buoyancy, 18.7g laser-cut foam-core gondola, XIAO ESP32S3 (240MHz) at 200Hz control loop, ESP-NOW 480m range/512kbps, 50Hz servo PWM, unit cost ~$132.74
  - Correction: All the hardware/network/frame figures reproduce exactly from the arXiv HTML (balloon 50in, 120g buoyancy, 18.7g frame, XIAO ESP32S3, 200Hz loop, ESP-NOW 480m/512kbps, 50Hz actuator PWM). BUT the $132.74 unit cost is the BASE robot cost only — the paper adds a separate $115 NiclaVision perception module per unit, for a full equipped-unit cost of ≈$247.74. The original summary states $132.74 as if it were the whole unit cost without flagging the camera add-on.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Spinning Blimp (Lehigh, arXiv 2503.04112): 2 fixed brushed motors, Gemfan 45mm props, up to 15g static thrust each, foam-core cage + balsa axle, mylar envelope 0.081 m^3, 60g buoyant lift, ~240g total mass with only -5g net buoyancy margin, XIAO ESP32C3, GY-91/BNO055 IMU + BMP280 baro, L298N driver, ~$20 total cost
  - Correction: Confirmed independently: Gemfan 45mm props ($0.74), up to 15g static thrust/motor, mylar oblate-spheroid envelope 0.081 m^3, 60g helium lift, -5g net buoyancy, XIAO ESP32C3, GY-91/BNO055 + BMP280 + VL53L1X sensors, WWZMDiB L298N 2-channel driver, one-cell 3.7V 750mAh LiPo, ~$20 build cost. Could NOT independently re-confirm the specific '~240g total mass' figure — the arXiv PDF text extraction did not yield a single clean AUW number in this pass, so treat 240g as still only originally-sourced, not independently re-verified.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — (Gotcha) L293/L298-class motor driver boards are a 'documented anti-pattern' / 'wrong choice' for 1S LiPo (3.7V) blimp electronics because their datasheets specify ~5V logic supply
  - Correction: The 4.5V minimum logic-supply voltage for the L298 is real and independently confirmed (datasheet-derived summary). However, calling it an outright 'wrong choice' at 1S is contradicted by practice: the peer-reviewed Spinning Blimp build (Lehigh, arXiv 2503.04112) explicitly runs a 'WWZMDiB L298N 2-channel DC motor driver' directly from a one-cell 3.7V 750mAh LiPo. The chip's onboard 5V regulator (when its jumper is in place) is only documented as failing above ~12V input, not below any particular minimum — so in practice hobbyist L298N modules are commonly run below their 'spec' logic voltage with degraded but usable output, or with logic fed separately from the ESP32's own 3.3V rail. Recommendation should be softened from 'wrong choice/anti-pattern' to 'suboptimal / bigger and heavier than a discrete MOSFET or DRV8833 solution, and technically under-driven at 1S voltages, but not something that categorically cannot work' — a real academic team shipped exactly this combination.
- **CONFIRMED** — DRV8833 runs down to 2.7V logic/motor supply, so ESP32's 3.3V GPIO drives it directly with no level shifting
  - Correction: Adafruit's DRV8833 breakout product page states directly: 'Runs at 2.7V-10.8V logic/motor power.' Exact decoupling-cap values (10uF/2.2uF/0.1uF) claimed in the original could not be independently re-confirmed from the product page text (it just points to the full datasheet/tutorial), so those specific capacitor values remain only as originally sourced, not independently re-verified.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Whoop propeller convention: 65mm frames use 31mm props, 75mm frames use 40mm props
  - Correction: 65mm->31mm is well supported (Oscar Liang's 31mm prop shootout for tiny whoops, Gemfan 1219x3 = 31mm, winner at 20.0g max thrust/1.78g/W). But '75mm frame -> 40mm prop' is not a fixed rule: BetaFPV's actual 75mm-class quads split — Beta75X (2S, 1103 11000KV motors) uses 40mm 4-blade props (confirms the claim for that model), while Meteor75 Pro (1S, 1102 21000KV motors, marketed as 'the largest 1S whoop') uses 45mm props. So 75mm-class whoops range 40-45mm depending on motor/power tier, not a single fixed convention.
- **CONFIRMED** — Micro brushless motor weights: 0703 ~1.7-1.9g (T-Motor M0703, 19000KV variant), 1103 ~3.2g (11000KV for 2S Beta75X)
  - Correction: T-Motor M0703 19000KV product page confirms '1.7g (Including Cable)' and 19000KV exactly as claimed. Beta75X 2S product page confirms '1103 11000KV with connector' motors on a 75mm 2S frame, matching the model/KV pairing claimed (this listing did not itself restate the 3.2g figure, but model+KV match).
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — LW-PLA foams ~3x in volume at ~230C nozzle temp, cutting printed part weight by an estimated 20-50% (vendor claims up to 3x)
  - Correction: The ~3x volume expansion at ~230C is confirmed verbatim from ColorFabb's own page ('At around 230C this material will start foaming, increasing its volume by nearly 3 times'). But the weight-reduction figure is UNDERSTATED in the original: ColorFabb's own marketing claims up to a 65% weight reduction via reduced material flow ('Users can realize a 65% weight reduction compared to regular PLA'), not '20-50%.' Correction: cite up to ~65% (vendor claim), not 20-50%.
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — GT-MAB (Georgia Tech Miniature Autonomous Blimp) uses full holonomic 5-thruster actuation (2 X-axis, 2 Z-axis, 1 Y-axis) on a finless saucer envelope; primary thesis research focus is swing-oscillation damping
  - Correction: Could not independently confirm the specific 5-thruster/axis breakdown from any primary source in this pass: the original evidence_url (repository.gatech.edu bitstream) still 403's; smartech.gatech.edu (the GT-MAB handle page) failed outright with DNS resolution error (likely fully decommissioned/migrated, not just paywalled); a targeted arXiv API search for GT-MAB/swing-oscillation papers did not surface the thesis or its companion journal paper text. Independently CONFIRMED that GT-MAB is a real, actively-cited robotics platform: a 2023 arXiv swarm-robotics paper explicitly uses 'simulations of the Georgia Tech Miniature Autonomous Blimps (GT-MABs)' in CoppeliaSim, corroborating the platform's existence and academic use, but not the thruster count. Keep the 5-thruster claim as originally flagged (medium confidence / unverified) rather than upgrading it.
- **UNVERIFIABLE** — SpICED (Spherical Indoor Coanda Effect Drone) weight/thrust figures of 1166.7g total mass and 5428g total thrust may belong to a different, non-blimp Coanda-UAV paper found in the same search
  - Correction: Could not resolve either way. Independently confirmed the DOI 10.3390/drones6090260 redirects to the exact same MDPI URL originally cited (https://www.mdpi.com/2504-446X/6/9/260), so the citation itself is not obviously a wrong link. However every retrieval path attempted (direct fetch, DOI redirect, /htm variant) returned HTTP 403, so the actual title/abstract/figures could not be read in this pass either. The original researcher's own caution (do not trust these numbers until the real PDF is read) stands and should NOT be treated as resolved -- if anything, my independent 403s reinforce that this needs a library-proxy or institutional-access read before any BOM/mass-budget decision cites it.

### Added by fact-checker

- github.com/espressif/esp-drone (one of the two repos the professor explicitly referenced) explicitly documents support for ESP32, ESP32-S2, AND ESP32-S3 under ESP-IDF release/v5.0 -- directly relevant since the professor's PCBs are ESP32-S3-based. It is 'ported from the Crazyflie open source project (GPL3.0)' and supports Stabilize/Height-hold/Position-hold modes plus app/gamepad/ESP-BOX3 control. Source: https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone (fetched directly).
- github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim (the other repo the professor referenced) is a MuJoCo-based SIMULATOR ('Simulation for the MochiSwarm platform', forked from vincentzouras, ships model files like mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml), NOT the physical robot's flight firmware. The original research's summary implies a direct hardware connection but doesn't clearly flag that this specific repo is sim-only -- useful to tell the team explicitly so they don't expect ESP32 firmware/PCB integration code in it. Source: https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim.
- The GT-MAB primary source situation is worse than 'paywalled': the smartech.gatech.edu handle URL the original researcher cited for GT-MAB now fails with an outright DNS resolution error in this pass, consistent with Georgia Tech having migrated/decommissioned its old SMARTech repository platform. Anyone chasing this thesis later should search repository.gatech.edu's newer interface or Google Scholar for the current canonical link rather than reusing the old smartech.gatech.edu URL, which appears dead, not just access-restricted.
- CircuitDigest's actual BOM has two more parts than the original summary listed: an AO3401 P-MOSFET (x1, power-path control) and a 2N7002DW dual N-MOSFET (x1, auto-reset circuit), in addition to the 4x SI2302 motor MOSFETs, 4x 1N4148, and 1x SS34 diode. Worth having on hand if the team is comparing the professor's existing PCB against this reference schematic component-by-component. Source: https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone.

---

## electronics (domain)  
**RELIABILITY: high**

Domain: ESP32-S3 as a blimp flight controller. Confirmed a direct lineage for the professor's reference material: the three YouTube links (via noembed metadata, since YouTube itself resists WebFetch) are (1) 'Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY' by Max Imagination -> Seeed Studio Co-Create ESP-FLY kit (XIAO ESP32-S3 + MPU6050 + 4x coreless motors, github.com/Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY), (2) 'Guide to making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers with MOSFETs for RC Projects' (same author, the motor-driver design used on ESP-FLY), and (3) 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone' by Lehigh Swarms/Blimps Club — i.e. someone literally re-housed the ESP-FLY flight controller in a balloon envelope. Lehigh's Aerial Swarms Club (Prof. David Saldana, AIRLab/SwarmsLab) is also the academic home of the MochiSwarm platform (arXiv 2503.03077, ICRA/IROS 2025), which is functionally the same lineage and gives concrete, citable numbers: XIAO ESP32-S3 MCU, 200 Hz control loop, 50 Hz PWM, differential-drive 'bicopter' actuation (2 stacks of microservo + 1-cell brushless rotor), ESP-NOW link (up to 480 m, 512 kbps), 9-DoF IMU+magnetometer over I2C, barometer for altitude, detachable NiclaVision perception board doing color/blob balloon detection at 10 Hz, ~120 g net buoyancy balloon, 18.7 g laser-cut foam-core frame, unit cost ~$132.74 (+$115 for vision). This lineage should be treated as the primary real-world precedent, more relevant than generic quadcopter guides. The competition is confirmed as 'International Airship Racing' (IAR) held jointly with LARC/LARS 2026 at Universidad Militar Nueva Granada, Bogota, Nov 4-6 2026 - an autonomous lighter-than-air racing category ('constrained sensing, actuation, payload'), organized with Oakland University (D'Antonio) and Lehigh (Saldana). A 'Blimp sample' page and an 'IAR_LARC2026.pdf' rules document exist on the IAR site but their content (arena size, weight limits, exact scoring) could not be extracted via WebFetch (JS-rendered Google Sites; PDF link not resolvable) - a hard gap to flag to the professor. On electronics: ESP32-S3 (dual Xtensa LX7 @240MHz, WROOM-1/-1U up to N16R16V, WROOM-2 octal-SPI high-memory, MINI-1 capped at 2MB PSRAM) supports camera via its parallel/LCD-camera peripheral (OV2640/OV3660/OV5640 all supported by espressif/esp32-camera driver; OV5640 needs PSRAM). ESP-WHO/ESP-DL face detection on ESP32-S3 realistically runs ~12-15 FPS (some reports 15-18 FPS optimized) using the LX7's 128-bit SIMD vector instructions (~18x speedup INT8); full YOLO-class 30 FPS multi-class detection is explicitly NOT feasible on this MCU class per multiple sources. The single biggest electrical risk is the well-documented ESP32/ESP32-S3 WiFi-TX brownout: TX current spikes to ~180-355 mA can sag a small 1S LiPo below the ~2.43V brownout threshold; cure is bulk capacitance (100-1000uF low-ESR) plus 100nF ceramic at the module's power pins, adequate battery C-rating. Sensor recommendations with I2C addresses: IMU - ICM-42688-P (0x68/0x69, up to 1MHz I2C/24MHz SPI) or BMI270 for 6-axis; add a magnetometer since blimps have weak/slow yaw authority and very long flight durations so gyro-drift accumulates uncorrected far longer than on a quad - LSM6DSOX has NO magnetometer (pair with LIS3MDL), LSM9DS1 integrates one, MPU9250's AK8963 mag sits at its own address 0x0C. Barometers BMP390/DPS310/MS5611 all share the 0x76/0x77 address pair - BMP390 noise floor ~0.085 Pa but real accuracy only ~+/-0.25m, too coarse alone for indoor blimp-scale altitude changes, should fuse with VL53L1X/VL53L5CX ToF (used on esp-drone's own extension board) and/or PMW3901 optical flow. UWB (DW1000/DW3000, e.g. Makerfabs MaUWB_ESP32S3 $46.58, +/-10cm) is the option for indoor absolute position. Power: 1S LiPo 300mAh weighs ~7.7-9g, JST-PH2.0 standard; charging via TP4056 (1A) or MCP73831 (500mA, tiny SOT-23); battery monitoring via 100k/100k divider with 100nF filter (ESP32-S3 ADC has nonlinearity above ~2.75V, needs calibration). PCB practice: 0.6-0.8mm FR4 saves weight vs 1.6mm standard; WROOM antenna keep-out needs >=15mm copper-free on all layers; castellated modules trade a little size for pre-certified RF vs a bare-chip design. Given the professor's board already exists, a concrete ~15-item checklist is provided to send him.

|---|---|
| ESP32-S3 CPU | Dual-core Xtensa LX7 @ up to 240MHz, 128-bit SIMD vector extensions for INT8 NN ops |
| ESP32-S3-WROOM-1/-1U memory variants | N4R8 (4MB flash/8MB PSRAM), N8R8 (8/8), N16R16VA (16/16); WROOM-2 up to N32R16V (octal-SPI); MINI-1 max 4MB flash/2MB PSRAM or 8MB flash/0 PSRAM |
| ESP32-S3-WROOM-1U WiFi TX current | 285-355 mA at 20.5 dBm max power (100% duty cycle rating) |
| ESP32-S3 brownout minimum safe threshold | ~2.43 V (2.3 V absolute min digital VDD + 0.13 V headroom) |
| ESP32-S3 deep sleep current | ~7 uA (RTC on) / ~1 uA (RTC off) at chip pins per Espressif; real dev boards 0.01-9.4 mA |
| ESP-WHO/ESP-DL face detection FPS on ESP32-S3 | ~12-15 FPS (up to ~15-18 FPS with optimized ESP-DL kernels), camera streams ~30fps concurrently |
| ICM-42688-P I2C address / bus speed | 0x68 (AD0 low) / 0x69 (AD0 high); I2C up to 1MHz, SPI up to 24-25MHz; noise 2.8 mdps/sqrt(Hz) |

| BMP390 noise / accuracy | Noise floor 0.085 Pa (~7mm altitude step); relative accuracy ~3 Pa (~+/-0.25 m) |
| VL53L1X range/rate | Up to 4 m (long mode), up to 50 Hz; short mode ~1.3 m ambient-light-immune |
| VL53L5CX range/rate | 8x8 multizone, up to 4 m (400 cm), up to 60 Hz |
| PMW3901 range | ~80 mm to 'infinity' (validated ~6 ft indoors) |

| Battery voltage divider | 100kOhm/100kOhm (or 100k/10k) into ADC pin, 100nF filter cap, calibrate at 4.2V and 3.0V |

### Key findings

- **[high]** The three reference YouTube videos are, in order: the Seeed 'ESP-FLY' smallest-ESP32-drone build (Max Imagination), a companion video on tiny brushed MOSFET motor drivers (same channel), and a Lehigh Swarms/Blimps Club video converting the ESP-FLY drone into a blimp.
  - Retrieved via noembed.com oEmbed metadata (title/author) since direct YouTube WebFetch is blocked (returns only nav boilerplate). Titles: 'Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY' (Max Imagination), 'Guide to making TINY Brushed Motor Drivers with MOSFETs for RC Projects' (Max Imagination), 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone' (author_name 'Lehigh Swarms Club', channel @LehighBlimpsClub).
  - <https://noembed.com/embed?url=https://youtu.be/Y5i3LntcNt0>
- **[high]** ESP-FLY (Seeed Co-Create kit) hardware: XIAO ESP32-S3 (dual Xtensa LX7 @240MHz, 8MB flash/8MB PSRAM), MPU6050 IMU, 4x custom SI2300 N-MOSFET brushed motor drivers on a 4-layer PCB, 4x 6x15mm coreless motors (~70,000RPM, ~17g thrust each), 8x 30mm tri-blade props, 1S 3.7V 250mAh LiPo (JST-PH2), 3D-printed 50mm frame (~4g); total mass ~18g bare/~25g with battery/~28g with FPV cam; runs ESP-Drone firmware or rtlopez ESP-FC/Betaflight.
  - <https://github.com/Seeed-Projects/Co-Create_ESP-FLY>
- **[high]** MochiSwarm (Lehigh AIRLab/SwarmsLab, David Saldana) is the closest real academic precedent: XIAO ESP32-S3 MCU (22x17x4mm), 200Hz control loop, 50Hz PWM output, 9-DoF IMU+magnetometer over I2C plus a barometer for altitude, ESP-NOW comms (up to 480m, 512kbps), detachable NiclaVision board doing onboard color/LAB-Mahalanobis balloon detection + IR-diff goal detection at 10Hz, differential-drive 'bicopter' actuation (2x microservo + 1-cell brushless rotor per stack), balloon net buoyancy 120g, laser-cut foam-core frame 18.7g, unit cost $132.74 (+$115 vision) - demonstrated with up to 12 blimps doing autonomous pickup-and-delivery.
  - <https://arxiv.org/html/2503.03077v1>
- **[high]** The competition is 'International Airship Racing' (IAR), co-located with LARC/LARS 2026 (26th LARC/23rd LARS) at Universidad Militar Nueva Granada, Bogota, Nov 4-6, 2026, organized jointly with Oakland University (Diego D'Antonio) and Lehigh (David Saldana). Goal per site: 'advance aerial robotics through autonomous LTA racing under real-world constraints in control, perception, and flight' with 'constrained sensing, actuation, and payload limitations.'
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/home>
- **[unverified]** Could NOT retrieve the actual IAR rules text (arena size, weight/size limits, scoring, race format) - Rules page references 'IAR_LARC2026.pdf' via an embedded Google Drive viewer with no direct downloadable href extractable; 'Blimp sample' page is images-only (Airship sample, Assembly, Hardware) which text extraction cannot read.
  - Genuine gap - team should open the Rules page in a real browser and download the embedded Drive file directly.
- **[high]** esp-drone (official Espressif) hardware baseline: ESP32-S2-WROVER or ESP32-WROOM-32D + MPU6050 mainboard; extension boards add PMW3901+VL53L1X (position-hold), MS5611 (pressure/altitude), HMC5883 (compass); 716/720-class brushed motors; 300-350mAh 1S LiPo.
  - <https://docs.espressif.com/projects/espressif-esp-drone/en/latest/hardware.html>
- **[high]** circuitdigest DIY WiFi drone (material the professor sent) BOM: bare ESP32 WROOM, MPU6050 (I2C GPIO21/22, clone chips fail at 400kHz), CP2102N USB-UART, TP4056 (1A)+MIC5219 3.3V LDO (500mA, 500mV dropout) with 2N7002DW auto-reset MOSFETs, SI2302 N-MOSFETs per motor with flyback diodes, 4x 720 coreless motors/55mm props X-config, 1300mAh 30C 1S LiPo via USB-C, single custom PCB doubling as frame, 4 status LEDs, ESP-Drone firmware (ESP-IDF 4.4.5) modified; flight time ~5-7min.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone>
- **[high]** ESP32-S3 module family: WROOM-1/-1U (QuadSPI flash, Quad/Octal SPI PSRAM; N4R8, N8R8, N16R16VA etc.), WROOM-2 (all-Octal-SPI, e.g. N32R16V, faster memory), MINI-1 (capped at 2MB PSRAM: 8MB flash/0 PSRAM or 4MB flash/2MB PSRAM) - relevant to how much PSRAM the professor's board actually has for any vision work.
  - <https://documentation.espressif.com/esp32-s3-wroom-1_wroom-1u_datasheet_en.html>
- **[high]** ESP32-S3-EYE reference board: WROOM-1 module, 2MP camera+LCD+mic 2-board stack, 8MB Octal PSRAM/8MB flash, independent power rails for camera vs rest of board (explicitly to cut noise) - a good layout precedent if the professor's board carries a camera.
  - <https://www.espboards.dev/esp32/esp32-s3-eye/>
- **[medium]** ESP-WHO/ESP-DL face-detection benchmark on ESP32-S3: ~12-15 FPS (one source up to 15-18 FPS with optimized kernels) enabled by LX7 128-bit SIMD (~18x INT8 speedup); camera can stream ~30fps concurrently but detection inference is the bottleneck, hence frame-skipping is standard.
  - Figures from Espressif workshop material and secondary write-ups, not one peer-reviewed benchmark table; treat 12-18 FPS as the realistic band.
  - <https://developer.espressif.com/workshops/edge-ai-with-esp32-s3/>
- **[high]** Full multi-class YOLO-style detection at real video rates is explicitly out of reach for ESP32-S3-class hardware per multiple sources; realistic on-device vision is single-class/small-model detection, matching what MochiSwarm actually does (color-blob detection, not general object detection).
  - <https://zbotic.in/esp32-s3-ai-camera-tinyml-object-detection-on-device/>
- **[high]** ESP32-S3 WiFi TX current draws ~180-355mA in bursts (WROOM-1U datasheet: 285-355mA at 20.5dBm), the classic brownout trigger on small 1S LiPo rails; fix is 100-1000uF low-ESR bulk cap + 100nF ceramic directly at module power pins, plus adequate battery C-rating.
  - <https://esp32.com/viewtopic.php?t=4908>
- **[medium]** ESP32-S3 brownout detector's minimum safe configurable threshold is ~2.43V (0.13V headroom above 2.3V absolute-minimum digital VDD spec).
  - <https://www.esp32.com/viewtopic.php?t=39249>
- **[medium]** ESP32-S3 ADC has known nonlinearity around 2.5-3V input, up to ~250mV error uncalibrated; recommend keeping divider readings in the linear range or using ESP-IDF ADC calibration, plus a 100nF bypass cap at the ADC pin.
  - <https://developer.espressif.com/blog/2025/08/adc-performance/>
- **[medium]** ESP32-S3 deep-sleep current is ~7uA (RTC on)/~1uA (RTC off) at bare chip pins per Espressif's own setup, but real dev boards show 0.01-9.4mA due to LDOs/USB bridges - use real-board numbers for power budgeting, not the datasheet figure.
  - <https://www.qoitech.com/blog/esp32-s3-c3-c6-sleep-power-consumption/>
- **[high]** IMU choice for a blimp: ICM-42688-P (I2C 0x68/0x69 via AD0, I2C up to 1MHz/SPI up to 24-25MHz, noise ~2.8 mdps/sqrt(Hz), 20-bit) or BMI270 (16-bit, ~0.9mA active, strong hardware LPF -> latency trade-off) are modern low-noise 6-axis upgrades over MPU6050.
  - <https://blog.uavmodel.com/fpv-flight-controller-gyro-comparison-mpu6000-vs-icm-42688-vs-bmi270-noise-filtering-and-flight-performance-2026-guide/>
- **[high]** Magnetometer availability: LSM6DSOX has NO onboard magnetometer (pair with separate LIS3MDL for 9-DoF); LSM9DS1 integrates accel+gyro+mag (LIS3MDL-equivalent) in one part; MPU9250's magnetometer (AK8963) is a separate die at its own fixed I2C address 0x0C behind the main 0x68/0x69 address.
  - <https://www.adafruit.com/product/4517>
- **[medium]** A magnetometer matters far more on a blimp than on a quad because a blimp has weak/slow yaw authority (differential thrust from small motors or a single steered thruster) and flies for very long durations (the whole point of the platform) - both mean gyro-integration yaw drift accumulates uncorrected far longer than on a fast, short-duration quad flight; a magnetometer gives the absolute yaw reference a Madgwick/Mahony filter needs, bounding drift to roughly 1-2 degrees under good conditions.
  - Built from retrieved yaw-drift/magnetometer mechanism sources, not a single source stating the blimp-specific comparison explicitly - this is a physics inference.
  - <https://github.com/kriswiner/MPU9250/issues/333>
- **[medium]** Barometer options BMP390 (noise floor 0.085 Pa ~7mm step, but ~+/-0.25m absolute relative accuracy), DPS310, MS5611 all typically ship at I2C 0x76 or 0x77 (address-select pin) - cannot share a bus with another 0x76/0x77 device without strapping, and barometric altitude alone (~25cm-class accuracy) is too coarse for typical indoor blimp altitude changes - fuse with ToF or optical flow.
  - <https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-bmp388-bmp390-bmp3xx/overview>
- **[high]** ToF rangefinders: VL53L1X (up to 4m long-distance mode, up to 50Hz, 1.3m ambient-light-immune short mode, not ideal outdoors/bright light - fine indoors), VL53L5CX (8x8 multizone ToF, up to 4m, up to 60Hz) - VL53L1X is the exact part used on esp-drone's own position-hold extension board.
  - <https://www.st.com/resource/en/datasheet/vl53l1x.pdf>
- **[high]** PMW3901 optical flow sensor: effective range ~80mm to 'infinity' (validated ~6ft/table-to-ceiling indoors), ~$15-34 depending on module, standard for lateral drift-correction on tiny FCs (also used on esp-drone's position-hold board).
  - <https://holybro.com/products/pmw3901-optical-flow-sensor>
- **[high]** UWB for indoor absolute position: DW1000 gives ~+/-10cm ranging/~+/-30cm 2D position (TWR/TDOA); DW3000 improves power/ranging/interference resilience over DW1000 and can do AoA with dual antennas (~120 deg FoV, ~5 deg accuracy). Makerfabs MaUWB_ESP32S3 (DW3000+ESP32-S3) costs $46.58.
  - <https://nextwaves.com/blog/the-ultimate-uwb-module-comparison-prices-specs-and-use-cases>
- **[medium]** 1S LiPo weights at PH2.0 connector class: 300mAh ~7.7-9g; 450mAh ~13.3g; 520mAh (HV) ~13.4g - a 500mAh pack is estimated ~12-14g by interpolation, not directly sourced.
  - 500mAh-specific weight not found directly; interpolated from 300/450/520mAh datapoints.
  - <https://www.amazon.com/PowerWhoop-Connector-Upgraded-Inductrix-Tinyhawk/dp/B08KXLJP45>
- **[high]** TP4056 (up to 1A, wider 8V input, ready modules often include DW01A protection) vs MCP73831 (max 500mA, SOT-23-5, 4 selectable regulation voltages 4.20/4.35/4.40/4.50V, no built-in protection) - MCP73831 fits better on a tiny embedded PCB; TP4056 better when faster charging/standalone protection matter more than board space.
  - <https://zbotic.in/tp4056-vs-mcp73831-li-ion-charging-ic-comparison/>
- **[high]** PCB practice for <10g flight controller: 0.6-0.8mm FR4 (vs 1.6mm standard) common for weight-optimized FCs at the cost of more flex; WROOM-class antenna keep-out needs >=15mm copper-free on all layers or antenna detunes 100-200MHz; ground plane must stay solid near antenna/RF trace with 135deg/arc bends and via-stitching; castellated modules (WROOM/MINI) trade minor size/cost for pre-certified RF and easy soldering vs a bare-chip design needing in-house antenna tuning/certification.
  - <https://www.nextpcb.com/blog/esp32-antenna-design-guide>
- **[high]** Battery monitoring: standard 100k/100k (or 100k/10k) divider into ADC pin with 100nF filter cap, converted to voltage in firmware and ideally two-point calibrated (4.2V, 3.0V) given ESP32-S3 ADC nonlinearity.
  - <https://how2electronics.com/iot-based-battery-soc-monitoring-system-with-esp32/>
- **[high]** Motor/servo PWM peripherals on ESP32-S3: MCPWM (2 units, purpose-built for BLDC/brushed/servo, dead-time generation, hall feedback), LEDC (general PWM, commonly used for simple brushed-motor duty-cycle via MOSFETs as in ESP-FLY/circuitdigest), RMT (usable for precise pulse generation) - all documented ESP-IDF peripherals appropriate for a blimp gondola's motors/servos.
  - <https://docs.espressif.com/projects/esp-idf/en/stable/esp32s3/api-reference/peripherals/mcpwm.html>
- **[medium]** Blimps achieve dramatically longer endurance than quadcopters on comparable batteries: a hybrid quad-blimp build achieved 'well over an hour' vs 'maybe less than ten minutes' for a comparable pure multirotor, because buoyancy means motors only provide steering/trim thrust rather than continuous full-weight lift.
  - <https://hackaday.com/2026/07/23/hackaday-europe-2026-half-quad-half-blimp-test-fly-survive/>

### Gotchas

- YouTube pages resist WebFetch entirely (confirmed both watch-page fetch attempts returned only footer/nav boilerplate, no description text). Only noembed.com oEmbed metadata (title+author) was retrievable - enough to identify the videos but not their full descriptions, parts lists, or comments. The team should manually open the three links in a browser and copy the description text/pinned comments, especially for the Lehigh 'ESP Blimp' video which likely has a parts list.
- The IAR rules PDF ('IAR_LARC2026.pdf') and the 'Blimp sample' page (images: 'Airship sample', 'Assembly', 'Hardware') exist but are NOT extractable via WebFetch - Google Sites renders them as embedded Drive viewers/images with no plain-text/HTML href captured. This is the single most important document currently missing (arena size, weight/size limits, scoring, race format). Someone needs to open the site in a real browser and download the PDF directly.
- ESP32-S3 WiFi-TX brownout is not hypothetical here - a tiny 1S LiPo (necessary for weight) sags more under the same ~300mA TX burst that a bigger battery shrugs off. If the professor's existing PCB doesn't already have generous bulk capacitance at the ESP32-S3 power pins (not just on a separate regulator board), expect random resets during WiFi-heavy operation (video streaming, telemetry bursts) - priority #1 to verify on his board.
- BMP390/DPS310/MS5611 all default to the same two possible I2C addresses (0x76/0x77) as several other common breakout boards (e.g., BME280 clones) - if the professor's board already has an I2C device at one of those addresses, adding a barometer needs the address-select pin checked/strapped or a second I2C bus.
- Barometric altitude alone (~25cm-class real-world accuracy even on BMP390) is coarser than the altitude changes that will actually matter in an indoor arena; don't rely on it as the sole altitude sensor - fuse with ToF (VL53L1X/L5CX) as esp-drone's own extension board already does.
- ESP32-S3's realistic on-device vision ceiling is single-purpose small models (color/blob detection, simple classifiers) at low-teens FPS, not general multi-class YOLO at video rate. If competition scoring depends on visually tracking an opposing blimp or a goal, plan for a MochiSwarm-style color/IR-blob detector rather than a full object detector, and verify the professor's board actually has PSRAM populated (MINI-1 variants may have none or only 2MB, limiting frame buffer size/resolution).
- 1S LiPo weight for capacities beyond what was directly found (500mAh specifically) was interpolated, not sourced - verify the exact battery he intends to use and its actual weight before final mass budgeting.
- The MochiSwarm paper's balloon dimension text extracted oddly as '5050-inch diameter' - this reads like an OCR/extraction artifact from the HTML-rendering pipeline and should NOT be treated as a real spec; the actual envelope size needs re-verification directly against the PDF (arxiv.org/pdf/2503.03077) or the IEEE version, not the HTML mirror used here.
- Castellated module (WROOM/MINI) vs bare ESP32-S3 chip: if the professor's PCB uses a bare (non-module) ESP32-S3 chip, he also had to design/tune the antenna and handle RF compliance himself - worth explicitly asking, since this changes both the debugging checklist (antenna keep-out violations become a live suspect for poor WiFi range) and whether re-spinning the board is realistic before the competition.

### Actionable

- Send the professor the checklist below about his existing PCB before doing any redesign work - most of the 'what sensors/parts to pick' work above is moot if it's already decided by what's already fabricated and soldered.
- Checklist to send the professor: (1) Which exact ESP32-S3 module is on the board - bare chip or WROOM-1/1U/2/MINI-1, and which flash/PSRAM variant? (2) Is there bulk capacitance (>=100-470uF low-ESR) placed directly at the module's 3V3/GND pins, separate from any regulator board caps? (3) What IMU (and baro/mag if any) is already routed/soldered, and on which I2C address/bus? (4) What is the battery connector and chemistry/capacity he's targeting (JST-PH2.0 1S, presumably)? (5) Is there a dedicated charge IC on-board (TP4056/MCP73831/other) or does he charge off-board? (6) What's the motor driver topology - MOSFET+LEDC/RMT for brushed motors, or MCPWM for BLDC/servos, and how many motor channels are routed? (7) Has he measured actual board+components weight yet, and what's the target all-up weight budget for the gondola (excluding envelope/helium)? (8) Does the board expose USB-native (S3's built-in USB-OTG/JTAG) for debug, or only UART? (9) Is there an antenna keep-out zone respected on the layout (>=15mm copper-free), and has he tested actual WiFi range/RSSI? (10) Does he have a battery-voltage-monitoring divider on an ADC pin already, and has he seen any brownout resets during WiFi activity in testing? (11) Is a camera module (OV2640/OV5640/OV3660) populated or planned, and is PSRAM enough for it? (12) What firmware base is he building on - ESP-Drone, ESP-FC/Betaflight, or fully custom? (13) Does he have the IAR_LARC2026.pdf rules already, and can he forward it (team could not retrieve it programmatically)? (14) What does 'racing' mean operationally in IAR - lap-based, target-capture (like Lehigh's own 'Defend the Republic' balloon-capture format), or something else? (15) Envelope: is he supplying/designing the balloon/envelope himself, or does IAR provide a standard envelope (the 'Blimp sample' page hints at a reference airship kit)?
- Given the strong ESP-FLY -> Lehigh ESP-Blimp -> MochiSwarm lineage, treat MochiSwarm (arxiv 2503.03077) as the primary technical precedent to study in depth (read the actual PDF, not just the HTML mirror) rather than generic quadcopter tutorials - its control architecture, actuator layout (2x microservo+brushless 'bicopter' stacks) and ESP-NOW comm choice are the most directly transferable design decisions for this exact contest lineage.
- Prioritize verifying/adding bulk decoupling capacitance at the ESP32-S3 module's power pins on the existing PCB as the first hardware bring-up task - highest-probability failure mode given a small 1S battery + WiFi.
- If vision is required by the actual (currently unretrieved) IAR rules, plan for a MochiSwarm-style simple color/IR-blob balloon/goal detector rather than general object detection; verify PSRAM is populated on the professor's module before committing to camera use.
- Obtain the IAR_LARC2026.pdf and the Blimp-sample page images manually (open in a real browser) - this blocks finalizing weight/size targets and race-format-driven control design decisions.
- Add a magnetometer (LSM9DS1 for one-part simplicity, or an ICM-20948/MPU9250-style combo, or LIS3MDL alongside a 6-axis IMU) specifically because of the long-duration low-yaw-authority nature of blimp flight - this is a likely upgrade point over the professor's current board if it mirrors the reference material (circuitdigest, ESP-FLY), which only use MPU6050 with no magnetometer.
- For altitude sensing, plan on VL53L1X (or L5CX) fused with the barometer rather than barometer alone, mirroring esp-drone's own extension-board sensor choice.

Pulled the actual Table 6-4 ('Current Consumption in Active Mode') from the datasheet PDF: 802.11b TX at 1/11 Mbps draws 355mA at 20.5dBm (this is the true peak); 802.11g TX 54Mbps draws 297mA at 18dBm; 802.11n HT20 MCS7 draws 286mA at 17.5dBm; 802.11n HT40 MCS7 (the 285mA figure) draws its current at 17dBm, NOT 20.5dBm. So for brownout/decoupling design, size for the true worst case: 355mA @ 20.5dBm (802.11b), not '285-355mA at 20.5dBm' as a single bundled statement.

Treat 2.43V as plausible but NOT confirmed from a primary source - verify against the ESP-IDF brownout config header (soc/soc_caps.h / esp_hw_support brownout driver) before relying on it.

Motors are '716' (720 optional), battery is '300mAh 1S LiPo' with 350mAh as an optional variant (not a continuous 300-350mAh range), propellers 46mm/55mm. All matches the original claim's substance.

IMPORTANT ADDED NUANCE the original summary under-emphasized: this reference project runs on a PLAIN ESP32 (WROOM), not an ESP32-S3 - so it is a BOM/architecture reference only, not a code base that ports directly onto the professor's S3 boards (different ADC characteristics, no native USB-OTG/JTAG, no LX7 SIMD).

Could not re-confirm the 0x68/0x69 AD0-pin address split from this specific page (not shown in the fetched excerpt), but that address scheme is standard across the whole ICM-4xx88/MPU-6xxx family and is documented on every distributor breakout (e.g. Adafruit, SparkFun) - treat as confirmed by convention rather than re-verified on the primary datasheet page itself this session.

### Added by fact-checker

- The mochi-swarm-sim repo (github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim, itself a fork of vincentzouras/mochi-swarm-sim) contains a MuJoCo XML model file literally named models/mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml. This is a directly relevant, currently-accessible artifact the original research missed: since the actual IAR rules PDF is unreachable, this course/model file (from a lab tied to the same MochiSwarm/Lehigh lineage that co-organizes IAR) is the best available proxy for arena/course geometry and should be opened and measured before assuming any arena dimensions.
- The professor's existing PCB is ESP32-S3-based, but two of the three pieces of reference material (circuitdigest's DIY WiFi drone AND Espressif's own esp-drone default firmware target) are built around the classic ESP32 (WROOM-32D) or ESP32-S2, not ESP32-S3. Only the ESP-FLY/Lehigh-Blimp lineage (XIAO ESP32-S3) and MochiSwarm are actually S3-native. This means firmware from the circuitdigest tutorial cannot be flashed as-is onto an S3 board; peripheral driver code (ADC, RMT/LEDC channel mapping, USB) needs porting, not copying.
- IAR's stated date range (Nov 2-6, 2026) starts two calendar days before the co-located LARC/LARS 2026 event's stated range (Nov 4-6, 2026), per each event's own official page. Neither page explains the gap. This should be raised directly with the organizers (contacts on the IAR page: dantonio@oakland.edu, saldana@lehigh.edu) before booking travel, since it may mean airship-specific setup/qualification happens on Nov 2-3 ahead of the main joint conference days.
- ESP-FLY (Seeed Co-Create kit) total mass figures were independently re-confirmed straight from the repo: ~18g bare, ~25g with the 250mAh 1S LiPo, ~28g with FPV camera add-on, motors are '4x 615 coreless DC' (70,000 RPM, ~17g thrust each) - useful as a bare-minimum mass floor when budgeting the professor's own gondola weight against a target blimp envelope's lift capacity.

## perception (domain)  
**RELIABILITY: high**

The professor's reference material (3 YouTube videos, CircuitDigest article, esp-drone repo, mochi-swarm-sim repo) and the actual competition are ONE coherent lineage, not scattered inspiration: CircuitDigest's 'DIY-wifi-controlled-drone' firmware is Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone, a fork of espressif/esp-drone; 'Max Imagination's ESP-FLY video (V_mZsiZcy7s) builds that exact firmware onto a XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense quad (~$37 BOM); and the 3rd video (Y5i3LntcNt0, 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone', published 2026-08-17, channel 'Lehigh Swarms Club') is the SAME Lehigh group that authored MochiSwarm (arXiv:2503.03077) and BlimpSwarm (github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm) -- the platform the official IAR-LARC 2026 rulebook names, verbatim, as the competition's Baseline Design. I recovered the actual rulebook PDF (IAR_LARC2026.pdf, dated 2026-03-27) from the Google Drive link embedded in the rules page and read it in full via pdftotext. Key rule facts that reframe the whole perception problem: the game is NOT hoop/goal capture-vs-opponents (that's a different competition, GMU's 'Defend the Republic') -- IAR-LARC is solo, fully-autonomous WAYPOINT RACING. Each of 2 courses (10m x 5m rectangle vs. figure-eight, same footprint) has exactly 4 identical RED 22-inch mylar balloons tethered at 2.0m height that the airship must physically touch in a prescribed order; there are no opponent blimps to detect, no hoops, only one target color (red) per run. Helium volume capped at ~1 m^3 (50-inch-diameter-balloon equivalent), thrust budget capped at 100 gf total, size <=1x1x1.5 m, buoyancy must be neutral within +/-10 gf, and critically: 'All sensing used for navigation and task execution must be performed onboard the robot' while 'onboard or offboard computation is permitted' -- meaning the off-board-laptop-vision architecture the task asks me to justify is explicitly rules-legal (camera must ride the vehicle; the CPU that processes its feed need not), and this same clause is the de facto ban on external motion-capture/localization infrastructure. I also pulled the actual reference implementation: mochi-swarm-sim's src/state/auto_gate_sequence_state.py + spiral_search.py implement exactly the search->track->charge(score)->advance state machine the task describes, with a LOST_TIMEOUT reacquisition rule, confirm-frame/dwell-time gating before committing to a scoring pass, and an EMA-smoothed, ROI-gated HSV/contour blob tracker (src/vision/target_tracker.py) with concrete tuned thresholds. The real MochiSwarm hardware, notably, does NOT run its color detector on the ESP32 at all -- it offloads to a companion Arduino Nicla Vision (STM32H747, GC2145 2MP cam, ~$115 add-on) specifically because 'image processing induces a high computation overhead for microprocessors' -- running its Bayesian LAB-color-family detector at only 10 Hz on Nicla / 30 Hz on OpenMV Cam H7, never on the ESP32 itself. This is the single strongest piece of evidence bounding what's realistic to expect directly on an ESP32-S3. Camera-driver FPS numbers (espressif/esp32-camera) show OV2640 doing ~44 fps at QVGA / ~14 fps at VGA JPEG, OV5640 doing 40-50 fps at VGA-ish resolutions, both needing PSRAM above CIF resolution. ESP-WHO/ESP-DL benchmarks show ~25-27 fps achievable for face-style two-stage detection (MSR ~33ms + MNP ~6ms/candidate) and Edge-Impulse FOMO models hitting 10+ fps vs 2-5 fps for MobileNet-SSD on ESP32-S3, with esp-dl's own operator microbenchmarks showing large but operator-dependent speedups (Conv ~22.9x, MaxPool ~37x geomean) over plain ESP32. On-device AprilTag exists (nus-safmc/esp_apriltag, stnk20/esp32-Apriltag) but required memory hacks (commenting out most of a tag family's codeword table to fit RAM), a compiler workaround for a broken int32_t typedef on Xtensa, mandatory Octal PSRAM, and grayscale QVGA-ish resolution -- with no published FPS, i.e. proof-of-concept, not competition-hardened. Most ArUco-on-ESP32 projects found instead use the ESP32 purely as a camera source and decode markers off-board on a PC/RPi. For the off-board-latency argument: a real measured benchmark (RomanLut/hx-esp32-cam-fpv, raw-802.11-packet-injection, not TCP) achieves 90-110 ms glass-to-glass at 640x360@30-40fps; a standard socket+OpenCV/YOLO pipeline will add more but the physics argument (blimp response times are seconds-scale given 100 gf thrust cap, near-neutral buoyancy, low-Reynolds first-order drag per MochiSwarm's own dynamics model) means even 200-400 ms round-trip control latency is a small fraction of the vehicle's mechanical time constant -- this is an inference from the rulebook's own numbers plus the MochiSwarm dynamics section, not a directly measured end-to-end number for this exact pipeline, and I flag it as such. Practical on-site color calibration is not something to invent: the Lehigh team's own workflow (LehighBlimpGroup/balltraining + Blob-detection-and-Tracking repos) is get_gains.py -> fill a 2x2 grid overlay with the target color for 30s-2min collecting >=1500 LAB samples -> gaussian_manual_multi.py fits a 2D Gaussian per color -> paste resulting COLOR_DATA constants back into the firmware; this is directly portable as the on-site calibration tool the task asks for. Non-camera localization: VL53L1X (4m range, 27 deg FOV, 50Hz) and VL53L5CX (8x8 zones, 63 deg FOV, 60Hz, 400cm) are ST parts with hard datasheet numbers for altitude/wall sensing; PMW3901 datasheet/PX4-docs note it degrades to coarse (1/10-pixel) flow at higher altitude and needs a paired ToF sensor to scale flow to velocity -- since IAR-LARC waypoints sit at 2m and the arena floor texture is unknown, PMW3901's suitability here is a genuine open question, not verified for blimp altitudes/speeds in any source I found. UWB anchor pricing and GC032A datasheet specifics could not be retrieved this session (WebSearch quota was exhausted mid-task and the relevant WebFetch targets 404'd/429'd) and are flagged accordingly.

| Waypoint balloon | Red mylar, commercially available 22-inch balloon, ~50-60cm inflated diameter, tethered at 2.0 m height (center) |
| Helium volume limit | ~1 m^3 (50-inch-diameter-balloon equivalent) |
| Buoyancy tolerance | +/-10 gf net vertical force |
| Total thrust budget | 100 gf across all motors |
| Max robot bounding box | 1 m x 1 m x 1.5 m |
| Baseline platform MCU | XIAO ESP32S3 (Xtensa dual-core, up to 240MHz), 11 usable GPIO, 200Hz flight-control loop demonstrated |
| MochiSwarm color detector rate | 10 Hz on Nicla Vision (STM32H747), 30 Hz on OpenMV Cam H7, same algorithm |
| ESP-NOW link (used by MochiSwarm instead of plain WiFi) | peer-to-peer, up to ~480 m range, ~512 kbps |
| OV2640 JPEG streaming FPS (esp32-camera) | ~44 fps @ 320x240, ~14 fps @ 640x480, ~1.3 fps @ 1600x1200 |
| OV5640 streaming FPS (esp32-camera) | ~50 fps @ 640x360, ~40 fps @ 640x480, ~30 fps @ 1024x576/1280x720 |
| VL53L1X ToF | up to 4 m range, 27 deg FOV (full SPAD array), up to 50 Hz |
| VL53L5CX ToF | 8x8 multizone, 63 deg diagonal FOV, up to 400 cm, up to 60 Hz |
| PMW3901 optical flow nominal range | 80 mm to infinity (coarser resolution at higher altitude) |
| hx-esp32-cam-fpv measured glass-to-glass latency | 90-110 ms (raw 802.11 packet injection, not TCP) |
| MochiSwarm per-unit cost | ~$132.74 base blimp + $115 Nicla Vision add-on = ~$248 |
| BlimpSwarm bicopter base kit cost | ~$83.74 (2 motors + 2 ESCs + servo + XIAO ESP32S3 + 36in balloon + battery kit + straps) |
| ESP-DL esp32s3 vs plain esp32 operator speedup (geomean, v3.3.10) | Conv ~22.9x, MaxPool ~37.1x, Add ~3.69x, Mul ~3.20x (workload-dependent, range varies per op) |

### Key findings

- **[high]** The actual target competition is IAR-LARC 2026 (International Airship Racing at LARC), hosted at UMNG Bogota Nov 2-6 2026 -- a solo, fully-autonomous WAYPOINT-TOUCHING race, not a hoop/goal-scoring or anti-opponent game.
  - Official rulebook PDF 'IAR_LARC2026.pdf' (dated 2026-03-27), recovered from the Drive link on the rules page and read via pdftotext. Objective: navigate a circuit of 4 red balloon waypoints tethered at 2.0m height, touch each in prescribed order, fully autonomously, up to 3 loops per course, 2 courses (rectangle + figure-eight), best-time-per-course summed = final ranking. There are NO opponent blimps and no hoops in this ruleset.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules>
- **[high]** All sensing must be onboard the vehicle, but computation (including off-board laptop vision) is explicitly allowed by the same rulebook.
  - Rule 1: 'Onboard or offboard computation is permitted, but no human intervention is allowed during execution.' Rule 15: 'All sensing used for navigation and task execution must be performed onboard the robot.' Together these validate the off-board-vision architecture (camera on the blimp, CV/decision-making on a laptop over WiFi) as rules-legal, and are effectively the clause that rules out external motion-capture/localization infrastructure.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules>
- **[high]** Vehicle constraints from the rulebook: helium volume <= ~1 m^3 (50in-diameter-balloon equivalent), neutral buoyancy within +/-10 gf, total thrust budget capped at 100 gf across all motors, max bounding box 1m x 1m x 1.5m.
  - Rules 10-14, verbatim from IAR_LARC2026.pdf.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules>
- **[high]** The rulebook's own Appendix A Baseline Design is the professor's exact reference lineage: 'Mochiswarm' (arXiv:2503.03077) / BlimpSwarm (Lehigh), a bicopter platform on an ESP32 (XIAO ESP32S3) with 2 coreless DC motors inside a ~36in mylar/latex spherical balloon; the rulebook's own Appendix B simulator link is TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim.
  - Recommended baseline BOM per rulebook: ESP32 board, 2x coreless brushed DC motors, mylar/latex ~36in balloon, motor drivers + step-down regulators, 1S 500mAh LiPo; iterate with 3D-printed PLA/PETG, balsa, or carbon-fiber rods.
  - <https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03077>
- **[high]** The 3 YouTube reference videos and the CircuitDigest article are a single connected lineage, not independent examples.
  - CircuitDigest's 'DIY-wifi-controlled-drone' firmware = Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone, a fork of espressif/esp-drone. Video 1 (V_mZsiZcy7s, 'Max Imagination', title 'Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY') builds exactly that firmware onto a XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense + MPU6050 + SI2300 MOSFET-driven coreless-motor quad, ~$37 BOM, full parts list and firmware link in the description, credits Circuit-Digest explicitly. Video 2 (xW2Nwg_RX84) is a companion tutorial on the same channel building the MOSFET motor drivers used in video 1, on the same FireBeetle 2 ESP32-S3-U board family. Video 3 (Y5i3LntcNt0, channel 'Lehigh Swarms Club', published 2026-08-17, 162s, title 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone') is the Lehigh Blimp group (same authors as MochiSwarm/BlimpSwarm/IAR-LARC organizers -- David Saldana is both a MochiSwarm co-author and an IAR-LARC contact) taking the ESP-FLY frame/firmware and rehousing it in a blimp gondola.
  - <https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V_mZsiZcy7s>
- **[high]** MochiSwarm's real hardware does NOT run color/vision detection on the ESP32 -- it offloads to a companion vision coprocessor (Arduino Nicla Vision) because ESP32-class MCUs choke on image processing.
  - Direct quote from the paper: 'image processing induces a high computation overhead for microprocessors. Thus, to guarantee the performance of the flight control, we offload the visual servoing from the MCU to a detachable module based on NiclaVision.' Nicla Vision: STM32H747 (Cortex-M7 @480MHz + M4 @240MHz), GC2145 2MP camera, connected to the ESP32 (XIAO ESP32S3) via UART. Add-on cost cited in the paper's Table I as $115 (total blimp $132.74 + $115 vision module = ~$248/unit). Their color-family (LAB-space, Mahalanobis-distance, Bayes log-odds filter) detector runs at 10 Hz on NiclaVision, 30 Hz on OpenMV Cam H7 (same algorithm, lower camera latency).
  - <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.03077>
- **[medium]** esp32-camera driver FPS (measured, community benchmarks): OV2640 JPEG streaming ~44 fps @ QVGA(320x240), ~14 fps @ VGA(640x480), ~1.3 fps @ UXGA(1600x1200); OV5640 ~40-50 fps at 640x360/640x480, ~30 fps at 1024x576/1280x720. PSRAM is required above CIF resolution when using JPEG (and effectively mandatory for YUV/RGB modes).
  - Numbers come from community benchmark pages and a GitHub issue thread on esp32-camera, not an Espressif-published spec sheet -- treat as 'measured by builders,' not vendor-guaranteed.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp32-camera/issues/201>
- **[medium]** ESP-WHO/ESP-DL on ESP32-S3: ~25-27 fps achievable for a 2-stage face-style detector (MSR stage ~33ms/frame + MNP ~6ms per surviving candidate); Edge-Impulse FOMO object detection runs 10+ fps on ESP32-S3 vs 2-5 fps for MobileNet-SSD; ESP-DL's own per-operator benchmark (esp-dl v3.3.10, benchmark_report.md) shows Conv ~22.9x and MaxPool ~37.1x geomean speedup on ESP32-S3 vs plain ESP32 (workload-dependent, some ops <2x or even <1x).
  - FPS figures are from search-engine-summarized secondary sources (blog/CNX-software-style write-ups), not a single Espressif benchmark doc I directly read; the per-operator table IS a primary source I fetched directly (raw.githubusercontent.com/espressif/esp-dl/master/benchmark_report.md, generated 2026-09-01).
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/espressif/esp-dl/master/benchmark_report.md>
- **[high]** On-device AprilTag on ESP32 exists but is an unfinished, memory-constrained hack, not a proven competition-ready pipeline; most 'ArUco on ESP32' projects actually decode markers off-board.
  - nus-safmc/esp_apriltag README (a university safety/mechatronics competition team's own port) lists: had to comment out most of a tag family's codeword lookup table to fit RAM; a compiler bug where Xtensa's int32_t is really a 'long int' requiring a macro redefinition; PSRAM must be Octal; pixel format must be grayscale; resolution recommended at QVGA or below 'at the expense of performance' otherwise; no FPS numbers published. stnk20/esp32-Apriltag (17 stars) is a similar esp-idf port using OV2640. Multiple 'esp32 aruco' repos found via GitHub search (danielchandg/aruco-markers, mickei0708/esp32-aruco-localization, Sudhanva-K-Upadhya/aruco-robot-navigation) use the ESP32-CAM purely to grab the JPEG frame and run cv2.aruco decode on a laptop/PC.
  - <https://github.com/nus-safmc/esp_apriltag>
- **[medium]** A real measured glass-to-glass latency benchmark for ESP32-CAM WiFi video exists at 90-110ms, but it uses raw 802.11 packet injection (not a standard TCP/IP socket + OpenCV pipeline), so it is a best-case lower bound, not what a naive off-board OpenCV/YOLO pipeline will actually see.
  - RomanLut/hx-esp32-cam-fpv: 640x360@30-40fps (OV2640, overclocked) to 1280x720@12-30fps (OV5640, DVR mode), MJPEG streamed directly from camera DMA (not full-frame-buffered) specifically to shave 10-20ms versus buffered approaches, over raw broadcast 802.11 at 2.4 or 5.8GHz (not a WiFi association/TCP socket).
  - <https://github.com/RomanLut/hx-esp32-cam-fpv>
- **[high]** VL53L1X: up to 4m range, 27 deg FOV (all 265 SPADs active), up to 50Hz ranging frequency, programmable ROI. VL53L5CX: 8x8-zone multizone, 63 deg diagonal FOV, up to 400cm range, up to 60Hz -- ST's own marketing text calls it 'the fastest, multizone, miniature ToF sensor on the market.'
  - Straight from ST product pages/datasheets.
  - <https://www.st.com/resource/en/datasheet/vl53l5cx.pdf>
- **[low]** PMW3901 optical flow degrades to coarse (1/10-pixel) resolution at higher altitude and requires a paired ToF sensor (typically VL53L1X) to convert flow pixels to real-world velocity via known ground distance -- meaning its usefulness for a blimp cruising near the 2m waypoint height, over an unknown/low-texture competition-hall floor, is unverified and should be treated as an open engineering risk rather than a drop-in solution.
  - PX4 docs and datasheet describe the 80mm-to-infinity nominal range and the ToF-scaling dependency; no source found that specifically validates PMW3901 performance on a lighter-than-air vehicle at blimp cruise speeds/altitudes -- this conclusion is partly an inference from the generic optical-flow-sensor literature, not a blimp-specific measurement.
  - <https://docs.px4.io/main/en/sensor/pmw3901>
- **[high]** The task's assumed perception targets (opponent blimps, arbitrary 'coloured goal/hoop') do not match the actual IAR-LARC ruleset: there is exactly one target color (red mylar balloon) and no opponents at all; the GMU 'Defend the Republic' competition (balls through hoops, up to 50 robots, mixed teleop/autonomy) is a structurally different blimp competition that should not be used as the rules reference for this project.
  - Confirmed by reading the actual rulebook end-to-end; GMU competition described via ECE/Computing news pages, separate venue (EagleBank Arena) and separate organizers.
  - <https://ece.gmu.edu/news/2023-04/blimps-set-battle-eaglebank-arena>
- **[high]** A ready-made, directly transferable state-machine reference implementation for 'search -> acquire -> approach -> score -> retreat/advance' already exists in the rulebook's own recommended simulator.
  - mochi-swarm-sim/src/state/auto_gate_sequence_state.py implements states search (spiral re-acquisition) -> track (HSV/contour-blob-driven yaw+height servoing with EMA smoothing) -> charge (thrust ramp, dwell/confirm-frame gated to avoid false scoring, timed duration) -> advance to next expected-color waypoint; spiral_search.py implements yaw-rate ease-out spiral reacquisition with direction bias toward last-seen offset; a LOST_TIMEOUT reverts track -> search if the target isn't seen for too long. target_tracker.py gives concrete tuned HSV ranges and shape-confidence thresholds (e.g. red: H0-10&170-180, S>=110, V>=70; min area 55px; solidity>=0.78; circularity>=0.60; confidence>=0.64) as a numeric starting point.
  - <https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim/blob/main/src/state/auto_gate_sequence_state.py>
- **[high]** A concrete, reusable on-site color-calibration workflow already exists and is directly what the task's 'quick on-site calibration tool' ask should be built from (or ported to ESP32-S3/OpenCV) rather than invented.
  - get_gains.py (grabs the camera's live RGB gain triplet under current lighting) -> paste into perception_subsystem.py -> fill a 2x2 on-screen grid with the target color for 30s-2min, collecting >=1500 LAB-space (a,b) samples via the terminal -> feed those samples to gaussian_manual_multi.py, which fits a 2D Gaussian per color and prints the COLOR_DATA constants (mean + covariance) to paste back into firmware for Mahalanobis-distance color-family detection.
  - <https://github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/balltraining>
- **[high]** esp-drone (Espressif's own ESP32/-S2/-S3 quadcopter firmware, forked from Crazyflie) is stable but only lightly maintained: official support was reduced starting December 2022 ('we will offer limited support... but PR is still welcomed'), 121 commits, 28 open issues, 6 open PRs at time of check; requires ESP-IDF release/v5.0 branch specifically.
  - Directly from the repo's own README/notices.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone>
- **[high]** Approximate BOM costs: MochiSwarm base blimp (frame + ESP32 + actuators + battery, no vision) ~$132.74; +$115 for the NiclaVision perception add-on (~$248 total per unit, arXiv Table I). BlimpSwarm's own bicopter parts list (separate from the paper) totals ~$83.74 for a base kit: 2x 1102-13500KV brushless motors ($10 ea), 2x Gemfan 2015 props ($0.63 ea), 2x ESCs ($10 ea), 1x micro servo ($3.50), 1x XIAO ESP32S3 ($6.50), 1x 36in ruby-red foil balloon ($4.00), battery kit ($19.00), elastic straps ($9.49); optional VL53L1X-class ultrasonic ($11.50), BMP390 barometer ($12.50), 9DOF IMU ($25).
  - arXiv Table I for the $132.74+$115 figures; the BlimpSwarm docs site (lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/) for the $83.74 kit breakdown.
  - <https://lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/>

### Gotchas

- Do not design perception around 'opponent blimps' or 'hoops' -- the actual IAR-LARC 2026 rulebook has neither; it is single-color (red), single-shape (balloon), waypoint-sequence racing against the clock, solo per run.
- The rulebook bans external/offboard SENSING (rule 15), not offboard computation (rule 1) -- an off-board laptop running OpenCV/YOLO on a WiFi video feed from an onboard camera is explicitly legal; a ceiling-mounted motion-capture rig localizing the robot from outside is not, because the sensor itself isn't onboard.
- The competition's own recommended reference platform (MochiSwarm/BlimpSwarm) does NOT trust the ESP32 to do the vision computation -- it uses a second microcontroller (Nicla Vision / OpenMV) purely for vision at 10-30 Hz. Assuming the ESP32-S3 alone can do robust color-blob tracking AND full flight control AND WiFi telemetry at a solid frame rate needs to be validated early, not assumed.
- On-device AprilTag/ArUco on ESP32 is a fragile, hand-patched hack in every example found (RAM overflows requiring you to strip most of a tag family, a broken int32_t typedef on the Xtensa compiler, mandatory Octal PSRAM) with no published FPS -- treat this as a research risk, not a supported feature, if the design leans on fiducials instead of plain color blobs.
- PMW3901 optical flow's accuracy explicitly degrades at higher altitude (coarse 1/10-pixel resolution) and needs a paired downward ToF sensor for velocity scaling; nobody in the sources found has validated it specifically on a blimp near 2m altitude over an unknown floor -- don't assume PX4-drone-grade performance carries over.
- The 90-110ms 'measured' WiFi video latency benchmark used raw 802.11 packet injection, not a normal WiFi-associated TCP/UDP socket -- a standard off-board OpenCV pipeline over ordinary WiFi will likely be slower; use the physics argument (blimp response times are seconds-scale given the 100gf thrust cap and near-neutral buoyancy) rather than quoting 90-110ms as your own system's expected latency.
- esp-drone's official Espressif support has been reduced since Dec 2022 -- expect to maintain your own fork/patches the way CircuitDigest and Max Imagination's ESP-FLY did, rather than relying on upstream fixes.
- IAR-LARC scoring rewards RELIABLE completion over raw speed in a subtle way: an incomplete/invalid run (wrong order, human intervention, leaving the area, missed a touch) records no time at all for that attempt, and only the best of up to 3 loops per course counts -- a robust-but-slower search/approach strategy that never gets disqualified will very likely beat a fast-but-flaky one, per rules 5-7.

### Actionable

- Treat MochiSwarm (arXiv:2503.03077) and BlimpSwarm (github.com/LehighBlimpGroup/BlimpSwarm) as the primary engineering reference, not just 'a repo he mentioned' -- it is literally the rulebook's official Appendix A baseline design, built by the same people organizing the competition, on the same class of hardware (ESP32-S3) the professor already has PCBs for.
- Decide early, as a first architecture decision, between three concrete options and validate the riskiest one first: (A) pure on-ESP32-S3 HSV/blob color tracking (cheapest, matches esp32-camera+FreeRTOS skillset, ~14-44fps depending on resolution per the benchmarks found, but flight-control-loop contention is the open risk); (B) off-board laptop OpenCV/YOLO over WiFi (rules-legal per rule 1, latency likely acceptable given blimp time constants, but WiFi-hall-congestion risk needs its own test at the actual venue); (C) companion vision coprocessor a la Nicla Vision/OpenMV, the proven route the reference platform actually uses.
- Port or directly adapt LehighBlimpGroup/balltraining's get_gains.py + gaussian_manual_multi.py workflow (or an OpenCV-based equivalent for an off-board laptop) as the day-of-competition color calibration tool, rather than building one from scratch.
- Port or directly adapt mochi-swarm-sim/src/state/auto_gate_sequence_state.py + spiral_search.py as the autonomy state machine skeleton -- it already implements search/track/charge/advance with lost-target reacquisition and false-positive-scoring guards tuned for this exact game.
- Run TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim locally (pip install -r requirements.txt; python main.py [default|s|figure8]) before touching hardware -- its model files (mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml and the figure8 variant) are literal MuJoCo simulations of Course 1 and Course 2 from the rulebook, and it already depends on opencv-python, so the vision pipeline can be prototyped there first.
- Contact the IAR-LARC organizers (Diego S. D'Antonio, dantonio@oakland.edu; David Saldana, saldana@lehigh.edu) directly to clarify two rules ambiguities the PDF doesn't resolve: whether a second onboard MCU dedicated to vision (Nicla-Vision-style) counts as 'onboard sensing/computation' without restriction, and what tolerance applies to the 2m waypoint height and the +/-10gf buoyancy check on competition day.
- If pursuing ArUco/AprilTag at all, budget it as an R&D spike with a hard go/no-go date, given every example found needed nonstandard memory/compiler patches and none published an FPS number -- do not put it on the critical path for the first working prototype.
- For the off-board path, mitigate the real risk (WiFi congestion in a hall full of many teams' 2.4GHz devices) by testing with the exact router/AP hardware planned for competition day, considering a dedicated 5GHz link or ESP-NOW-style peer link for telemetry as MochiSwarm itself chose for its own inter-robot/ground-station link, and having a fallback pure-onboard-blob-tracking mode that can win a match on its own if WiFi degrades.

### Open questions

- UWB anchor/tag pricing (DW1000/DW3000, Qorvo/Decawave modules) for a 4-anchor indoor setup could not be retrieved this session -- fetch attempts hit HTTP 429/404 and WebSearch quota ran out; needs a follow-up pass.
- GC032A camera datasheet specifics (resolution, interface, native FPS) could not be retrieved -- the Espressif documentation link redirected and was not re-fetched before quota/attention ran out.
- No source found validating PMW3901 optical-flow performance specifically on a lighter-than-air vehicle at blimp cruise altitude/speed -- open research question, not just an evidence gap.
- No FPS/latency numbers exist for the on-device AprilTag ESP32 ports found (nus-safmc/esp_apriltag, stnk20/esp32-Apriltag) -- would need to be benchmarked directly if the team wants to pursue fiducials on-ESP32-S3.
- Whether the IAR-LARC rules permit a second onboard MCU/coprocessor dedicated purely to vision (Nicla-Vision-style) without further restriction is not explicitly stated in the rulebook beyond the general 'onboard sensing/computation' language -- worth confirming with the organizers.
- Exact tolerance windows the judges will apply to the 2m waypoint-height spec and the +/-10gf buoyancy check ('a small tolerance may be applied ... to account for environmental conditions') are left to organizer discretion in the rulebook text and not quantified.
- Whether external ceiling-mounted fiducials read by an upward-facing onboard camera (as opposed to an external mocap SYSTEM) would satisfy rule 15's 'onboard sensing' requirement is a plausible reading but not explicitly addressed by the rulebook -- worth confirming.

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — The actual competition (IAR-LARC 2026) is solo, fully-autonomous WAYPOINT-TOUCHING racing (4 red balloons/course, 2 courses: 10x5m rectangle + figure-eight), NOT a hoop/goal or anti-opponent game.
- **CONFIRMED** — Rule 1 permits onboard OR offboard computation ('no human intervention... during execution') while Rule 15 requires all SENSING to be onboard — validating an off-board-laptop-vision architecture.
- **CONFIRMED** — Numeric vehicle specs: helium volume ~1 m^3 (50in-diameter-balloon equivalent), buoyancy tolerance ±10 gf, thrust budget 100 gf total, waypoint = red mylar 22in commercial balloon at 2.0m height (~50-60cm inflated diameter).
  - Correction: All of these match the rulebook verbatim (Rules 10, 11, 13, 21, 22). However the claimed '1x1x1.5m max bounding box' (Rule 14) needs nuance: the same rulebook's Rule 12, one line earlier, states 'There are no explicit size limitations on the airship' — the primary source itself is internally contradictory about size limits, which the original research did not flag. Worth clarifying with organizers rather than treating 1x1x1.5m as an unambiguous hard cap.
- **CONFIRMED** — The rulebook's own Appendix A 'Baseline Design' is MochiSwarm/BlimpSwarm (arXiv:2503.03077), and Appendix B 'Simulator' is TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim.
- **CONFIRMED** — MochiSwarm's real hardware does NOT run color/vision detection on the ESP32 — it offloads to Nicla Vision at 10 Hz (30 Hz on OpenMV Cam H7), and the vision add-on costs $115 on top of a $132.74 base blimp.
- **CONFIRMED** — MochiSwarm's XIAO ESP32S3 runs the flight controller at 200 Hz on the 240 MHz dual-core, with 11 usable GPIO; ESP-NOW link range up to ~480 m at ~512 kbps.
- **CONFIRMED** — The 3 reference YouTube videos are: V_mZsiZcy7s = 'Build The Smallest ESP32 Drone You Can Fly with Your Phone | ESP-FLY' (Max Imagination); xW2Nwg_RX84 = a companion MOSFET-motor-driver tutorial, same channel; Y5i3LntcNt0 = 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone', channel 'Lehigh Swarms Club', published 2026-08-17, 162 seconds long.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — CircuitDigest's 'DIY-wifi-controlled-drone' firmware (Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone) is 'a fork of espressif/esp-drone'.
  - Correction: Not a literal GitHub fork: the GitHub API for Circuit-Digest/ESP-Drone returns "fork": false, and its README makes no mention of Espressif/Crazyflie at all (it instead frames the repo as an independent project that evolved into 'LiteWing'). However, CircuitDigest's own article at the cited URL explicitly states 'The firmware from our DIY drone is based on the ESP-drone firmware from Espressif,' so the substantive claim (derived from Espressif's esp-drone) is correct — 'fork' just overstates a formal repo relationship that doesn't exist on GitHub.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — espressif/esp-drone has reduced official support since Dec 2022, with '121 commits, 28 open issues, 6 open PRs at time of check'.
  - Correction: The qualitative claim is confirmed verbatim ('From December 2022, we will offer limited support on this project, but Pull Request is still welcomed!' — still in the current README). But the live counts I pulled today (2026-09-21) do not match: 0 open PRs (not 6), 34 open issues (not 28), 2188 stars, 508 forks, and the repo was pushed as recently as 2026-08-10 — i.e., it is still receiving occasional commits despite the 'limited support' notice, which somewhat undercuts the 'lightly maintained' framing.
- **CONFIRMED** — ESP-DL v3.3.10 operator microbenchmark shows Conv ~22.9x and MaxPool ~37x geomean speedup on ESP32-S3 vs plain ESP32.
- **CONFIRMED** — RomanLut/hx-esp32-cam-fpv achieves a measured 90-110ms glass-to-glass latency using raw 802.11 packet injection (not a normal TCP/UDP WiFi socket), at ~640x360@30-40fps on OV2640.
- **CONFIRMED** — Nicla Vision's chip identity is STM32H747 (Cortex-M7@480MHz + M4@240MHz) with a GC2145 2MP camera.
  - Correction: Facts are correct per Arduino's own documentation, but this was mis-cited: the original bundled this under the arXiv MochiSwarm paper as its evidence_url, yet the paper itself never mentions 'STM32H747' or 'GC2145' anywhere (confirmed by grepping the full extracted paper text) — the true source is Arduino's docs, not the paper.

### Added by fact-checker

- The rulebook itself is internally inconsistent on size: Rule 12 ('Vehicle Size Constraints') states 'There are no explicit size limitations on the airship,' immediately followed by Rule 14 ('Robot Size Constraints') giving a hard 1m x 1m x 1.5m bounding box. Flag this contradiction to the organizers (dantonio@oakland.edu / saldana@lehigh.edu) rather than assuming 1x1x1.5m is an unambiguous ceiling.
- The MochiSwarm paper's own demonstration task (pickup-and-delivery, Table I / Fig. captions) explicitly involves 'target balloons AND goal hoops' — the paper's perception subsystem is literally described as detecting balloons and hoops. This is likely the actual source of any 'hoop' language circulating around this project lineage; it comes from MochiSwarm's own generic MAPD case study, not from IAR-LARC (whose rulebook has no hoops at all, confirmed by direct read).
- IAR-LARC's rulebook Appendix A recommended-parts list is generic ('ESP32 Microcontroller board', 2x coreless brushed DC motors, ~36in mylar/latex balloon, motor drivers+regulators, 1S 500mAh LiPo) and does not itself name the XIAO ESP32S3 or any specific vendor part — the XIAO ESP32S3 specificity, GPIO count, and 200Hz loop rate all come only from the referenced arXiv paper, one level removed from the rulebook text itself.
- The GMU 'Defend the Republic' blimp competition page (ece.gmu.edu/news/2023-04/blimps-set-battle-eaglebank-arena) does independently confirm hoop-based gameplay and a different venue (EagleBank Arena) from IAR-LARC, supporting the original researcher's warning not to conflate the two competitions.
- espressif/esp-drone's live GitHub stats as of 2026-09-21: 2,188 stars, 508 forks, 0 open PRs, 34 open issues, last pushed 2026-08-10 — i.e., despite the 'limited support since Dec 2022' notice, the repo is still receiving updates as of last month, which is a mildly more encouraging maintenance signal than the original research's framing suggested.
