## comms (domain)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

Researched comms/ground-station/dev-tooling for the ESP32-S3 blimp entering IAR-LARC 2026 (Nov 2-6, Universidad Militar Nueva Granada, Bogotá) - the inaugural "International Airship Racing at LARC" category, an autonomous lighter-than-air (LTA) event run alongside LARC/LARS 2026 (Nov 4-6, same venue), organized by Diego S. D'Antonio and David Saldana (Lehigh Swarms/SWARMS Lab). Critical discovery: the competition's own "Blimp Sample" page embeds two Lehigh Swarms Club videos - "ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone" (youtu.be/Y5i3LntcNt0, one of the professor's 3 reference videos) and a second "Blimp assembly tutorial" (youtu.be/Sybc0XwAaL8, not previously known to the user). This means the organizers themselves point teams at the exact same ESP-FLY-derived build the professor already sent - the professor's material is not just inspiration, it is the de facto reference platform. ESP-FLY (by YouTuber "Max Imagination") is a $37 XIAO-ESP32-S3 + MPU6050 micro-quad whose firmware/app is literally Espressif's esp-drone (ESP-IDF release/v5.0 branch, ESP32/S2/S3 supported) controlled via the official "ESP-Drone" phone app; ESP-FLY's own build uses ESP-IDF v5.0.7 offline installer, and its 3D frame is on Cults3D with a PVC-frame no-print alternative. esp-drone's PC ground-station path uses a forked Crazyflie client (cfclient, github.com/leeebo/crazyflie-clients-python), inheriting Crazyflie's CRTP protocol (4-bit port/2-bit channel header, <=30B payload, port-priority ordering) as the multiplexing model for commands+telemetry+logging - directly reusable as an on-wire framing design regardless of transport chosen. On links: ESP32-S3 radio is hard-capped at 2.4GHz WiFi b/g/n + BLE5 (no 5GHz silicon at all, confirmed on Espressif's own product page), so "does 5GHz help" is moot - it isn't available. Independent logic-analyzer benchmarking (Electric UI/Hackaday) gives real median E2E latencies: ESP-NOW 5.6ms(12B)/5.8ms(128B)/24ms(1024B); WiFi-TCP ~6ms flat; WiFi-WebSocket 10-14ms; BLE Bluedroid 14.6-31ms; BLE NimBLE 22-37ms; dedicated nRF24 radio 0.3-23ms (fastest for small payloads but needs extra hardware); 802.15.4/Thread(C6) 2.5-45ms; other sources report ESP-NOW commonly <1-3ms at short range degrading to 70ms+/lossy beyond ~100m. ESP-IDF's esp_now.h documents hard limits: 250B/packet (v1) or 1470B (ESP-NOW v2, IDF v5.x), max 20 total peers/17 encrypted. The single biggest coexistence gotcha (confirmed across ESP32 forum, Arduino forum, RandomNerdTutorials, circuitlabs.net, and the existence of a whole helper lib gmag11/QuickESPNow): ESP32 has ONE radio, so in WIFI_MODE_APSTA the ESP-NOW channel is forced to match whatever channel the STA connection (router/AP) negotiated - you cannot run ESP-NOW on channel 6 while STA-associates on channel 1. For the crowded-arena problem, since ESP-NOW still shares the same 2.4GHz band as venue WiFi (no immunity, just no AP-association/beacon overhead), the strongest congestion-avoidance play is to add a dedicated non-2.4GHz-WiFi radio: ExpressLRS (ELRS, github.com/ExpressLRS/ExpressLRS) runs on ESP32/ESP8285/STM32 hardware, supports 900MHz (better penetration, avoids WiFi band entirely) or 2.4GHz (up to 1000Hz packet rate), and outputs CRSF/SBUS/MAVLink/PWM to a flight controller - good candidate for a manual-override/teleop channel fully decoupled from the telemetry/autonomy link. For Bluetooth-gamepad-based manual override, Bluepad32 (github.com/ricardoquesada/bluepad32) supports DualSense/Xbox/Switch/8BitDo etc. on ESP32-S3 natively (but S3 only supports "a subset" of controllers vs classic ESP32 - verify specific pad before buying), runs on one core only, low latency, works with both ESP-IDF and Arduino. On dev tooling for Windows: current official ESP-IDF installer is the "EIM" (Espressif Installation Manager, GUI or `winget install Espressif.EIM`/`EIM-CLI`), which pulls toolchain+git+Python(>=3.10) and writes a reusable eim_config.toml (older docs/tutorials, including ESP-FLY's own, still reference the classic Windows .exe installer + export.ps1 workflow, which remains functionally equivalent). Arduino-ESP32 core v3.3.12 is itself built on ESP-IDF 5.5 (i.e. Arduino is a wrapper/component over IDF, not a rival stack), and PlatformIO's `framework = espidf` gives full native IDF (CMake, menuconfig, security features) inside PlatformIO's project tooling. Since esp-drone/ESP-FLY firmware is plain ESP-IDF pinned to the v5.0 branch, staying in native ESP-IDF (or PlatformIO-wrapped IDF) is the lowest-friction path to reuse that codebase; Arduino would require a substantial port. OTA is well documented in ESP-IDF's app_update component (ota_0/ota_1 + ota-data partitions, esp_ota_begin/write/end, rollback-safe mode that only commits the new image after esp_ota_mark_app_valid_cancel_rollback() succeeds) - a strong practical case for this project since the professor's PCB will be taped inside a sealed gondola under a helium envelope, making USB-serial reflashing physically inconvenient and helium-leak-risky every time the envelope has to be reopened. For micro-ROS: the official micro_ros_espidf_component (github.com/micro-ROS/micro_ros_espidf_component) does support ESP32-S3, tested on ESP-IDF v5.4/5.5/6.0 over UDP(WiFi) or UART transport, but is explicitly labeled "not ready for production use" (419 stars/25 open issues) and needs a newer IDF than esp-drone's pinned v5.0 branch - a real integration/version-reconciliation cost to flag before committing to ROS2 on this hardware. MAVLink prior art: heartbeat convention is 1Hz nominal rate, link considered lost after ~4-5 consecutive missed heartbeats (mavlink.io) - directly reusable as the failsafe-timeout design pattern regardless of whether MAVLink itself is used as the wire protocol. For blimp-specific failsafe behavior (reasoned from LTA physics, not sourced from a single document): unlike a quadcopter, a blimp sized for slight positive buoyancy fails safely by simply cutting commanded thrust and going inert - it drifts and slowly rises rather than falling, which is a materially different and generally safer failsafe posture than trying to force an active "descend" that would require thrust authority you may not want to rely on during a link-loss event; the professor's team should design specifically to exploit this (buoyancy-based inherent failsafe) rather than copying a multirotor failsafe checklist. mochi-swarm-sim (github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim) is a MuJoCo/Python physics simulator, not comms/firmware code, but its scenario files (literally named "mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml") indicate it was built to simulate blimp-racing swarms - very likely usable as-is for pre-flight autonomy/multi-blimp coordination testing given the same competition family, though it defines no real radio protocol. Could not retrieve: the actual IAR_LARC2026.pdf rules document (linked/embedded on the rules page but blocked from headless/text fetch, returns 401) - this is the single most important missing artifact, since it alone would contain the real weight/size/RF/frequency rules; the professor should open it directly in a browser. Also could not retrieve: ESP32-S3 exact WiFi/BLE TX power in dBm (datasheet PDF was not machine-readable in this pass), and full YouTube video descriptions/comments for 2 of the 3 professor-supplied videos (YouTube blocks headless scraping even via jina.ai reader in most cases; only oEmbed title/author metadata and, for one video, the full public description were recoverable).

### Key findings

- **[high]** IAR-LARC 2026's official 'Blimp Sample' reference page embeds the same Lehigh Swarms Club video the professor sent, plus one more the professor doesn't have
  - sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/blimp-sample embeds youtube.com/watch?v=Y5i3LntcNt0 ('ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone') and a second, previously-unknown video youtube.com/watch?v=Sybc0XwAaL8 ('Blimp assembly tutorial'), both by the 'Lehigh Swarms Club' channel (linked to Lehigh's Swarms/SWARMS Lab, run by IAR-LARC co-organizer David Saldana). This means the professor's reference material is literally the competition's own suggested reference platform, not just 'inspiration'.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/blimp-sample>
- **[high]** IAR-LARC is a real, dedicated blimp-racing category, inaugural 2026 edition, at UMNG Bogotá
  - 'International Airship Racing at LARC' (IAR-LARC), Nov 2-6 2026, New Granada Military University, Colombia; teams from Colombia, US, Mexico, Ecuador; organizers Diego S. D'Antonio and David Saldana; runs alongside the 26th LARC / 23rd LARS (Nov 4-6 2026, same venue) per the UMNG mecatronica page, which is the same combined LARC-LARS 2026 event.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/home>
- **[high]** ESP-FLY (base design for the blimp) is a $37 XIAO-ESP32-S3 micro-quad running Espressif's esp-drone firmware, controlled by the official ESP-Drone phone app
  - Parts: Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 MCU, MPU6050 IMU, 4x micro coreless motors, 150-250mAh LiPo, SI2300 MOSFETs, ~$37.30 total. Software: ESP-IDF v5.0.7 (offline installer). 3D frame on Cults3D; PVC-frame no-print alternative offered. Firmware/PCB/gerbers distributed via a Google Drive folder linked in the video description.
  - <https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V_mZsiZcy7s>
- **[high]** espressif/esp-drone supports ESP32/S2/S3, requires ESP-IDF release/v5.0 branch specifically, uses WiFi for the phone app and separately ESP-NOW for a gamepad (ESP-BOX3) controller
  - Ground-station PC client is a fork of Crazyflie's cfclient (github.com/leeebo/crazyflie-clients-python), implying CRTP-style framing. Flight modes: stabilize, height-hold, position-hold (needs extension board), app-controlled. Repo notes 'limited support' as of Dec 2022 - treat as community-maintained, not actively supported by Espressif.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone>
- **[high]** Crazyflie's CRTP is directly reusable prior art for command/telemetry framing
  - Header = 4-bit port (0-15) + 2-bit channel (0-3), payload <=30 bytes. Strict ordering guaranteed only within a port; ports are priority-ordered (0=console highest priority...15=link/echo). Named ports: 0 console, 2 params, 3 commander (roll/pitch/yaw/thrust), 4 memory, 5 logging, 7 generic setpoint/position, 13 platform, 15 link/echo-ping.
  - <https://www.bitcraze.io/documentation/repository/crazyflie-firmware/master/functional-areas/crtp/>
- **[high]** ESP32-S3 radio is 2.4GHz-only, no 5GHz option exists on this chip at all
  - WiFi 802.11 b/g/n, 2.4GHz only, up to 40MHz bandwidth; Bluetooth 5 (LE only, no classic BT), Coded PHY long-range mode + 2Mbps PHY. So 'does 5GHz help with congestion' is not an available mitigation on this hardware - it must be answered with an add-on radio instead.
  - <https://www.espressif.com/en/products/socs/esp32-s3>
- **[high]** Measured wireless-link median latencies (logic-analyzer benchmark, 12B/128B/1024B payloads)
  - ESP-NOW(ESP32): 5.6/5.8/24 ms. WiFi-TCP(ESP32,optimized): ~6/~6/~6 ms. WiFi-WebSocket(ESP32): 14.4/13.6/10 ms. BLE Bluedroid(ESP32): 14.6/21/31 ms. BLE NimBLE(ESP32): 22/24/37 ms. nRF24 2Mbps: 0.3/1.9/23 ms. nRF24 256kbps: 1.5/7/68 ms. 802.15.4(ESP32-C6): 2.5/8.7/45 ms. LoRa(high-speed cfg): 22/109/817 ms.
  - <https://electricui.com/blog/latency-comparison>
- **[medium]** Other sources report ESP-NOW typically sub-ms to a few ms at short range, degrading sharply with distance
  - 127-byte ESP-NOW packets transfer in under 1ms with ~95% of round-trips under 1ms in one test; another report of median ~2ms with heavy-tailed jitter growing with distance; another of 5-20ms round-trip typical, up to 70ms with missed messages at range extremes; one report of >100m range with <3ms E2E latency and minimal loss. These are inconsistent across setups/antennas/environments - treat as order-of-magnitude, not a single hard number.
  - <https://forum.arduino.cc/t/esp-now-broadcast-transmisison-latency/1347159>
- **[high]** ESP-NOW hard protocol limits (ESP-IDF, current stable)
  - Max data length: 250 bytes (v1, ESP_NOW_MAX_DATA_LEN) or 1470 bytes (v2, ESP_NOW_MAX_DATA_LEN_V2, needs IDF v5.x). Max 20 total peers, max 17 encrypted peers (default limit 7, raised via CONFIG_ESP_WIFI_ESPNOW_MAX_ENCRYPT_NUM). v1 devices can only talk to other v1 devices unless payload stays <=250B.
  - <https://docs.espressif.com/projects/esp-idf/en/stable/esp32s3/api-reference/network/esp_now.html>
- **[high]** The ESP-NOW+WiFi-STA 'channel-lock' gotcha is real and well documented
  - ESP32 has a single 2.4GHz radio. In WIFI_MODE_APSTA, once STA associates to a router, the whole radio (including ESP-NOW) is forced onto the router's channel; ESP-NOW peers must match that channel or communication silently fails. A dedicated helper library (gmag11/QuickESPNow) exists specifically to manage channel-hopping/synchronization around this limitation.
  - <https://circuitlabs.net/esp-now-with-wifi-coexistence/>
- **[high]** ExpressLRS (ELRS) is viable prior art for a dedicated low-latency, congestion-resistant manual-override link
  - Open-source RC link on Semtech SX127x/SX1280 + ESP32/ESP8266/STM32 hardware. Packet rate up to 1000Hz at 2.4GHz, up to 200Hz at 900MHz (900MHz avoids the WiFi band entirely, trading data rate/size for penetration and congestion immunity). Outputs CRSF, SBUS, SUMD, HoTT telemetry, MAVLink, or PWM to a flight controller.
  - <https://github.com/ExpressLRS/ExpressLRS>
- **[high]** Bluepad32 is a mature option for Bluetooth-gamepad manual override on ESP32-S3
  - Supports DualSense/DualShock3-4/Xbox Wireless/Switch Pro/JoyCon/8BitDo/Stadia/etc; runs on original ESP32, ESP32-S3/C3/C6/H2, RPi Pico W; explicit note that 'ESP32-S3, ESP32-C3, ESP32-C6 and ESP32-H2 only support a subset' of controllers vs the original ESP32 - must verify the specific pad model against S3 before purchase. Uses only CPU0, described as low-latency/small-footprint; works with ESP-IDF native or Arduino. Depends on BTstack (commercial license needed for closed-source commercial use; not an issue for a university team).
  - <https://github.com/ricardoquesada/bluepad32>
- **[medium]** Current official ESP-IDF Windows install path is the EIM tool (GUI/CLI), not a separate installer+export.ps1 as older tutorials show
  - Espressif Installation Manager (EIM) is downloadable directly or via `winget install Espressif.EIM` (GUI) / `Espressif.EIM-CLI`; installs IDF + toolchains + Git + Python (>=3.10 required) and writes a reproducible eim_config.toml. Older material (including ESP-FLY's own video, which references an 'ESP-IDF offline installer v5.0.7') used the classic Windows installer .exe + export.ps1/export.bat workflow, which is still functionally how the environment gets activated per shell session.
  - <https://docs.espressif.com/projects/esp-idf/en/stable/esp32s3/get-started/windows-setup.html>
- **[medium]** Arduino-ESP32 is architecturally a component built on top of ESP-IDF, not a competing stack
  - Current Arduino core for ESP32 (v3.3.12, per docs header) is 'based on ESP-IDF 5.5' - meaning Arduino sketches ultimately compile against an ESP-IDF version underneath, and Arduino libraries can be pulled into a native ESP-IDF project as a component. Since esp-drone/ESP-FLY firmware is plain ESP-IDF pinned to the v5.0 branch, staying native ESP-IDF (or PlatformIO's `framework = espidf`) is the lowest-friction way to keep building on that existing codebase; a pure-Arduino rewrite would be extra work with no compatibility benefit.
  - <https://docs.espressif.com/projects/arduino-esp32/en/latest/>
- **[high]** PlatformIO's ESP-IDF framework integration gives full native IDF capability (CMake, menuconfig, security features) wrapped in PlatformIO project tooling
  - Set via `framework = espidf` in platformio.ini; supports `pio run -t menuconfig`, zero-config debugging, and 100+ ESP32 board variants including ESP32-S3. Useful if the team wants unified dependency/CI management across multiple boards (e.g. blimp MCU + a separate ground-station ESP32).
  - <https://docs.platformio.org/en/latest/frameworks/espidf.html>
- **[high]** ESP-IDF OTA is a strong practical fit given the gondola-under-balloon form factor
  - OTA requires ota_0/ota_1 app slots + an ota-data partition in the partition table. 'Safe mode' writes the new image to the inactive slot first (bootable-if-interrupted); esp_ota_write()/esp_ota_end()/esp_ota_set_boot_partition() perform the update; with CONFIG_BOOTLOADER_APP_ROLLBACK_ENABLE, a new image must call esp_ota_mark_app_valid_cancel_rollback() or the bootloader reverts to the previous good image automatically on next boot/crash.
  - <https://docs.espressif.com/projects/esp-idf/en/stable/esp32s3/api-reference/system/ota.html>
- **[high]** micro-ROS on ESP32-S3 is technically supported but explicitly pre-production, and its supported IDF versions are newer than esp-drone's pinned branch
  - micro_ros_espidf_component supports ESP32/S2/S3/C3/C6, tested on ESP-IDF v5.4, v5.5, v6.0, transports UDP(WiFi, default) or UART. README states 'This software is not ready for production use'; repo has 419 stars, 25 open issues. No documented RAM/flash footprint found. Adding it to the esp-drone-based (v5.0-branch) firmware would likely require an IDF version bump.
  - <https://github.com/micro-ROS/micro_ros_espidf_component>
- **[high]** MAVLink's heartbeat convention is reusable failsafe-timing prior art regardless of protocol choice
  - Heartbeat nominal rate on RF telemetry links is 1Hz; a component is considered disconnected after roughly 4-5 consecutive missed heartbeats; a component in a faulted state should stop broadcasting its heartbeat rather than broadcasting a 'bad' one.
  - <https://mavlink.io/en/services/heartbeat.html>
- **[unverified]** A blimp's link-loss failsafe should exploit buoyancy rather than copy multirotor logic
  - A blimp sized for slight positive/neutral buoyancy fails inherently safely by simply zeroing thrust on link loss (drifts/slowly rises) rather than needing an active 'descend' maneuver a multirotor requires; this is a physics-based inference from the platform, not a cited rule or forum claim, and should be validated against the actual IAR_LARC2026.pdf safety rules once retrieved.
- **[medium]** mochi-swarm-sim is a MuJoCo/Python simulator with scenario files apparently built for this exact competition family
  - Repo (TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim) is Python + MuJoCo, CLI-driven via main.py; file naming such as 'mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml' strongly suggests blimp-racing-swarm scenarios; README does not document a real inter-robot communication protocol, coordination algorithm, or design rationale - it's for offline autonomy/physics prototyping, not a comms reference.
  - <https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim>
- **[high]** The circuitdigest DIY WiFi drone article (professor's reference material) is a SoftAP+UDP+phone-app design with known real-world limits
  - ESP32 WROOM creates its own WiFi hotspot (password '12345678'); phone app talks UDP to it. ~5-7 min flight time on a 1300mAh LiPo, 30-50m range outdoors / 15-25m indoors, ~60g total weight, drifts in wind above 5-8mph, no GPS/no outdoor position-hold. Common failures cited: counterfeit/misbehaving MPU6050 IMUs, wrong propeller orientation, under-rated battery causing voltage sag, loose motor wiring.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone>
- **[unverified]** IAR-LARC 2026 rules PDF exists but is not text-extractable via automated fetch
  - The Rules page (sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules) references and links an embedded 'IAR_LARC2026.pdf' (apparently via a Google Drive viewer), but every automated fetch attempt (direct, r.jina.ai reader) returned either only navigation/structure or a 401 Unauthorized - the actual rules text (weight/size limits, RF rules, scoring, safety) could not be retrieved this pass.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules>

### Gotchas

- ESP32-S3 has exactly one 2.4GHz radio: you cannot run ESP-NOW on a different channel than whatever WiFi STA/AP is currently locked to when both are active in APSTA mode - channel must match or ESP-NOW packets silently vanish.
- ESP-NOW is NOT immune to the crowded-arena WiFi congestion problem - it shares the same 2.4GHz spectrum/channels as every venue router and every other team's AP; it only avoids per-packet AP-association/beacon overhead, it doesn't dodge interference.
- ESP32-S3 cannot use 5GHz at all (no radio hardware for it) - any 'switch to 5GHz to dodge congestion' idea is a dead end on this chip; the only way to leave the 2.4GHz WiFi mess entirely is an add-on radio (e.g. ELRS on 900MHz).
- esp-drone (and by extension ESP-FLY, which is a fork/derivative of it) is pinned to the ESP-IDF release/v5.0 branch specifically - the current official micro-ROS ESP-IDF component targets v5.4/5.5/6.0, and current Arduino-ESP32 targets IDF 5.5. Mixing these without a deliberate version-upgrade plan will likely break the existing esp-drone codebase.
- Bluepad32 explicitly documents that ESP32-S3 (unlike the original ESP32) only supports 'a subset' of Bluetooth controllers - confirm the exact gamepad model works on S3 before buying one for this project.
- esp-drone's GitHub repo itself states it has only 'limited support' (last noted activity context: Dec 2022) - treat it as community/legacy code to build on, not an actively maintained Espressif product; expect to debug/patch it yourselves.
- OTA rollback safety in ESP-IDF depends on the new firmware explicitly calling esp_ota_mark_app_valid_cancel_rollback() after self-test - forgetting this call (or CONFIG_BOOTLOADER_APP_ROLLBACK_ENABLE being off) removes the safety net that protects against bricking the device while it's sealed inside the gondola.
- ESP-NOW's max payload is only 250 bytes on the widely-used v1 API (1470B needs the newer v2 API/IDF v5.x) - plan telemetry/command packet sizes accordingly, or explicitly opt into ESP-NOW v2.
- YouTube pages actively block/return 401 to headless scraping (both direct WebFetch and r.jina.ai reader proxy) - full video descriptions/comments for 2 of the 3 supplied videos could not be retrieved even with the reader-proxy workaround; only oEmbed title/author metadata came through reliably.
- The IAR-LARC rules PDF is embedded via what looks like a Google Drive viewer that returns 401 to automated fetches - someone will need to open it manually in a real browser (possibly needs a Google sign-in) to get the authoritative weight/size/RF rules; do not proceed on assumptions about those numbers.

### Actionable

- Open https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/rules manually in a browser and download IAR_LARC2026.pdf directly - this is the single highest-priority missing document (weight/size limits, RF/frequency rules, scoring, safety requirements all live only there).
- Also open https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/blimp-sample directly in a browser to see the 'Hardware' and 'Assembly' sections in full (automated fetch only recovered headings/one image and two embedded video links, not the body text).
- Treat https://youtu.be/Sybc0XwAaL8 ('Blimp assembly tutorial' by Lehigh Swarms Club) as a 4th reference video - it's the competition's own embedded sample and was not in the original list from the professor.
- Since the professor's PCB and the esp-drone/ESP-FLY reference codebase are native ESP-IDF pinned to the v5.0 branch, standardize the team's toolchain on native ESP-IDF (install via the current EIM tool, `winget install Espressif.EIM`, or the classic installer+export.ps1 workflow shown in older docs/videos) plus the VS Code ESP-IDF extension, rather than porting to Arduino.
- Decide early whether to keep the phone-app/WiFi-SoftAP control path (simplest, matches ESP-FLY/esp-drone default) or move the main link to ESP-NOW/ELRS for competition-day robustness in a crowded hall - these have real coexistence and channel-lock consequences that should be designed in from the start, not patched later.
- If a manual-override/teleop channel is wanted for testing or is permitted by the (still-unretrieved) rules, prototype with Bluepad32 (Bluetooth gamepad, verify the exact model against ESP32-S3's supported subset first) as the lower-effort path, and consider an ELRS 900MHz receiver only if 2.4GHz congestion in the venue turns out to be severe.
- Design the packet format now around CRTP's port:channel model (reusable, well-proven, and already the pattern esp-drone's own PC client expects) rather than inventing a new framing from scratch.
- Build the failsafe policy around 'zero thrust and let buoyancy take over' rather than an active-descent policy, but validate this against the actual competition safety rules once the PDF is retrieved.
- Get https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim running early for autonomy/multi-blimp-coordination prototyping in simulation, independent of the comms-link decision.
- Look at espressif/esp-now's built-in OTA-over-ESP-NOW example (github.com/espressif/esp-now) if the team ends up choosing ESP-NOW as the primary link - it would let OTA updates happen without ever entering WiFi STA/AP mode, sidestepping the channel-lock gotcha entirely for that use case.

### Fact-check verdicts

- **REFUTED** — IAR-LARC 2026's official 'Blimp Sample' page embeds the same Lehigh Swarms Club video the professor sent (youtu.be/Y5i3LntcNt0, 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone') plus one more video (Sybc0XwAaL8)
  - Correction: Fetched the raw page HTML directly (curl, bypassing JS rendering issues) and searched for every YouTube ID and iframe: the page contains exactly ONE embedded video iframe, youtube.com/embed/Sybc0XwAaL8 ('Blimp assembly tutorial'). Y5i3LntcNt0 does not appear anywhere in the page source. The professor's ESP-FLY-quadcopter video is NOT referenced by the organizers at all on this page. The 'de facto reference platform' framing in the original summary is not supported by the evidence.
- **REFUTED** — The competition's baseline/reference platform is effectively the ESP-FLY-derived quadcopter blimp the professor sent
  - Correction: The actual rules PDF (IAR_LARC2026.pdf, dated March 27 2026 — retrieved successfully in this pass via the Google Drive file ID embedded in the rules page, previously marked unretrievable) names an explicit 'Baseline Design' in Appendix A: 'Mochiswarm' (arxiv.org/abs/2503.03077, docs at lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/), which is a BICOPTER: ESP32 controller (generic, not stated as S3), 2 coreless/brushless DC motors + 1 steering servo (6-channel actuator capability), ~36-inch mylar/latex balloon, 1S ~500mAh LiPo. This is a materially different actuator/airframe topology (2 motors + rudder servo, no quad-motor stabilization) than the ESP-FLY quadcopter frame in the professor's video. The 'Blimp assembly tutorial' video embedded on the Blimp Sample page (Sybc0XwAaL8) almost certainly documents this bicopter, not the ESP-FLY quad — the Lehigh bicopter page independently lists the identical parts (XIAO ESP32-S3 $6.50, 2x 1102 13500KV brushless motors $10 ea, gemfan 2015 props, 2 ESCs, 36-inch ruby-red foil balloon $4, 1 micro servo, Xbox joystick for teleop testing, $83.74 base / $126.74 with accessories).
- **CONFIRMED** — (Missing from original research entirely) What do the actual competition rules say about weight/size limits, thrust, RF, autonomy, and scoring?
  - Correction: Not a correction — this fills the single largest gap the original research flagged as unretrievable. Successfully downloaded and OCR'd the real IAR_LARC2026.pdf. Key load-bearing numbers: (1) AUTONOMY IS MANDATORY — 'No manual control or remote piloting is allowed once the run has started' (Rule 1); this directly contradicts the original's actionable suggestion to use ExpressLRS/Bluepad32 'for competition-day robustness' as manual override — that is only viable for testing/practice, using it live during a scored run is an instant disqualification. (2) Thrust budget capped at 100 gf total across all motors. (3) Buoyancy must be neutral within ±10 gf net vertical force. (4) Max bounding box 1m x 1m x 1.5m (no other size limit). (5) Helium volume capped at ~1 cubic meter (a 50-inch diameter balloon). (6) Two courses (rectangular + figure-8), 4 waypoints each (red 22-inch mylar balloons tethered at 2m height), up to 3 timed loops per course, sum of best times per course = final score. (7) All sensing must be onboard (no offboard/motion-capture assist). (8) Official simulator is explicitly mochi-swarm-sim (github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim.git), cited by name in Appendix B — this is NOT just an inference from filenames, it is the organizer-mandated tool.
- **CONFIRMED** — IAR-LARC 2026 is Nov 2-6 at Universidad Militar Nueva Granada, Bogotá, running alongside LARC/LARS 2026 (Nov 4-6, same venue)
  - Correction: Verified via raw HTML text extraction (bypassing potential small-model hallucination risk from a prior rendered fetch). IAR-LARC home page states verbatim 'from Nov 2 to Nov 6 International Airship Racing at LARC (IAR-LARC) at New Granada Military University'; schedule page breaks it down as Day 1 = Nov 2 (arrival/check-in/orientation), Day 2 = Nov 3-5 (qualification matches), Day 3 = Nov 6 (finals/awards). Separately, UMNG's own mecatronica page confirms LARS/LARC 2026 (the umbrella event) runs 'November 4-6, 2026' at 'Universidad Militar Nueva Granada' in Bogotá — so these are indeed two different, overlapping (not merely inconsistently-copied) date ranges: IAR-LARC's airship days start 2 days before the main LARC/LARS event and finish together on Nov 6. This resolves the original research's open question.
- **CONFIRMED** — ESP32-S3 radio is hard-capped at 2.4GHz WiFi b/g/n + BLE5, no 5GHz silicon exists on this chip
- **CONFIRMED** — ESP-NOW hard limits: 250 bytes/packet (v1) or 1470 bytes (v2, IDF v5.x); max 20 total peers/17 encrypted (default 7)
- **CONFIRMED** — espressif/esp-drone supports ESP32/S2/S3, requires ESP-IDF release/v5.0 branch specifically, has only 'limited support' since Dec 2022
- **CONFIRMED** — CRTP packet format: 4-bit port (0-15) + 2-bit channel (0-3) header, payload <=30 bytes
  - Correction: Port list in the original is slightly incomplete — it's missing port 6 (Localization) and port 9 (Supervisor: arm/emergency-stop/state queries), which are relevant if the team reuses CRTP framing and wants an arm/e-stop channel.
- **CONFIRMED** — Bluepad32 explicitly documents that ESP32-S3 only supports 'a subset' of Bluetooth controllers vs original ESP32
- **CONFIRMED** — Electric UI logic-analyzer benchmark: ESP-NOW 5.6/5.8/24ms, WiFi-TCP ~6ms flat, BLE Bluedroid 14.6/21/31ms, nRF24 2Mbps 0.3/1.9/23ms (12B/128B/1024B)
- **CONFIRMED** — MAVLink heartbeat: 1Hz nominal on RF links, link considered lost after ~4-5 missed heartbeats
  - Correction: Worth noting this is documented as a convention/example for RF telemetry links, not a protocol-mandated hard rule — MAVLink itself doesn't fix the rate or timeout, so the original's framing as 'reusable failsafe-timing prior art' is accurate but should be labeled 'common convention', not 'spec'.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — ESP-FLY parts cost ~$37.30 total (XIAO ESP32-S3, MPU6050, 4 coreless motors, 150-250mAh LiPo, SI2300 MOSFETs)
  - Correction: Could not independently verify the $37.30 self-sourced BOM figure — YouTube's full video description still resists automated retrieval even via the r.jina.ai reader proxy in this pass (matches original's own caveat). However, found a primary source the original missed: the video's pinned creator comment links an official assembled 'ESP-FLY Drone Kit' sold directly by Seeed Studio, confirmed powered by the 'Seeed Studio XIAO ESP32-S3', priced at $59.99 (10+ units: $49.99) — i.e., a real, sourced number exists but it's for the ready-built kit, not the raw-parts DIY total, and it's noticeably higher than the $37.30 figure. Treat $37.30 as unverified/DIY-estimate and $59.99/$49.99 as the confirmed retail alternative.

### Added by fact-checker

- CRITICAL RULE (missed entirely by the original research, which marked the rules PDF unretrievable): full autonomy is mandatory during the timed run — 'No manual control or remote piloting is allowed once the run has started' (IAR_LARC2026.pdf, Rule 1). Any ExpressLRS/Bluepad32/gamepad teleop channel must be strictly a testing/practice tool and must be verifiably disabled or absent during the actual scored attempts, or the run is invalid.
- Numeric competition envelope the professor's team must design against (from IAR_LARC2026.pdf, primary source, previously unretrieved): total thrust <=100 gf across all motors; neutral buoyancy within +/-10 gf; max bounding box 1m x 1m x 1.5m; helium volume <= ~1 m^3 (a 50-inch diameter balloon); two courses (4m x 5m rectangle and a figure-eight, both roughly 10m x 5m footprints per the rules diagrams), 4 waypoints each defined by red 22-inch mylar balloons tethered at 2m height; up to 3 timed loops per course; total score = sum of best time on each of the 2 courses (lowest wins); all navigation sensing must be onboard (no offboard motion capture / external tracking assist permitted).
- The rules PDF explicitly names 'Mochiswarm' (a bicopter, not a quadcopter) as the competition's Appendix-A baseline design, with its own paper (arXiv:2503.03077) and build docs (lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/) — teams are 'encouraged to iterate' beyond it with PLA/PETG, balsa, or carbon fiber, meaning a quadcopter-derived (ESP-FLY/esp-drone) approach is allowed but is a deliberate deviation from, not an instance of, the organizers' own reference design; this should be a conscious engineering decision, not an assumption.
- mochi-swarm-sim is not merely 'likely usable' — it is the officially designated simulator, named and linked directly in Appendix B of the rules PDF as the tool teams should use to validate control/sensing/navigation before physical deployment.
- The rules document is dated March 27, 2026 and the competition chair is confirmed as Diego Salazar D'Antonio (dantonio@oakland.edu, Oakland University), consistent with the original's organizer claim; it also references a Slack channel (join.slack.com link, generic/non-working invite in the PDF) for competition communication that the team should try to join.
- Waypoint touch mechanics matter for airframe design: a waypoint only counts if the airship 'physically touches' the tethered balloon (passing near does not count), and Rule 17 requires all external/contact surfaces be blunt (no sharp edges or rigid protrusions) specifically to avoid puncturing the mylar waypoint balloons — this is a hard constraint on nose/bumper design, separate from the general safety rule.

---

## failure-modes (domain)  
**RELIABILITY: medium**

Research focus: failure modes/troubleshooting for DIY autonomous blimps and competition-day ops, for a Colombia team (UMNG, Bogotá, ~2600m) entering IAR-LARC 2026 (Nov 2026). WebSearch was exhausted session-wide before any query for this task ran (200/200 used by other parallel subagents), so all findings come from WebFetch + Bash/curl against known/discoverable URLs (GitHub REST API, arxiv, official competition sites, Hackaday, YouTube oEmbed) — no Reddit/RCGroups access was possible (old.reddit.com is blocked for WebFetch in this environment). Despite that, I recovered unusually strong primary sources: (1) the actual IAR-LARC 2026 rulebook PDF (fully autonomous, ±10gf buoyancy tolerance, 100gf total thrust cap, 1m³ helium/50" balloon cap, 1m×1m×1.5m bbox, must physically touch tethered 22" red mylar balloons at 2m height, 2 courses × 3 loops, onboard-sensing-only); (2) the rulebook's own cited baseline design — Lehigh's "MochiSwarm" bicopter blimp (XIAO ESP32-S3, 2× brushless+servo bicopter stacks, 50" balloon = 120g net buoyancy, 18.7g laser-cut foam-core frame) — with its arxiv paper (2503.03077) reporting real deployment failures (AC-unit turbulence up to 1 m/s, blimps getting stuck near AC units/cables/walls, and only 17-42% task success rates even from the reference team); (3) the actual GitHub repo behind reference video #3 (BenGreenberg07/esp-fly-blimp, made by the "Lehigh Swarms Club," same lab) with a ready-made symptom→diagnosis troubleshooting table; and (4) ~15 real GitHub issues on espressif/esp-drone (the codebase the professor's PCBs likely derive from) documenting brownout-on-motor-spin-up, MPU6050 I2C bus lockup/cyclic reboot, FreeRTOS stack overflow reboots, and UDP telemetry-queue-full freezes, each with symptom/root-cause/fix from real users. I also physics-derived two things explicitly requested: battery mass loss during discharge is physically negligible (~10⁻¹³ kg, i.e. not a real trim driver — the real trim drivers are helium diffusion, temperature, and condensation), and Bogotá's 2600m altitude cuts air density to ~77% of sea level, which simultaneously cuts buoyant lift-per-volume by the same ~23% AND cuts propeller thrust-per-RPM by ~23% (needing ~14% more RPM / ~47% more power for equal thrust) — a compounding, altitude-specific risk not visible in any sea-level reference build.

### Specs

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| IAR buoyancy tolerance | ±10 grams-force from neutral |
| IAR total thrust cap | 100 grams-force across all motors combined (servos excluded) |
| IAR helium volume cap | ~1 cubic meter (50-inch diameter balloon equivalent) |
| IAR max vehicle bounding box | 1m x 1m x 1.5m |
| IAR waypoint balloon | 22-inch red mylar, ~50-60cm inflated diameter, tethered to ground, center at 2m height |
| IAR course dimensions | Two courses (rectangular path and figure-eight/infinity path), each ~10m x 5m field with a 2m start region, 4 waypoints per course |
| IAR loop/scoring structure | Up to 3 loops per course, best valid time per course recorded, total = sum of both courses' best times, lowest total wins |
| Reference baseline balloon lift | 50-inch diameter spherical foil balloon = 120 grams net buoyancy (measured at Lehigh, PA, near sea level) |
| Reference baseline frame | Laser-cut foam-core board frame, 18.7g, deforms under external force below 2 N |
| Reference baseline MCU loop rate | XIAO ESP32-S3 flight controller runs at 200 Hz on 240 MHz dual-core; 2.2cm x 1.7cm x 0.4cm package; 11 usable GPIO (4 PWM actuator / 2 I2C / 2 UART / 1 battery-sense) |
| ESP-NOW link spec (as cited in paper) | Up to 480 m range, 512 kbps peer-to-peer |
| Measured real-world indoor wind disturbance | AC/HVAC-driven turbulence up to ~1 m/s in a 20m x 15m industrial highbay |
| Reference platform real-world task success rate | 4 blimps: 2/8 and 3/12 successful deliveries; 8 blimps: 5/12 successful deliveries (5-minute trials) |
| Reference platform bicopter BOM core cost | ~$83.74 (motors, ESCs, servo, ESP32S3, balloon, battery kit, elastic); optional sensor suite (ultrasonic+barometer+9DOF IMU) ~$49 extra |
| esp-drone custom-hardware brownout root cause example | Power-path rectifier diode voltage drop measured 0.7V (1N4007) vs 0.2V (BAT60B Schottky, per official schematic); reboot occurred at ~700mA measured consumption on a 3.7V 1S 600mAh 70C battery |
| CircuitDigest reference quadcopter specs | ESP32-WROOM + MPU6050 + 4x '720' coreless motors + SI2302 MOSFET drivers + 1300mAh 30C 1S LiPo, ~60g AUW, ~5-7 min flight time, WiFi range ~30-50m open air / 15-25m indoors |
| Air density at Bogotá altitude (2600m), physics-derived | ≈0.77 x sea-level density (≈0.95 kg/m³ vs 1.225 kg/m³); pressure ratio ≈0.73 |
| Battery discharge mass loss, physics-derived | ~4e-14 to 4e-13 kg for a several-Wh LiPo discharge (E=mc²) — not measurable, not a real trim factor |
| esp-fly-blimp identified plant time constant | Forward drag time constant ~5-7 seconds (lead time needed for thrust-command response) |
| Blimpduino v2 reference numbers | ~40 min flight time on 500 mAh battery; Denver builder needed 36in balloon (vs stock) due to altitude (~1600m); one build abandoned when helium fill cost exceeded $200 |

### Key findings

- **[high]** IAR-LARC 2026 official rulebook: buoyancy must be neutral within ±10 grams-force; total thrust across all motors capped at 100 gf; helium volume capped at ~1 m³ (50-inch balloon equivalent); vehicle bounding box max 1m×1m×1.5m; fully autonomous, onboard-sensing-only (no external localization/human intervention once a run starts); waypoints are 22-inch red mylar balloons, ~50-60cm inflated, tethered at 2m height, must be PHYSICALLY TOUCHED; 2 courses (10m×5m rectangle, and a figure-8) with a 2m start region, 3 loops per course, best time per course summed for final score.
  - Downloaded and text-extracted directly from the primary-source PDF at https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/rules/IAR_LARC2026.pdf (dated March 27, 2026, 7 pages, Competition Chair Diego S. D'Antonio, dantonio@oakland.edu). This is the actual rulebook, not a summary.
  - <https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/rules/IAR_LARC2026.pdf>
- **[high]** The rulebook's own official baseline design is Lehigh's 'MochiSwarm' bicopter blimp: XIAO ESP32-S3, 2x coreless/brushless motor+servo actuator stacks (bicopter layout), 36" mylar/latex spherical envelope, 1S 500mAh LiPo, ~$83.74 core BOM (+$43 optional joystick/USB, +$49 optional ultrasonic/barometer/9DOF-IMU sensors).
  - Rulebook Appendix A explicitly names this as the baseline and links docs; BOM extracted from https://lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/. Line items: 2x 1102/13500kv brushless motors $20, 2x Gemfan 2015 props $1.25, 2x motor drivers/ESCs $20, 1x micro servo $3.50, 1x XIAO ESP32S3 $6.50, 1x 36in foil balloon $4.00, 1S battery kit(5)+charger $19.00, elastic $9.49.
  - <https://lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/>
- **[high]** The reference platform's own published paper documents real deployment failure modes: HVAC/AC units created 'unknown wind turbulence up to 1 m/s' in a 20m×15m industrial highbay test that visibly affected blimp motion; individual blimps got stuck near AC units, tether/goal cables, and uneven walls; even with the reference team's own design, autonomous pickup-and-delivery success rates were only 2/8, 3/12, and 5/12 targets across three trials (~17-42%).
  - MochiSwarm: A testbed for robotic blimps in realistic environments, Xu/Vu/D'Antonio/Saldaña, arXiv:2503.03077 (Lehigh AIRLab). Also gives: 50-inch spherical foil balloon → 120g net buoyancy; frame laser-cut foam-core board, 18.7g, deforms above 2N; XIAO ESP32S3 runs flight control at 200Hz on 240MHz dual-core, 11 GPIO budget (4 PWM/2 I2C/2 UART/1 battery-sense); ESP-NOW range up to 480m at 512kbps; blimp is passively stable in roll/pitch so only height+yaw are actively PD-controlled.
  - <https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03077>
- **[high]** Reference video #3 (Y5i3LntcNt0, 'ESP Blimp: Building a Blimp based on the ESPFLY drone') is by channel 'Lehigh Swarms Club' and its build is almost certainly github.com/BenGreenberg07/esp-fly-blimp, which ships an actual symptom→diagnosis troubleshooting table.
  - Table includes: 'Sinks during flight'→buoyancy loss or battery sag (check zff feedforward ≤ vertMaxPwm ceiling); 'Random reboot ~3s in'→foreign ESP-NOW traffic/FRAME_MAGIC mismatch; 'Wider circles than drawn'→forward-thrust trim (fwdMaxN) too high, not steering gain; 'Reversed turning'→software flip switch. Also documents: 5-7 second forward-drag time constant (lead time needed before the vehicle responds), no braking/in-place-rotation with the 2-forward+1up+1down brushed-motor layout (it can only orbit at min turn radius), and 'an off-centre mount gives a constant sideways drift that no controller can remove.' It uses OptiTrack mocap + a laptop for autonomy in the lab — which is NOT legal under IAR rule 15 (onboard-sensing-only), so this exact architecture cannot be copied as-is for competition day.
  - <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BenGreenberg07/esp-fly-blimp/main/README.md>
- **[high]** Real GitHub issues on espressif/esp-drone (the codebase family the professor's ESP32-S3 PCBs likely derive from or resemble) document exactly the electronics failure modes the task asked about, each with symptom/root cause/fix from real users.
  - Issue #85 'Custom ESP drone hardware power problem': ESP32C3 reboots when motors exceed ~20% power on a 3.7V 1S 600mAh 70C battery; traced to power-path diode voltage drop (1N4007 measured 0.7V drop vs official schematic's BAT60B Schottky measured 0.2V drop) and separately to counterfeit/clone MIC5219 3.3V LDO output noise (genuine Microchip part fixed it). Issue #105 'MPU6050 I2C latch up situation (reboot, sometimes cyclically)': on mid/low battery charge the IMU's SDA line latches low, ESP32 can't generate an I2C START condition, MCU cyclically reboots until battery is disconnected; suspected weak 10k pull-up/EMI/clone IMU; fixes = replace IMU or firmware bus-recovery. Issue #49: FreeRTOS 'Timer Svc' task stack overflow causing reboot loop, fixed by doubling 'FreeRTOS timer task stack size' in menuconfig. Issue #84/#114: cfclient ground-station telemetry freezes 3-5s after connect, root-caused by 'CRTP RX queue full' assert when an uninitialized firmware module doesn't drain its queue (still open/unresolved by multiple independent reporters). Issue #90: ESP32-S3 WiFi AP simply invisible on a custom board, fixed by changing GPIO pin assignment (implying a strapping-pin conflict). Issue #116: a variance-calculation bug in sensors_mpu6050_hm5883L_ms5611.c (wrong GYRO_VARIANCE_THRESHOLD_X math) can spuriously block the 'ready to fly' gate.
  - <https://github.com/espressif/esp-drone/issues/85>
- **[high]** CircuitDigest DIY WiFi drone article (a source the professor sent) is a QUADCOPTER, not a blimp, built on plain ESP32-WROOM + MPU6050 + 720 coreless motors + SI2302 MOSFET drivers + 1300mAh 30C 1S LiPo; its comment section documents brownout/reboot from WiFi-TX+motor-startup current sag on undersized-C-rating batteries, counterfeit-IMU I2C failures, and ~5-7 min flight time at ~60g AUW.
  - Confirms this article is a components/wiring reference only (motor driver topology, IMU wiring, WiFi hotspot control scheme), not blimp-specific; its battery-sag/brownout lesson directly parallels the esp-drone issue #85 finding above.
  - <https://circuitdigest.com/microcontroller-projects/DIY-wifi-controlled-drone>
- **[high]** TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim is a MuJoCo simulator built by a Lehigh Swarm Lab student (bio confirms 'Lehigh University Class of 2025, Lehigh Swarm Lab'), modeling a slalom/gate-touch race almost identical to the official IAR task: 6+ colored tethered gate balloons on multi-segment rope 'necks,' a differential 2-thrust-motor+1-servo bicopter control law, and a random-walk/resample wind 'turbulence' disturbance field (base XY force 0.01-0.10 N-scale, scale drifting 0.85x-1.45x with a ~1.8s time constant, random direction ±40deg) explicitly built into the sim to stress-test controllers against gusts.
  - Fetched directly: src/controller.py, src/robot/differential.py (PD height/yaw control with integrator clamps: KPZ .65/KDZ .45, KPYAW 1.0 with yaw-integral clamp ±0.30, FX limit ±0.35, FZ limit ±1.4, servo angle limit ±1.45 rad, thrust-total cap 0.72), src/disturbance/turbulence.py, src/preferences.py, and models/mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml (each gate balloon modeled with MuJoCo gravcomp=1.22, i.e. 22% net positive buoyancy in the sim). This is a genuinely usable dev tool for pre-tuning control gains before real flights, and its gate/course XML is a good template for practicing the exact IAR touch-the-balloon task in software first.
  - <https://github.com/TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim>
- **[medium]** Hackaday's own recent (2026) blimp-hybrid project explicitly engineered around 'the balloon can fail' as a first-class failure mode: BEAVIS→JANUS is a morphing quad/blimp that pivots its motors so it can still fly as a plain quadcopter if the balloon fails, after field deployment in Kenya; it claims >1hr endurance vs <10min for a pure quad.
  - Secondary source (Hackaday's write-up of a Hackaday Europe 2026 talk), not a paper/repo I read directly — treat specific numbers as reported-by-journalist, not independently verified.
  - <https://hackaday.com/2026/07/23/hackaday-europe-2026-half-quad-half-blimp-test-fly-survive/>
- **[medium]** Blimpduino v2 (established open hobby blimp platform, ARM M0 + ESP8266, 9-axis IMU, ToF altimeter, barometer) — community reports confirm altitude reduces achievable lift: a builder in Denver (~1600m) needed a larger 36in balloon than stock specifically because of reduced lift at altitude; helium fill cost 'exceeding $200' caused at least one build to be abandoned; flight time ~40min on a 500mAh battery.
  - Secondary source (Hackaday article + its comment thread), anecdotal not measured, but directly corroborates the altitude-buoyancy physics reasoning below at a lower altitude (1600m) than Bogotá's 2600m — meaning our team's effect should be proportionally larger.
  - <https://hackaday.com/2018/11/27/blimpduino-hits-version-2/>
- **[high]** PHYSICS REASONING (not sourced from a specific citation, standard-atmosphere/ideal-gas derivation): battery mass loss during a LiPo discharge cycle is physically negligible (~10⁻¹³ kg via E=mc² for a few Wh of energy released) — the drone does NOT get meaningfully lighter as the battery drains. The real drivers of the day-to-day/match-to-match trim drift the task asked about are (a) helium diffusing out through the envelope over hours, (b) ambient/venue temperature changes expanding or contracting the gas ('superheat' effect), and (c) moisture condensing on the envelope surface adding mass — not battery discharge.
  - This is a physics derivation I performed, not a web-sourced claim. Energy released by even a full LiPo discharge (order 1-10 Wh = 3600-36000 J) corresponds to a mass-energy-equivalent loss on the order of 4e-14 to 4e-13 kg — about 13-14 orders of magnitude below anything that could matter for a ±10gf buoyancy tolerance. LiPo cells are sealed and don't vent reaction products (unlike combustion), so essentially none of the pack's mass leaves the pouch during normal discharge.
- **[medium]** PHYSICS REASONING (International Standard Atmosphere barometric formula, computed this session): at Bogotá's ~2600m elevation, air density is approximately 77% of sea-level density (≈0.95 kg/m³ vs 1.225 kg/m³), and this affects BOTH sides of the buoyancy/thrust budget simultaneously: (1) net buoyant lift per unit envelope volume drops to ~77% of a sea-level design's value (because both air and helium densities scale down together with ambient pressure, so their difference shrinks proportionally) — meaning the reference '50in balloon = 120g lift' figure from the MochiSwarm paper would likely only yield roughly ~90-95g of lift at Bogotá altitude for the same envelope size; (2) propeller thrust at a fixed RPM also drops to ~77% (thrust ∝ air density), requiring ~14% more RPM and roughly ~45-50% more electrical power for the same thrust, which directly compounds the brownout/motor-heating risks found in the esp-drone issues above.
  - Derivation: ISA formula ρ(h)=ρ0*(1-Lh/T0)^(gM/RL -1) with L=0.0065K/m, T0=288.15K, g=9.80665, M=0.0289644kg/mol, R=8.3144598 gives ρ(2600m)/ρ0≈0.773. Pressure ratio from the same model ≈0.73 (≈740hPa), consistent with commonly-cited Bogotá average station pressure figures (this specific pressure figure itself is not sourced from a URL in this session — mark as reasonably well-known but unverified-in-session). Buoyancy and thrust scaling conclusions follow directly from Archimedes' principle and standard propeller momentum theory (thrust∝ρn²D⁴); high confidence in the methodology, medium confidence in the precise percentages since I did not cross-check against a measured Bogotá density-altitude table this session.
- **[high]** There is a small but real discrepancy between the two organizer sites on the 2026 competition dates: the IAR-LARC (Oakland) site states 'November 2-6, 2026' while the UMNG mecatronica LARC page states event dates 'November 4-6, 2026' for the same event.
  - Both fetched directly from the two official pages in this session.
  - <https://sites.google.com/oakland.edu/iar-larc/home>
- **[medium]** The only venue description found on the UMNG competition site (venue.html) is 'Aula Máxima Universidad Militar Nueva Granada,' a 450-person-capacity auditorium/assembly-hall at Carrera 11 # 101-80, Bogotá — this is very likely the LARS academic-symposium venue (paper presentations), NOT the actual IAR flight arena, since a 450-seat auditorium is incompatible with the rulebook's described 10m×5m courses with 2m-high tethered balloons. No public source found states the actual flight arena's dimensions, ceiling height, floor surface, or HVAC situation.
  - Inference based on combining venue.html's auditorium description with the rulebook's arena diagrams; not explicitly confirmed as two different rooms by any source — should be verified directly with organizers.
  - <https://mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/venue.html>

### Gotchas

- IAR rule 15 requires 'all sensing used for navigation and task execution' to be performed ONBOARD the robot — this rules out the exact architecture used in two of the three reference videos/repos (OptiTrack mocap in esp-fly-blimp, offboard visual-servoing base-station patterns common in swarm demos). Any lab prototyping done with external tracking must be treated as a dev-only crutch, not a competition-legal design.
- IAR rule 11's ±10 gf neutral-buoyancy tolerance is TIGHT relative to a hobbyist's usual practice of trimming 'slightly negative' for controllability margin (as esp-fly-blimp's README explicitly recommends) — the team needs a real ballast/trim procedure with gram-scale precision (a cheap 0.1g-resolution scale, not eyeballing), and must re-trim between the inflation room and the actual arena if there's a temperature difference.
- Vehicle Size Constraints rule 12 ('no explicit size limitations') and rule 14 ('max 1m×1m×1.5m bounding box') appear side-by-side in the same rulebook section and read as mutually inconsistent as written — worth a clarifying email to the competition chair rather than assuming which one governs.
- IAR total-thrust budget is only 100 grams-force across ALL motors combined (servos excluded) — this is a hard constraint that must be checked against the actual max static thrust of whatever motor/prop combo is chosen, especially given the ~23% thrust-per-RPM penalty expected at Bogotá's altitude (see altitude finding above): a motor/prop combo that hits 100gf at sea level on a bench will likely fall short at the actual venue.
- esp-drone's most common real-world electronics failure (per issue #85) was traced to a SPECIFIC PART CHOICE (rectifier diode vs Schottky diode in the power path, and genuine vs clone LDO) rather than a topology error — the professor's existing custom PCBs should be checked component-by-component against these two exact culprits before assuming a redesign is needed.
- The reference platform's own paper reports only 17-42% end-to-end task success rate in real deployment, even from the lab that designed the hardware/software — this is a realistic baseline expectation to set for the team, not a sign that a working prototype is 'broken' if it doesn't succeed every run.
- Both the mochi-swarm-sim turbulence model and the MochiSwarm paper's real HVAC measurement (~1 m/s) suggest the team should budget explicit thrust margin for indoor air currents, and that this margin is now smaller in absolute RPM/current headroom because of the altitude thrust penalty — the two problems compound rather than being independent risks.
- esp-drone/cfclient's UDP telemetry queue-full freeze (issues #84/#114) is, per the GitHub thread evidence, still UNRESOLVED as of the most recent comments — if the team's ground-station/telemetry link is derived from this same crazyflie-lib-python/cfclient stack, do not assume it will be reliable for a live competition run without independent stress-testing well before the event.
- WebSearch was fully exhausted (200/200) before this subagent could run a single query — none of the requested Reddit/RCGroups/Hackaday-comment mining was done via search; everything here came from direct URL fetches and the GitHub REST API via curl. Treat the 'gaps' list below as a real, not just formal, limitation.

### Actionable

- PRIORITY 1 — Buoyancy budget vs altitude: before finalizing envelope size, compute expected net lift at Bogotá's ~2600m (~77% of the sea-level lift the reference '50in=120g' figure implies) and size the envelope (up to the 1 m³/50in-equivalent rule cap) and gondola mass budget so the ±10gf tolerance is still achievable with margin, not exactly at the limit.
- PRIORITY 2 — Audit the existing custom PCBs' power path NOW for the two specific root causes found in esp-drone issue #85: (a) confirm the flyback/reverse-polarity or charge-path diode is a low-drop Schottky (e.g. BAT60-family) not a generic 0.7V-drop rectifier diode, and (b) confirm the 3.3V LDO is a genuine part (counterfeit/clone LDOs were reported to add output noise causing reboots) — do this before blaming firmware for any brownout seen on motor spin-up.
- PRIORITY 3 — Design the motor/prop selection to hit 100gf total thrust AT ALTITUDE, not at a sea-level bench test; budget ~40-50% more electrical headroom (current, battery C-rating, thermal path) than a sea-level design would need for the same thrust, since propeller thrust-per-RPM drops with air density.
- PRIORITY 4 — Decide the onboard-only perception/autonomy architecture early: study MochiSwarm's actual onboard approach (NiclaVision daughterboard doing color/shape balloon detection via I2C/UART to the main ESP32-S3, not an offboard laptop) as the competition-legal template, rather than the mocap-based esp-fly-blimp lab setup.
- PRIORITY 5 — Because the task requires physically touching a small tethered balloon (not just passing near it) and the most detailed open reference control code documents an inability to brake or hold station with a simple 2-forward+1-up+1-down layout, evaluate whether a 4-motor (or otherwise braking-capable) thrust layout is needed for reliable final-approach/contact behavior, budgeting within the 100gf total-thrust cap.
- Get written clarification from the IAR-LARC organizers (dantonio@oakland.edu) on: (a) the exact competition dates (Nov 2 vs Nov 4 discrepancy between the two official sites), (b) the actual flight-arena venue/ceiling height/floor/HVAC (distinct from the 450-seat Aula Máxima auditorium found on the public site), (c) whether helium cylinders may be brought/rented on-site or must be sourced locally in Bogotá, and (d) how rules 12 and 14 (size constraints) are meant to be reconciled.
- Before the event, deliberately stress-test the telemetry/ground-station link for the queue-overflow/freeze failure mode documented in esp-drone issues #84/#114, since it was reported as unresolved upstream.
- Use TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim (MuJoCo) to pre-tune PD gains and the gate-touch approach behavior in software against its randomized turbulence disturbance before committing flight time to real-world tuning — it already models a course extremely close to the real rulebook's rectangle/figure-8 waypoint task.
- Build a physical, written pre-match checklist and field kit informed by the failure catalogue above (spare envelope, gram-scale for trim, spare IMU/LDO/diode parts given the specific failure parts identified, and a battery-sag test procedure) rather than relying on memory on competition day.

### Fact-check verdicts

- **CONFIRMED** — IAR-LARC 2026 rulebook: buoyancy ±10gf; total thrust cap 100gf (servos excluded); helium volume cap ~1 m³ (50-inch balloon equivalent); bounding box 1m×1m×1.5m; waypoints are 22-inch red mylar balloons, ~50-60cm inflated, tethered at 2m height to balloon center, must be physically touched (near-miss doesn't count).
  - Correction: Exact match. Verified by downloading the actual PDF (curl) and extracting text with `pdftotext -layout` (WebFetch itself fails on this file — 13.2MB exceeds WebFetch's 10MB cap, and Read's PDF media path got silently stripped twice by a session media-request limit; pdftotext was the only path that worked). Full rule text: Rule 10 '~1 cubic meter of helium'; Rule 11 'within a tolerance of ±10 g (grams-force)'; Rule 13 '100 grams-force (gf)... Servos are not considered propulsion motors'; Rule 14 '1 m×1 m×1.5 m'; Rules 21-22 '22-inch balloons... approximate diameter of 50–60 cm... height of 2 m... to the center of the balloon'; Rule 3 'Passing near a waypoint without contact does not count.'
- **CONFIRMED** — Rules 12 and 14 are mutually inconsistent as written (one says no size limit, the other caps the bbox at 1x1x1.5m) — flagged by original researcher as an inference worth a clarifying email.
  - Correction: This is not just an inference — it is a verified, literal textual contradiction in the primary source. Rule 12 ('Vehicle Size Constraints'): 'There are no explicit size limitations on the airship.' Rule 14 ('Robot Size Constraints'): 'The maximum allowable dimensions of the airship are 1 m×1 m×1.5 m.' Two separately-numbered, differently-titled rules directly conflict two sections apart. Upgrade confidence: email the chair (dantonio@oakland.edu) before designing to the wrong constraint.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Competition dates: IAR-LARC/Oakland site says 'November 2-6, 2026'; UMNG mecatronica site says 'November 4-6, 2026' — a cross-site discrepancy.
  - Correction: Confirmed a discrepancy exists, but it's worse than 'two sites disagree' — UMNG's OWN site is internally inconsistent. mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/index.html states 'November 4-6, 2026' (as part of '26th LARC / 23rd LARS'), while mecatronica.umng.edu.co/index.html/larc.html (same domain) states 'From November 2 - 6, 2026', matching the Oakland/IAR-LARC site's 'November 2-6, 2026'. Since 2 of 3 fetched pages say Nov 2-6, that is more likely correct, but the professor's own institution's homepage shows the wrong date — get it in writing from dantonio@oakland.edu before booking travel.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — 3 loops per course (interpreted from rulebook: 3 loops, 2 courses, best time per course summed).
  - Correction: Rule 5 (General Rules, stated once, before the two courses are even introduced) says only 'up to three loops in a sequence... The best valid completion time across all loops will be recorded' — it never says 'per course' explicitly. Rule 6 then separately says 'For each course, the best valid completion time across all attempts is recorded... Total = sum of these two times,' which supports (but doesn't guarantee) a per-course reading of the 3-loop allowance. Treat 'up to 3 loops per course' as the most likely reading, not a rule-text certainty — worth the same clarifying email as the size-limit conflict.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — The rulebook's official baseline design (MochiSwarm bicopter) uses a Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 and 2x brushless 1102/13500kv motors ($10 each).
  - Correction: The BOM numbers ($10/motor, $6.50 XIAO ESP32S3, $4.00 36in balloon, $19 battery kit, $9.49 elastic, $83.74 base total) are confirmed exactly against lehighblimpgroup.github.io/BlimpSwarm/bicopter/ — that part of the claim is accurate. BUT the rulebook's OWN Appendix A text (the actual document a team would read first) describes the baseline generically and inconsistently with its own linked reference: it says 'ESP32 Microcontroller board' (not XIAO ESP32-S3 specifically) and 'Coreless brushed DC motors (x2)' — brushed, not brushless, contradicting both the Lehigh BOM ('Brushless Motor') and the arXiv paper's 'brushless rotor' description. A team following the rulebook's own wording could mistakenly source brushed coreless motors instead of the actual 1102/13500kv brushless units the reference design uses. Flag this gap to the team before ordering parts.
- **NEEDS-NUANCE** — Reference baseline balloon lift is 120g net buoyancy (50-inch balloon), used as the buoyancy-budget baseline for the team's payload/mass planning (esp. combined with the altitude derating).
  - Correction: IMPORTANT, load-bearing correction: the 120g figure is for a 50-INCH research-testbed balloon (arXiv:2503.03077's own MochiSwarm platform — the competition's maximum allowed size). But the rulebook's own recommended baseline build (Appendix A) and the Lehigh bicopter BOM both specify a 36-INCH balloon ('approx. 36-inch diameter'; BOM line item '36-ruby-red-foil-circle-balloon $4.00') — a materially smaller envelope. Buoyant volume scales with diameter cubed: (36/50)³ ≈ 0.373, so a 36in balloon should yield roughly only ~40-45g of net buoyancy at sea level (not 120g) — before any altitude derating. Combined with the ~77% air-density factor at Bogotá's 2600m already noted in the original research, the 36in baseline balloon likely nets only ~30-35g of usable lift at the venue. This is a much tighter payload/electronics/frame mass budget than '120g of lift' implies, and it directly affects go/no-go decisions on battery size, frame material, and sensor payload for anyone building to the rulebook's actual recommended baseline rather than the larger research platform.
- **REFUTED** — MochiSwarm real-world task success rates were 2/8, 3/12, and 5/12 targets across three trials, i.e. roughly '17-42%'.
  - Correction: The three ratios themselves are confirmed exactly against the paper ('4 blimps deliver successfully 2 of the 8 targets and 3 of the 12 targets, and the 8 blimps deliver successfully 5 of the 12 targets'), each trial lasting 5 minutes — but the stated range is arithmetically wrong. 2/8=25%, 3/12=25%, 5/12=41.7%. The correct range is ~25-42%, not ~17-42% (nothing in the paper corresponds to 17%; that number doesn't match any of the three reported ratios). Use 25-42% as the realistic success-rate baseline to set expectations with the team.
- **CONFIRMED** — esp-drone GitHub issue #85 documents a real brownout-on-motor-spin-up failure traced to (a) a lossy rectifier diode (1N4007, 0.7V drop) vs. the official schematic's low-drop Schottky (BAT60B, 0.2V drop), and (b) clone/counterfeit MIC5219 3.3V LDOs adding output noise that caused reboots; genuine Microchip MIC5219 fixed it.
  - Correction: Verified verbatim against the live GitHub issue and its comment thread via the GitHub REST API. Reporter (sasha240100): 'the voltage drop of that diode I measured was 0.7V... 1N4007... instead they use a BAT60B Schottky diode... I measured as 0.2V.' Commenter jobitjoseph: 'change your 3.3V LDO. We had similar issues. The drone works fine with LDO from Microchip(MIC5219). But when we used MIC5219 from another brand we had similar issues... excessive noise on the LDO's output.' Issue is on an ESP32-C3-based custom board, still open.
- **CONFIRMED** — esp-drone GitHub issue #105 documents MPU6050 I2C bus lockup (SDA latched low, MCU can't generate a START condition) causing cyclic reboots on mid/low battery charge, attributed to weak pull-up/EMI/clone-IMU.
  - Correction: Confirmed verbatim, plus one important addition the original research missed: a linked PR/commit on the same issue (user zhongchaoesp) identifies a SEPARATE, purely-firmware root cause for MPU6050-related reboots: 'mpu6050GetDeviceID() returning 0, which triggers an assert(0)... in rare cases the first device ID read returns 0, but subsequent reads succeed... a short delay before the return statement mitigates this.' So there are two independent candidate causes for this failure mode — (1) hardware (weak pull-up/EMI/clone IMU, per the original finding) and (2) a firmware race condition in the device-ID read with no retry logic — and a fix should address both, not just swap hardware.
- **CONFIRMED** — esp-fly-blimp (github.com/BenGreenberg07/esp-fly-blimp, ESP32-S3-based) uses OptiTrack motion capture for localization, which would violate IAR Rule 15 (onboard-sensing-only) if copied as-is for the competition.
  - Correction: Confirmed directly from the repo's own README, including an explicit architecture diagram: 'OptiTrack ──Wi-Fi──▶ Laptop ──USB──▶ XIAO ESP32-C6 ──ESP-NOW──▶ ESP32-S3 (blimp)'. Important nuance the original research didn't spell out: per Rule 1 ('Onboard or offboard computation is permitted'), the OFFBOARD COMPUTE side of this architecture is actually already rules-legal — and per the README, the real-time guidance/control math runs onboard the ESP32-S3 firmware itself ('blimp_guidance.c'), with the laptop only relaying position and gain changes. The only non-compliant piece is the SENSOR itself (OptiTrack's external camera-based pose estimate) — meaning the fix needed is smaller than a full redesign: swap the position source for an onboard one (onboard camera/color-blob detector, IR beacon triangulation, etc.), the firmware control loop can likely stay as-is.
- **CONFIRMED** — TongshuWu1/mochi-swarm-sim's control code contains specific PD/clamp gains: KPZ=0.65, KDZ=0.45, KPYAW=1.0, yaw-integral clamp ±0.30, FX limit ±0.35, FZ limit ±1.4, servo angle limit ±1.45 rad, thrust-total cap 0.72, and each gate balloon modeled with MuJoCo gravcomp=1.22.
  - Correction: Every single value confirmed exactly by fetching and grepping the actual source files (src/preferences.py, src/robot/differential.py, models/mochi_BlimpRace_balloon_course.xml) directly from the repo this session. Note: the repo's top-level README is only 19 lines (install/run instructions) and does not itself describe these files/values — they are real but had to be verified in the source, not the README, so anyone skimming just the README would not find this detail.
- **CONFIRMED** — Hackaday's BEAVIS→JANUS morphing quad/blimp project claims >1hr endurance vs <10min for a plain quadcopter, and was field-deployed in Kenya.
  - Correction: Confirmed via direct fetch of the article (not just a search snippet). Quotes: 'Endurance times of well over an hour were possible with this build, compared to maybe less than ten minutes for a comparable pure multirotor' and 'The build was eventually deployed in Kenya to aid in ecological data collection for conservation efforts.' Still correctly flagged as a secondary (journalist-reported) source, not a paper/repo with raw data — treat the exact minute/hour figures as approximate.

### Added by fact-checker

- Rulebook General Rule 1 explicitly states 'Onboard or offboard computation is permitted, but no human intervention is allowed during execution' — offboard COMPUTE (e.g., a laptop/base-station running the control/vision algorithm) is completely rules-legal; only offboard SENSING (Rule 15) is banned. This is more permissive than a literal reading of 'fully autonomous, onboard-sensing-only' suggests, and it changes the redesign scope for any lab prototype that currently uses a laptop in the loop: only the sensor data source needs to move onboard, not necessarily the whole compute stack.
- esp-drone's official README explicitly states 'Currently support ESP32、ESP32S2、ESP32S3, please using ESP-IDF release/v5.0' — confirming ESP32-S3 is an officially supported target, which supports the working assumption that the professor's ESP32-S3 PCBs are plausibly esp-drone-derived and that the cited esp-drone GitHub issues are directly relevant (not just analogous).
- In the MochiSwarm paper's architecture, the onboard companion vision module (NiclaVision) connects to the main MCU via UART specifically (not I2C) — 'We connect the NiclaVision to the main MCU using a custom PCB via UART protocol for bidirectional serial communication'; I2C on that platform is reserved for the barometer/IMU, not the camera link. Minor correction to the original research's 'via I2C/UART' phrasing for that specific link.
- The MochiSwarm paper's own reported total unit cost, '$132.74' (base $83.74 + $49 optional sensor suite = $132.74, consistent across both the paper and the Lehigh docs page), cross-validates cleanly — a good sign the BOM figures in both sources are internally consistent and trustworthy.
- IAR Rule 17 ('Safety — Structural Constraints') requires 'no sharp edges, pointed components, or rigid protrusions intended to puncture or damage objects' and that contact elements be designed to avoid puncturing the waypoint balloons — this is a concrete, checkable safety/design rule the original research did not mention, and it directly affects the design of whatever 'balloon-touch' contact mechanism (bumper/finger/ring) the team puts on the airship's nose.
- IAR Rule 23 confirms teams get pre-competition 'dedicated practice time' at the actual venue/arena with other teams' vehicles excluded from their end of the arena — useful for planning on-site trim/calibration time, and it somewhat answers (for practice, not necessarily the live run) part of the original research's 'need to re-trim between rooms' concern.
- The two air-density/physics derivations in the original research (battery-discharge mass loss ~1e-13 to 1e-14 kg via E=mc²; Bogotá 2600m air density ≈0.77x sea level via the ISA barometric formula) check out as standard, correctly-applied physics on inspection — no correction needed to the methodology, only to how the 120g baseline number was applied (see the balloon-size correction above).
